## PerMIT: Securing Persistent Memory for LLM Agents

via Machine-Checked Integrity Theorems

and Scalable Community Trust

Tanqiu Jiang
††thanks: Work was done during an internship at AWS AI Labs.
Affiliation: Stony Brook University

Min Bai

Affiliation: AWS AI Labs

Sadat Shahriar

Affiliation: AWS AI Labs

Wanyu Du

Affiliation: AWS AI Labs

Sandesh Swamy

Affiliation: AWS AI Labs

Abstract

Persistent memory is a largely undefended surface for injection and poisoning, and the same risk limits memory sharing among agents. We present PerMIT, a memory architecture with provable integrity guarantees under an explicit trust assumption: only user-typed inputs and values confirmed at a gate are trusted. PerMIT mediates the release and use of untrusted environment-derived values through a finite memory interface, while preserving agent-driven multi-turn planning and tool choice. Over this finite interface, we prove two integrity theorems that are also machine-checked in Lean 4, which together ensure that environment-derived memory reaches a sensitive action only if confirmed at the gate and influences the agent’s behavior only through what the gate displays. Every attack in our threat model thereby reduces to a single auditable decision, which a human, an LLM verifier, or both can make in practice. On Trojan Hippo, PerMIT records zero observed attack success with self-verification and lightweight verifiers. Because each confirmation is a logged verdict on exact content, endorsements accumulate across users, and sufficiently endorsed items become community-confirmed with no further verification required. Under explicit assumptions, we prove and corroborate in simulation a co-scaling property: as the community grows, the risks of promoting poison and of wrongly evicting benign knowledge fall, while per-user verification effort does not rise. On $\tau^{2}$ -bench, communities of up to 90 autonomous agents use PerMIT to build shared knowledge bases with no direct memory injection promoted, and the resulting knowledge significantly improves new agents on held-out tasks, suggesting a path toward agents that safely self-improve at test time.

## Contents

## Part I Main Paper

## 1 Introduction

By carrying preferences, decisions, and hard-won context across sessions, persistent memory transforms a stateless LLM into an agent that becomes more capable and personalized with continued use.
Although agents benefit from reading and writing persistent memory, this capability also creates an attack surface that the underlying models are poorly equipped to defend.
Poisoned information can readily enter persistent memory when an agent browses webpages or invokes tools containing planted injections (Das et al., 2026).
Such attacks can turn a one-shot prompt injection (Debenedetti et al., 2024) into a persistent compromise, and a single compromise could easily propagate to a broader community if that memory were ever shared.
Existing memory-sharing frameworks such as the Open Knowledge Format (McVeety & Hormati, 2026) (OKF) define formal protocols for writing and exchanging knowledge among agents but do not address memory attacks.
This difficult-to-control risk has limited the practical deployment of memory sharing between agents.

Existing defenses validate retrieved content, govern access, or enforce information-flow policies during agent execution.
A-MemGuard empirically detects context-dependent poisoning through consensus among memory-induced reasoning paths (Wei et al., 2025), whereas Collaborative Memory enforces dynamic read/write policies over provenance-tagged private and shared memory fragments (Rezazadeh et al., 2025).
However, neither approach provides formal security guarantees against malicious memories.
Beyond memory-specific defenses, CaMeL addresses prompt injection during agent execution through isolated planning with capability-based policies and user confirmation (Debenedetti et al., 2025); FIDES formalizes IFC guarantees and supports selective hiding and constrained inspection of tool results (Costa et al., 2025). PerMIT builds on this isolation-and-mediation lineage to secure the persistent-memory lifecycle.

PerMIT enforces its trust boundary at the memory interface. The harness captures environment-derived content, including tool outputs, as untrusted and mediates its release and use. The protected agent chooses its next operation and can re-plan on endorsed feedback, so its internal reasoning is not inspected or taint-tracked. The noninterference proofs quantify over arbitrary agent strategies using this finite interface (Goguen & Meseguer, 1982). However, preventing all influence from untrusted memory would also prevent legitimate tasks whose next steps depend on information recalled from that memory.
To mitigate the safety–utility tradeoff, PerMIT adopts endorsement (Myers et al., 2006), a formal relaxation that promotes an untrusted value to trusted status only after the user confirms the exact value displayed at the gate, so untrusted memory can be selectively endorsed and used rather than barred.
Using this finite mediation surface and endorsement mechanism, we prove Action Integrity as an exact authorization property and Reasoning Integrity as noninterference modulo the display transcript (Sabelfeld & Myers, 2004; Askarov & Sabelfeld, 2007), and machine-check their single-user core in Lean 411
 1

 The Lean 4 artifact is available upon request and will be released with the code when the paper is published. Its scope and correspondence are documented in Appendix E and Supplement S9..
These machine-checked guarantees hold over unbounded interface-conformant executions (§2.2), separating enforced mediation from the correctness of individual endorsement decisions.

Endorsement preserves benign utility by allowing external values to become trusted through an interface with a finite set of operations. The gate displays the exact value, original user instruction, and source, making the decision auditable. On Trojan Hippo, PerMIT combines live agent rollouts with targeted action-boundary probes; self-verification and lightweight verifiers record zero observed ASR while retaining useful memory capabilities (Table 1).

Although security concerns have limited the deployment of shared memory, Karpathy’s LLM-Wiki (Karpathy, 2026) illustrates its potential: thousands of users adopted his shared coding-agent setup to improve their agents’ performance.
LLM-Wiki’s success was an exception because the author is highly reputable, and the shared knowledge had accumulated thousands of public endorsements through GitHub stars.
However, scaling shared memory to diverse communities requires accepting contributions from ordinary users and agents, allowing adversaries to pose as legitimate contributors and spread harmful content. To solve this dilemma, PerMIT learns from the success of LLM-Wiki and allows each shared knowledge item to accumulate trust independently, paralleling security mechanisms in distributed systems that aggregate endorsements over shared objects (Walsh & Sirer, 2006).
Each logged confirmation is bound to the exact content, allowing verdicts from different users and agents to accumulate as item-level trust.
Each item requires confirmation until its accumulated trust reaches the “Community Confirmed” threshold.
As the population grows, confirmations and endorsements allow more items to reach this status and become directly available to new users without further verification, so safety and convenience improve together.
Under explicit assumptions, we prove that poison-promotion and wrongful-eviction risks decrease as the community grows, while the average per-user confirmation burden does not grow (Supplementary Theorem S6.13).
Our simulation spanning five billion promotion attempts also corroborates this co-scaling behavior.
PerMIT thus supplies the security assurance missing from the Open Knowledge Format (McVeety & Hormati, 2026) while remaining compatible with its knowledge-sharing protocol.

Finally, we close the loop by using PerMIT to construct community-confirmed shared knowledge bases on $\tau^{2}$ -bench (Barres et al., 2025) with up to 90 fully autonomous agents and no human involvement.
None of the tested direct injection payloads reaches the Community-Confirmed tier, and a zero-state agent (i.e. a fresh agent with no prior context or memory) connected to the resulting knowledge base significantly outperforms an agent without shared knowledge on held-out, experience-dependent telecom tasks (§5).

In summary, this paper makes three contributions:

1. 

Securing agent memory with machine-checked integrity.

PerMIT mediates a finite memory interface and binds each endorsement to an exact displayed value and its intended use. Under explicit trust assumptions, PerMIT provides two integrity guarantees, machine-checked in Lean 4 for the single-user core, and an auditable confirmation gate for human or automated verification.

2. 

Shared memory in which safety and convenience scale together rather than trade off.
Because each gate decision is a logged verdict on exact content, verdicts accumulate into community-confirmed knowledge, which is the layer that existing memory-sharing frameworks such as OKF lack. Under explicit assumptions, we
prove and corroborate in simulation that poison-promotion and wrongful-eviction risks fall as the community grows while per-user verification effort does not rise.

3. 

Safe, autonomous self-construction of shared knowledge.
Our experiments show that autonomous agent communities can safely construct useful knowledge bases with PerMIT. By enabling agents to generate, verify, and certify knowledge in complex environments, PerMIT provides a concrete path toward test-time learning and self-evolving agents.

## 2 Background and Related Work

## 2.1 Persistent Memory as an Attack Surface

Persistent-memory architectures manage context across sessions through tiered storage, consolidation, and revision (Packer et al., 2023; Chhikara et al., 2025; Xu et al., 2025). Because stored observations later return to the agent’s context, transient exposure can become durable influence. Attacks enter through direct poisoning of a memory or knowledge base (Chen et al., 2024), query-only interaction (Dong et al., 2025), or indirect ingestion of retrieved artifacts and webpages (Srivastava & He, 2025; Zou et al., 2026; Yang et al., 2026b); shared state can also contaminate users without an adversary (Yang et al., 2026a). The defining risk is temporal: Trojan Hippo plants a dormant payload through one untrusted tool call and activates it only in a later session (Das et al., 2026). This persistence makes turn-local prompt-injection defences insufficient and motivates guarantees over the memory lifecycle (Lin et al., 2026).

## 2.2 Defenses and Formal Coverage

Existing defenses draw their boundaries at different layers. Content-based methods detect anomalous memories or task drift (Wei et al., 2025; Abdelnabi et al., 2024). Isolation and privilege-control methods constrain how untrusted information can influence tool interactions (Wen et al., 2026; Shi et al., 2025). TMA-NM (Louck, 2026) binds memory authority to origin and studies consequential-action safety using TLA+ model checking and an inductive-invariant argument. Willison’s Dual LLM pattern introduced privileged planning, quarantined processing, and opaque references (Willison, 2023). CaMeL adds capability-based enforcement and user confirmation, while leaving formal verification as future work (Debenedetti et al., 2025, §§2,5,10). AgentDyn reports zero utility for its CaMeL configurations under automated dynamic-task evaluation (Li et al., 2026). FIDES provides a formal IFC model with integrity noninterference, selective variable hiding, and schema-constrained quarantined inspection (Costa et al., 2025). Its type-aware policies can permit bounded influence; PerMIT centers exact-value, tool-scoped endorsement and the complete display transcript. Whereas CaMeL and FIDES address prompt injection in agent execution more generally, our contribution centers the persistent-memory lifecycle, with Action Integrity and Reasoning Integrity guarantees for arbitrarily long interactions through the memory interface under explicit trust assumptions. We machine-check the integrity theorems in Lean 4 and aggregate item-level endorsements in the federated tier. Planning and tool choice remain agent-driven, including replanning on endorsed feedback.

## 2.3 Shared Memory and Evaluation

Shared-memory work has primarily addressed exchange, access, and portability. OKF standardizes knowledge exchange (McVeety & Hormati, 2026), Collaborative Memory enforces dynamic policies over private and shared fragments (Rezazadeh et al., 2025), and Portable Agent Memory proposes cryptographically verified transfer and injection-resistant rehydration (Ravindran, 2026). These mechanisms complement PerMIT but do not establish recipient-side action and reasoning integrity for adopted content.

General agent-security benchmarks emphasize prompt injection within a task episode (Debenedetti et al., 2024; Li et al., 2026; Weng et al., 2026); Trojan Hippo most directly matches our cross-session threat model (Das et al., 2026). We therefore use Trojan Hippo to evaluate persistent-memory security and $\tau^{2}$ -bench (Barres et al., 2025) to evaluate the end-to-end utility of autonomously constructed shared knowledge.

## 3 The PerMIT Framework

## 3.1 Threat Model

PerMIT assumes an adversary capable of controlling or manipulating content obtained from the external environment, including tool outputs, web content, external files and codebases. Poisoned payloads may enter persistent memory when the agent invokes tools, browses the web, or inspects external files or code. PerMIT establishes a provenance-based trust boundary: only values typed in directly by the user or exact values explicitly confirmed by the user through the confirmation interface are treated as trusted. All other environment-derived content remains untrusted and cannot reach the protected LLM unless it first passes through this confirmation gate and gets verified to be trusted.

## 3.2 The Single-User Tier: a Finite Memory Interface

The P-LLM remains an agent that chooses its next operation and revises its plan using endorsed feedback. Prompts describe the interface; the harness enforces its observation and flow rules without inspecting the agent’s internal reasoning. Gate decisions control which values are endorsed; the harness enforces how values cross the interface.
Figure 1 summarizes PerMIT’s architecture, trust boundary, and shared-memory trust-accumulation mechanism.
Architecturally, PerMIT places a
Protected LLM agent (P-LLM) on the protected side of the trust boundary and uses a Quarantined Reader (Q-LLM) to inspect and retrieve relevant content from untrusted memory. The Q-LLM can read the memory store but has no tool capabilities. In our implementation, it is realized as a recursive language model (RLM) (Zhang et al., 2025), enabling it to navigate stores larger than a context window without loading their contents into the P-LLM’s trusted context (implementation details in Section B.4). Untrusted content is exposed to the P-LLM only through opaque handles rather than as readable bytes, and the content can be released only after endorsement through the confirmation gate (Lemma D.1).
The P-LLM interacts with persistent memory through a fixed algebra of seven
operations: $\mathsf{write}$ , $\mathsf{recall}$ , $\mathsf{fill}$ ,
 $\mathsf{crystallize}$ , $\mathsf{display}$ , $\mathsf{confirm}$ , and
 $\mathsf{evict}$ . In particular, $\mathsf{recall}$ returns readable bytes only
for trusted content and otherwise returns an opaque handle; $\mathsf{fill}$ 
searches the untrusted region of memory and likewise returns handles; and
 $\mathsf{crystallize}$ produces a schema-bound summary that remains untrusted
when written back to memory. Appendix B explains all seven
operations from a systems perspective, with a worked poisoning trace. The full interface also mediates tool invocation, trusted retrieval, input channels, and batched iteration (Section D.1). Sensitive-tool literals must byte-match typed or tool-endorsed content to bypass the gate; handles always gate. Data tools may receive agent-composed bytes. Trust denotes provenance and authorization, not factual correctness.

Figure 1: PerMIT mediates observations and data flows at the persistent-memory interface while leaving planning and tool choice to the protected agent.

PerMIT’s finite memory interface yields two guarantees, stated compactly below. Both assume source-based labels, immutable stored blocks, and faithful displays; Appendices D.2.1 and D.2.2 summarize the arguments; Supplement S3 gives the full proofs.

Action Integrity (Theorem D.3, compact form).

Assume trusted endorsement: acceptance authorizes the exact displayed bytes for the named tool. For every execution from a well-formed initial state, under any attack, each argument reaching a sensitive tool is either an exact user-typed value or a value endorsed by that user at its exact bytes for that exact tool, at or before the call. Endorsement grants reusable authority for an exact value and tool. It does not bind argument position, task, purpose, time, or the other arguments of a call (Appendix D.2.1).

Reasoning Integrity modulo the display transcript (Theorem D.5, compact form).

Consider any two executions $\rho_{1},\rho_{2}$ from matched initial configurations (the same protected view, interaction history, and logs), with identical user-typed inputs, coupled randomness, the same schedule, and a common horizon. Their untrusted content may differ arbitrarily. Then

 $\mathsf{DT}(\rho_{1})=\mathsf{DT}(\rho_{2})\quad\Longrightarrow\quad\mathsf{Beh}_{P}(\rho_{1})=\mathsf{Beh}_{P}(\rho_{2}).$ 

Here $\mathsf{DT}$ is the complete display transcript, including previews, decision panes, and decisions; $\mathsf{Beh}_{P}$ is the sequence of P-LLM operations, tool invocations, and replies. Thus hidden untrusted content has no additional influence once the display transcript is fixed.

A never-endorsed, untrusted-derived value cannot be exfiltrated through a URL, search query, or file path without an accepting confirmation over that exact value at that exact tool in that call (Corollary D.4). This protection applies to information that has never been confirmed. Once a value has been confirmed for a particular tool, PerMIT allows the agent to recall it and reuse the exact value directly with that tool without repeating the confirmation. Likewise, an attacker cannot alter the P-LLM’s operations, tool invocations, or ungated sink flows without producing a corresponding difference in the complete user-facing display transcript (Corollary D.6). Within this threat model, every attempt to release untrusted content across the trust boundary is therefore reduced to a single auditable event: the confirmation decision.

As such, PerMIT isolates the risks posed by untrusted memory. Its safety guarantees now rest on
an explicit trust assumption: any value approved at the gate is treated as trusted. By mediating all
memory risk at this single decision point, the confirmation gate becomes a common verification interface
that can be operated by the human user, an LLM verifier, or both.

## 3.3 The Federated Tier

The federated tier extends the single-user confirmation mechanism to shared memory, allowing users to build on one another’s endorsements instead of independently reviewing every entry.
Because every confirmation is a signed and logged decision on exact content for an exact tool use, it serves two purposes: releasing the content for the confirming user and depositing an endorsement on the entry itself. For a shared entry, these endorsements accumulate across users, with each identity contributing at most one endorsement to a given retrieved memory entry. Once an entry collects at least $N$ fresh endorsements for a tool, it is promoted to the “community-confirmed" tier.
Subsequent users may then use the entry without repeating the confirmation process.

Because honest verifiers can make mistakes and adversaries may reject indiscriminately, treating every rejection as grounds for immediate deletion would create a denial-of-service attack surface against benign knowledge in the shared memory. PerMIT instead handles disputed entries through a demand-paced panel. A qualifying rejection does not immediately delete an entry. Instead, it freezes promotion, visibly flags the entry, and routes the dispute to an auditable panel process. The entry remains retrievable and may still be used after personal confirmation. The first $k$ eligible verdicts cast by fresh subsequent demanders form the panel. If rejecting verdicts reach the rejection threshold $m_{\mathrm{rej}}$ , the entry is evicted from the shared candidate tier. If accepting verdicts reach the acceptance threshold $m$ , the entry is vindicated. Together with rejection-rate limits and rejecter accountability, this mechanism tolerates isolated honest mistakes while containing adversaries that issue systematic false rejections.

This construction makes the safety–utility trade-off quantitatively explicit. PerMIT bounds poison-promotion and wrongful-eviction risks, and models confirmation labor, as functions of verifier accuracy, adversarial capacity, the endorsement quorum, and the panel configuration. A poisoned entry can reach the community-confirmed tier through only two routes: the ordinary endorsement path without a qualifying rejection, or a path that resumes after panel vindication. Let $f$ be the adversary’s lifetime identity budget, of which at most $f_{\mathrm{s}}$ identities ever become eligible for panel seats, and let $m$ be the acceptance threshold for a panel of $k$ jurors. The probability that a poisoned entry succeeds in any single promotion attempt is bounded by

 $\mathbb{P}[\text{promotion}]\;\leq\;B_{\mathrm{att}}\;:=\;(1-q_{\mathrm{pre}})^{N-f}\;+\;\sum_{h=m-f_{\mathrm{s}}}^{k-f_{\mathrm{s}}}\binom{k-f_{\mathrm{s}}}{h}\,(1-q_{\mathrm{pan}})^{h}\,q_{\mathrm{pan}}^{\,k-f_{\mathrm{s}}-h}.$ 

(1)

Here, $h$ denotes the number of accepting verdicts cast by honest panelists. Because the adversary can contribute at most $f_{\mathrm{s}}$ accepting verdicts, panel vindication requires at least $m-f_{\mathrm{s}}$ honest acceptances; the summation covers all such outcomes among the remaining $k-f_{\mathrm{s}}$ panel seats. The parameters $q_{\mathrm{pre}}$ and $q_{\mathrm{pan}}$ lower-bound the conditional probabilities that an honest reviewer detects poison at the ordinary gate and on the panel, respectively.
These bounds are conservatively evaluated for the most fallible honest cohort that the adversary can target. They hold uniformly over adaptive presentation, stopping, and re-entry strategies, regardless of how many users encounter the entry.
Under the illustrative simulation setting specified in Appendix A.1, the expression evaluates to $B_{\mathrm{att}}=8.96\times 10^{-3}$ , an analytical ceiling of $0.896\%$ per promotion attempt for the assumed reviewer rates.

The corresponding utility risk is also bounded. Let $p_{b}$ lower-bound the conditional probability that an honest panelist accepts a benign entry, and define the wrongful-eviction margin as $\delta_{b}:=p_{b}-m/(k-f_{\mathrm{s}})>0$ . A benign entry is then wrongfully evicted with probability at most
 $e^{-2\delta_{b}^{2}(k-f_{\mathrm{s}})}$ .

Under the specified demand, coverage, and threshold schedules, poison-promotion and wrongful-eviction risks decrease as the community grows, while the average per-user confirmation burden for covered benign shared demand does not grow. Required user approvals are capped at one per user, value, and tool when endorsed copies are retained. Personal demand and nonconforming shared demand retain their stated residual costs (simulations: Section 4.2). Formal statements and proofs are given in Supplementary Sections S5–S6.

Sharing can improve safety and convenience together because later users reuse earlier endorsements. Both simulations (Section 4.2) and live tests (Section 5) provide complementary empirical support: the former characterize the safety–effort trade-off, while the latter demonstrate growing reusable knowledge with roughly level per-agent review effort.

## 4 Results

We evaluate single-user security and utility on Trojan Hippo (§4.1) and test federated-tier certified ceilings against adaptive protocol adversaries (§4.2).

## 4.1 The Single-User Tier

Setup.

We evaluate four backbones across Trojan Hippo memory and defense configurations (Table 1; Das et al., 2026). PerMIT’s live containment harness places poison in untrusted persistent memory and combines a subsequent agent rollout with a targeted reader-to-tool probe. OpenGate accepts every confirmation request; OracleGate models a user who always makes the correct decision; LLMVerifier makes the decision with a language model. We instantiate LLMVerifier using self-verification and two lightweight Qwen models. Utility reports arithmetic and harmonic means (AM/HM) over seven capability axes, with HM primary. Appendix A.2 details the protocol and counting; Appendix A.5 reports benign FPR.

Existing defenses do not provide consistent protection. Every baseline has at least one model–backend configuration with nonzero ASR. Capability-restricting defenses reduce exposure by disabling functionality required by benign tasks, frequently collapsing HM, whereas prompt-level and retrieval-time defenses preserve more functionality but continue to admit attacks in other settings.

PerMIT structurally closes the ungated memory-injection path. Under the formal model, untrusted memory cannot reach the protected agent as readable content without endorsement (Lemma D.1), confining memory-mediated attacks to an auditable interface (Corollary D.6). Our live probes test this interface after poison is already stored. OpenGate records 0–10% ASR across the four backbones: low attack success persists even without a rejecting confirmation policy. Self-verification and both Qwen verifiers record zero observed ASR while preserving useful memory capabilities (Table 1).

Endorsement preserves useful capabilities with verifier-dependent cost. OracleGate models correct user decisions, providing an idealized reference for capability retained under PerMIT’s confirmation rules. With the RLM reader and interface held fixed, comparisons between OracleGate and the three LLM-verifier configurations show how replacing idealized user decisions with LLM judgments affects task performance. Appendix A.5 reports the FPR of different verifiers.

## 4.2 The Federated Tier

Figure 2: Reviewer quality, not quorum size, sets the federated safety floor (deployed
geometry of Section 3.3, $q_{\mathrm{pan}}=q_{\mathrm{pre}}=q$ ). (a) Certified ceiling
 $B_{\mathrm{att}}$ of Equation 1 over reviewer miss rate $1-q$ and quorum
 $N$ ; the contours are near-vertical because the panel term contains no $N$ .
(b) Correlated verdicts (pairwise $\rho$ ) raise both the promotion ceiling and
wrongful eviction. (c) The acceptance bar $m$ trades poisoned vindication
against wrongful eviction; $m{=}20$ keeps both below $10^{-2}$ .

We test the federated analysis with five billion simulated promotion attempts at the geometry of Section 3.3 and a 120-user community sharing 122 entries, including two poisoned entries. Simulations use controlled reviewer responses with poison catch rate $q$ and benign acceptance rate $p_{b}=0.9$ ; the correlation sweep varies verdict dependence. Appendix A.1 gives settings and additional figures.

The safety frontier in simulation.

Figure 2(a) shows how the certified ceiling $B_{\mathrm{att}}$ changes with the endorsement quorum $N$ and honest reviewer accuracy. Increasing either lowers the bound on poison-promotion risk, but the gain from a larger quorum saturates because the panel term of Equation 1 does not depend on $N$ . Figure 2(b) shows that correlated reviewer errors raise risk, which argues for drawing reviewers from diverse models and users. Figure 2(c) justifies the panel setting: for a 31-seat panel, an acceptance threshold of $m{=}20$ with the complementary rejection threshold $m_{\mathrm{rej}}{=}12$ keeps both poisoned vindication and wrongful eviction below the $10^{-2}$ design target. We then attacked the simulated protocol and measured how often a poisoned entry is actually promoted. For every tested number of honest users shown a poisoned entry, the observed promotion rate remains below the theoretical bound (Figure 3), and none of the adaptive attack strategies we simulated exceeds the bound (Figure 4).

Safety with less verification effort.

In a 120-user community (Figure 5), reusing community-confirmed entries saves each user $2.36$ required approvals on average ( $38\%$ ) compared with verifying alone, and a larger quorum provides substantial safety gains at a modest cost: raising $N$ from $3$ to $14$ reduces the certified risk bound by a factor of $58$ while adding only $0.64$ required approvals per user.

## 5 Self-Constructing the Knowledge Base

We test autonomous shared-knowledge construction on $\tau^{2}$ -bench telecom (Barres et al., 2025), with one construction run per model and community size $M\in\{12,24,60,90\}$ . A single model family explores tasks, verifies extracted knowledge, and reviews shared entries. Publication follows safety verification, sharability screening, and consent to the exact content.

The protocol uses a promotion quorum $N{=}10$ and fixed dispute thresholds $k{=}31$ , $m{=}20$ , and $m_{\mathrm{rej}}{=}12$ . To demonstrate the safety performance through PerMIT, we manually submitted direct memory-injection payloads in crafted entries through $\tau^{2}$ -bench’s normal submission path. None of the 12 direct memory-injection payloads reached the Community-Confirmed tier.

Utility uses 24 held-out tasks with two trials each and matched seeds (48 paired episodes per arm). Table 2 reports environment-completion scores (env_frac) and four-size pooled task-cluster bootstrap intervals, conditional on the observed bases; Appendix A.7 details the resampling.

Across $M{=}12,24,60,90$ , the retained knowledge bases contain 3–7, 7–12, 30–40, and 45–64 entries respectively, across the three backbones. Review effort per agent remains roughly level from 24 to 90 agents as the shared stock grows. This supports the practical co-scaling pattern of more reusable knowledge without proportional growth in individual review effort.

At $M{=}90$ , all means exceed the knowledge-free baseline; pooled gains across community sizes survive three-backbone Holm correction for Sonnet and Kimi, but not GLM-5. Kimi-built knowledge also improves a zero-state Claude-Haiku-4.5 agent by $+0.198$ $[+0.073,+0.333]$ in a separate 400-step, 48-episode evaluation (details in Appendix A.7). Results for the banking domain, and an analysis of when shared knowledge does and does not add utility, are in Appendix A.6.

## 6 Conclusion and Future Directions

PerMIT contributes a finite memory interface with machine-checked integrity guarantees, a federated protocol that turns exact-content endorsements into reusable trust, and an autonomous workflow for constructing useful shared knowledge. Under explicit assumptions, the protocol bounds poison promotion and wrongful eviction while reducing repeated verification. The agent communities promoted no direct injection payloads and improved held-out task performance through PerMIT.

These results motivate three future directions. First, provenance and endorsement do not ensure usefulness: shared memory may contain redundant, inaccurate, or hallucinated knowledge, motivating quality-aware gates and active memory management. Second, estimating rare promotion events required five billion simulated attempts and over 50 hours of computation; more efficient calibration with live verifiers and users would strengthen deployment evidence. Third, adaptive payload search against live verifiers remains an open empirical question. Our simulations test the protocol’s conditional safety boundaries through adaptive protocol attacks under controlled reviewer responses; they do not replace adaptive payload search against live verifiers. Human or agent memory managers could combine memories and review their joint meaning and intended use, then seek fresh user endorsements of the combined content before it becomes trusted. Testing this workflow against adaptive endorsement and delayed-combination attacks is a future direction. Matched component controls and measured human review effort would further clarify deployment trade-offs.

Figure 3: Promotion routes and the certified ceiling under the non-adaptive blast
schedule ( $q=0.6$ , $N=10$ , $f=2$ ). (a) Path decomposition on a linear reach
axis: the ordinary route follows the closed form $(1-q)^{R}$ ; the panel route opens at
 $R=m+1=21$ and follows $q(1-q)^{R-1}\sum_{J}S(J)$ (Proposition S8.11); open markers are
zero-event cells at their 95% Clopper–Pearson upper bounds. (b) Total
measured rate on a logarithmic reach axis against the certified ceiling
 $B_{\mathrm{att}}=8.96\times 10^{-3}$ and the panel-leak floor
 $p_{\mathrm{panel}}=8.3\times 10^{-3}$ . The measured total peaks at $R=k+1=32$ and then
declines below the simulation’s resolution, while the no-dispute baselines (charitable
and matched $W_{\mathrm{base}}$ ) approach certain promotion as reach grows.

Figure 4: Adaptive adversaries at the deployed geometry. (a) Measured
promotion rate as a fraction of the certified ceiling for nine strategies—blast,

no-sharing counterfactual on the same workloads and the analytic distinct-demand
ceiling $d_{\mathrm{tool}}$ ; confirmation effort saturates in $N$ , with a measured causal saving of
 $2.36$ per user ( $38\%$ ). (b) Safety–effort frontier at central overlap for
 $q_{\mathrm{pre}}\in\{0.2,0.4,0.6\}$ (decoupled $q_{\mathrm{pan}}=0.6$ ): raising $N$ 
from 3 to 14 costs $0.64$ required approvals per user and buys a $58\times$ lower
certified ceiling; no quorum reaches below the panel-leak floor
 $p_{\mathrm{panel}}=8.3\times 10^{-3}$ , and moving between $q$ -curves is not free.

## A.2 The Trojan Hippo Benchmark in Brief

Trojan Hippo (Das et al., 2026) evaluates persistent memory poisoning under an
indirect-injection threat model: the user is trusted, the attacker controls only
content the agent reads through its tools, and the attacker’s goal is to exfiltrate
sensitive user data to an attacker-controlled address. The attack has two stages. In
the injection stage, a crafted inbound email read through a mailbox tool carries a
payload that the memory backend silently stores; in the activation stage, after $N$ 
unrelated benign sessions, a user request on a sensitive topic triggers the stored
payload, which induces the agent to send the user’s message to the attacker through an
outbound tool. Table 3 summarizes the benchmark’s coverage and scale.

PerMIT live containment protocol.

PerMIT replaces the benchmark memory backend with its RLM reader and
confirmation interface. The four main-table backbones are served
through AWS Bedrock. Each case is placed in a fresh store as
untrusted email-tool output in an ingestion session and exercised
from a subsequent trigger session. The harness combines a live
protected-agent rollout with a targeted reader-to-tool probe:
the reader fills an address field, and the probe attempts to pass
the resulting handle to send_email, with an additional
literal-address variant. This explicitly attempts to exercise the
action boundary without relying solely on the planner to initiate
exfiltration. A case succeeds if either executed probe produces a
recorded sink invocation whose serialized arguments contain the
benchmark attacker address. The probes run sequentially in the
same per-case store. In LLMVerifier mode, the reader probe also
performs retrieval-stage crystallization and confirmation.
OpenGate accepts every confirmation; OracleGate supplies the
idealized correct-user decisions; LLMVerifier uses the designated
model. The comparisons are between complete configurations.

Audit baselines.

Our A-MemGuard and LLM-Auditor reimplementations share the benchmark
retrieval path and reuse the corresponding undefended cases.
A-MemGuard cross-checks per-record reasoning chains and maintains
a per-case lesson store; LLM-Auditor independently audits each
retrieved record. Retrieval depth is $K{=}8$ for RAG and $K{=}10$ 
for Mem0. Both discard flagged records; our auditor is a drop-only
variant, not the rewriting auditor described in the original work.

Table 3: Trojan Hippo at a glance (Das et al., 2026).

Aspect

Benchmark specification

Threat model

Indirect injection only: the attacker controls the From, Subject, and
Body of inbound email; cannot modify the agent’s code, system prompt, memory
implementation, or user queries; is white-box with respect to prompt, backend, and
tool interface but black-box with respect to model weights; and observes nothing but
a successful exfiltration.

Environment

A tool-calling email assistant. read_all_emails and
search_emails are the untrusted read tools; send_email,
reply_to_email, and forward_to_email are the outbound tools
through which exfiltration occurs.

Trigger topics

Finance, health, legal, tax, identity; user prompts are templated
with sampled sensitive entities (account balances, diagnoses and medications, case
details, W-2 figures, ID numbers).

Attack generation

OpenEvolve-style evolutionary search per (topic, backend,
defense): an LLM mutator proposes payload variants, an agent-in-the-loop scorer
checks exfiltration and an LLM critic scores 1–10, over a MAP-Elites diversity
grid. One training case and four held-out test cases per (topic, backend), drawn
from disjoint template and entity pools.

Benign histories

Multi-session, single-user conversation histories generated with a
modified LoCoMo pipeline; the payload is injected before session $N{=}0$ and the
trigger arrives after $N$ benign sessions. Undefended ASR does not decay up to
 $N{=}100$ .

Memory backends

Context (sliding window); RAG (512-character chunks, top-8);
Explicit (an update_memory list injected into the system prompt); Mem0
(LLM-extracted atomic facts, top-10); plus a no-memory control.

Defenses

User-prompt-only (index only user messages); No-untrusted-write (“No
untrusted tools” in our tables: suppress memory writes after a read tool);
Limit-memory-length (80-character entries); Provable policy (two-label IFC that
taints a session on any read tool or tainted retrieval and blocks outbound tools in
tainted sessions). Undefined pairs: User-prompt-only $\times$ Explicit and
Limit-memory-length $\times$ Context.

Utility

Seven benign capability suites—memory_only,
assistant_responses, untrusted_probe, disable_send,
untrusted_send, memory_tools, long_memory—aggregated
by arithmetic and harmonic mean; the harmonic mean collapses if any suite does.

Reported strength

Undefended ASR up to 85–100% on Gemini 3.1 Pro and GPT-5-mini;
the four defenses reduce ASR to 0–5% in most configurations, the provable policy
to 0% everywhere at a harmonic-mean utility near zero.

## A.3 Implementation Gap in the Provable-Policy Baseline

In the supplied Trojan Hippo implementation, GLM-5 records
10% ASR under the provable-policy baseline with both Explicit
memory and Mem0 (two successful attacks among twenty cases
per configuration). The successful cases are health/01 and
legal/03 for Explicit, and finance/01 and legal/03 for Mem0.

Our code audit identified inconsistent taint propagation in
search_emails: the tool marks the session untrusted
in SessionTrustManager but does not update the separate
ProvablePolicyManager state consulted by
send_email. An untrusted search can therefore leave
the outbound guard’s trusted state unchanged. All four
successful traces exhibit the corresponding inconsistency:
an untrusted-read notification is followed by an outbound
check with is_trusted=True and
will_block=False, and the benchmark’s outbox-based
attack checker reports success. These logs do not establish
that the successful sends were triggered by retrieval of
poisoned persistent-memory entries.

We retain the measured rates as results of the supplied
benchmark implementation. The observed failures illustrate
a practical enforcement gap: missing taint updates or
inconsistent security state can permit attacker-controlled
content to induce disclosure despite an intended
information-flow restriction. Formal guarantees depend on
faithful implementation of their assumptions across all
relevant execution paths; these results expose a loophole
in the evaluated enforcement rather than a counterexample
to the intended policy’s guarantee.

## A.4 Additional Backbones and Serving Configurations

Three backbones are reported here rather than in Table 1.
Qwen-3.6-27B and Gemma-4-31B-it were served locally under vLLM with a
common resource configuration (tensor parallelism over four GPUs,
32,768-token context, 0.92 GPU memory utilization, automatic tool
choice) and family-specific parsers. Qwen-3.6-27B:

vllm serve --tensor-parallel-size 4 --max-model-len 32768
--gpu-memory-utilization 0.92 --enable-auto-tool-choice
--tool-call-parser qwen3_coder --reasoning-parser qwen3

Gemma-4-31B-it additionally requires a model-specific chat template
and, on our stack, the native sampler:

VLLM_USE_FLASHINFER_SAMPLER=0 vllm serve
--tensor-parallel-size 4 --max-model-len 32768
--gpu-memory-utilization 0.92 --enable-auto-tool-choice
--tool-call-parser gemma4 --reasoning-parser gemma4
--chat-template tool_chat_template_gemma4.jinja

Tool-call parsing on a self-hosted server is part of the measurement
rather than a detail of it, because a defence that mediates tool calls
is sensitive to how those calls are extracted from the model’s output,
and the parser is necessarily family-specific. The two backbones
therefore share a serving stack but not a parser or chat template: they
are comparable to each other at the stack level, not to the
Bedrock-served set of the main table, and the per-family parser and
template choices are recorded rather than left implicit. The
lightweight verifiers (Qwen-3.5-2B and Qwen-3.5-4B) were served under
the same vLLM stack, one GPU each, with a 16,384-token context, 0.90
GPU memory utilization, the native sampler, and the family’s reasoning
parser; verifiers render verdicts rather than tool calls, so no
tool-call parser is involved. GPT-5.5 is served through the same
Bedrock APIs as GPT-5.4.

The pattern of Table 1 survives the change of serving stack.
Both self-hosted backbones are highly vulnerable undefended, peaking at
 $40$ per cent attack success on Qwen-3.6-27B and $75$ on Gemma-4-31B-it,
both on sliding-window context; both zero the harmonic mean under the two
capability-removing defences; and both reach $96.7$ AM under PerMIT with
the oracle at the gate, with $\mathrm{ASR}=0$ in every occupant cell.
The one instructive difference is on the small verifiers, which are
harder on these two backbones than on any Bedrock one. Whether the cause is the serving stack, the family-specific parsing and
chat template, or the backbone itself is not identified by these runs,
since the three are confounded by construction; the honest statement is
that a small external verifier needs validating against the backbone,
the parser, and the stack it will actually run behind.

Three readings the main table compresses are visible here. First, attack
success is zero in all twenty-eight cells, which is the only column with
no spread at all. Second, verifier size does not order the results: the
4B verifier has the higher harmonic mean on four of the seven backbones
and the lower one on three, its false-positive rate is lower on five, and
its two directions do not agree, since it wins by $25.0$ points of HM on
Qwen-3.6-27B and loses by $17.0$ on Kimi-K2.5. The two are therefore
better described as two draws from one quality tier than as a size
ladder, and a two-point size difference is not the axis to select on.
Third, the backbone matters more than the verifier. For a fixed verifier
the spread across backbones reaches $18.3$ points of AM and $45.1$ of HM
under the 2B model, while for a fixed backbone the spread across the
three model occupants reaches $20$ points of AM on Kimi-K2.5. A
deployment choosing a small verifier should therefore validate it against
its own backbone rather than against a published ranking.

Reading the security results.

ASR summarizes each complete configuration. Reader outputs may
already be clean or refuse an attack, so not every case presents
harmful bytes to the verifier. Appendix A.2
specifies the live probes and case-counting protocol.

Gate activity and utility.

Table 7 reports calls and rejections across six benign diagnostic suites with 20 checks each. These 120 checks have a different accounting unit from the seven capability axes summarized in Table 1. FPR is rejections divided by calls, and calls per check is calls divided by 120. These totals measure gate activity; they do not measure human review time or the fraction of tasks interrupted.

Verifier choice can substantially affect utility. Kimi with Qwen-3.5-4B has HM 50.3, compared with 89.7 under its oracle gate. For GPT-5.4, the authoritative main-table values are oracle AM/HM 89/86 and self-verification 83/79, a decrease of approximately 6 AM and 7 HM points at that precision. Larger verifiers do not consistently have lower FPR. Within PerMIT, these oracle-to-verifier comparisons retain the RLM reader and interface, characterizing the utility cost associated with automated gate decisions relative to correct endorsement. Comparisons against other memory backends instead evaluate complete systems; separating retrieval quality from the overhead of mediation would require matched reader controls. Human review time is a separate deployment measure.

## A.6 Domain Dependence of Self-Constructed Knowledge

We repeat the end-to-end autonomous construction experiment in the banking domain of $\tau^{2}$ -bench using the same three model families. Table 8 reports the resulting held-out utility. Unlike the telecom experiment in the main text, banking provides a golden-retrieval oracle rather than a hand-written policy manual; because these reference conditions are not directly comparable, the table reports the confirmatory contrast against the knowledge-free baseline only.

Table 8: Utility of the autonomously constructed knowledge base in the
banking domain. Contrasts against the no-knowledge baseline are paired
by task and seed; intervals are 95% confidence intervals.

Backbone
No knowledge
 $\bm{\Delta}$ vs. no knowledge [95% CI]

GLM-5
0.428
 $-0.000\;[-0.048,+0.048]$ 

Kimi-K2.5
0.467
 $+0.002\;[-0.027,+0.030]$ 

Claude-Sonnet-4.6
0.590
 $-0.015\;[-0.081,+0.045]$ 

The self-constructed knowledge base produces no detectable utility gain in banking: all confidence intervals include zero. This contrast with telecom is consistent with the information available in the two environments. Telecom tasks require causal, experience-derived knowledge about how the environment behaves—knowledge that is absent from the supplied documentation and can be acquired only through interaction. Banking tasks primarily require locating information in a well-indexed document corpus that the environment’s retrieval system already makes available on demand. Consequently, the telecom community can contribute knowledge that later agents would otherwise lack, whereas the banking community largely distils information that each agent can already retrieve independently. Together with GLM-5’s near-ceiling telecom result, these findings identify a practical boundary on the utility of shared memory: self-constructed knowledge is most valuable when it captures scarce, experience-derived information, and least valuable when the environment already supplies the required information or the underlying agent has little remaining performance headroom.

## A.7 Autonomous Telecom Construction: Methods and Additional Results

Construction and evaluation.

The construction ladder comprises one run for each of three model families (Claude-Sonnet-4.6, GLM-5, and Kimi-K2.5) and four community sizes $M\in\{12,24,60,90\}$ . Each run uses one model family for all construction roles. Agents crystallize session knowledge into an untrusted handle, submit its exact content to the safety gate for reasoning use, screen approved content for generality and privacy, and consent to publication. Other agents then review shared entries.

The standard utility evaluation uses 24 held-out tasks with two trials each (48 task/trial pairs per arm) and a 200-step limit. No Prior Knowledge (A0) provides no shared knowledge base, PerMIT (A2) supplies community-confirmed knowledge, and Expert Manual (A4) uses the benchmark’s alternative hand-written workflow policy. A4 is not the original policy with extra instructions appended, nor an oracle upper bound on task performance. A0 and A2 keep prompts identical outside the knowledge block. A2 includes all retained entries, with a coarse ordering that distinguishes uncontested from vindicated entries, without truncation. Generated experience primarily covers MMS troubleshooting; the reported Sonnet and Kimi gains are concentrated in that task family. This supports transfer of relevant experience, without establishing gains for every task family.

Pairing and uncertainty.

Seeds are matched by task and trial across arms. Table 2 separates the $M{=}90$ means from the analysis pooled across all four A2 community sizes against corresponding A0 episodes. Table 9 gives the complete size ladder and repeats the pooled analysis for reference. Pooling yields 192 paired differences but reuses the evaluation tasks and baseline; these are not 192 independent tasks. The analysis resamples whole tasks jointly across their trials and sizes: 95% confidence intervals use 10,000 cluster-bootstrap resamples, and the continuous-score test uses 20,000 task-cluster sign-flip permutations, both with analysis seed 20260811. Holm correction across the three same-backbone comparisons gives adjusted $p$ -values of $0.0066$ for Claude-Sonnet-4.6, $0.0325$ for Kimi-K2.5, and $0.9356$ for GLM-5. The bootstrap confidence intervals are pointwise, not multiplicity-adjusted. Using $m{=}5$ as a conservative multiplicity sensitivity analysis gives adjusted $p{=}0.0110$ for Sonnet and $p{=}0.0650$ for Kimi. These quantify evaluation uncertainty for the observed knowledge bases, not construction variability; pooled intervals must not be read as $M{=}90$ intervals. Step-limit terminations receive zero environment-completion score; infrastructure failures are excluded from the common paired set and counted separately.

Table 9: Same-backbone telecom utility across community sizes (upper panel), and the separate four-size pooled paired analysis (lower panel). Each size uses 48 task/trial pairs. Pooled 95% intervals use task-cluster bootstrap resampling and do not describe the $M{=}90$ result alone.

Fixed geometry and finite review opportunities.

The construction ladder keeps $N{=}10$ , $k{=}31$ , $m{=}20$ , and $m_{\mathrm{rej}}{=}12$ fixed. The panel can terminate when a decision threshold is reached; 31 votes are not required for every resolution. Nevertheless, $M{=}12$ cannot supply 20 fresh accepting panelists, nor the initiating rejection plus 12 further rejecting panelists required for eviction. Entries can be detected and frozen without reaching either terminal outcome. At $M{=}24$ , early threshold resolution can be feasible, subject to eligibility. Construction is sequential within one epoch: an entry published by agent $i$ has at most $M-i$ later reviewers. Raw promotion fractions therefore mix review quality with available demand. From $M{=}24$ to 90, reviews per agent change from 13.92 to 14.27 for Sonnet, 9.58 to 7.16 for GLM-5, and 12.25 to 10.70 for Kimi, while evaluated knowledge grows from 7 to 48, 10 to 45, and 12 to 64 entries, respectively. This is a finite-scale knowledge/effort trade-off, not a monotonic task-performance trend or an asymptotic theorem. At matched remaining-review horizon $H{=}30$ , benign yield from $M{=}60$ to 90 is 0.833 to 0.833, 0.824 to 1.000, and 0.826 to 0.976 for Sonnet, GLM-5, and Kimi, respectively; one run per setting limits inference about population effects.

Unfiltered shared-knowledge control.

The existing A1 arm supplies shared knowledge without the community-promotion filter. Table 10 compares A1 and A2 at the same $M{=}90$ construction budget, rather than comparing A1 to the four-size pooled result. A1 has higher mean utility on all three backbones. These are descriptive comparisons: the retained contents and prompt lengths differ, and a paired A1–A2 test is not reported here. This is a benign-utility comparison, not a measurement of relative attack resistance. It establishes that unfiltered shared experience can help; it does not isolate the promotion filter on identical candidate pools or substitute for a simpler voting control.

Table 10: Same-backbone utility at $M{=}90$ . Entries are counts of knowledge items supplied to each arm.

Cross-backbone transfer.

Claude-Haiku-4.5 uses knowledge built at $M{=}90$ by the indicated producer; its simulated user is GLM-5. The 200-step comparisons use 47 common task/trial pairs after one infrastructure failure in the wider Haiku comparison set. Table 11 reports that common set. The separate 400-step Kimi-to-Haiku evaluation uses 24 tasks with two trials each (48 pairs) and yields $\Delta{=}+0.1979$ $[+0.0729,+0.3333]$ . Its 24 task clusters comprise 11 MMS, four mobile-data, and nine service tasks. This result is reported separately because its episode set and step budget differ; its effect is not added to the 200-step baseline to reconstruct an absolute mean.

Scope of the safety probes.

The separate 12-payload direct memory-injection campaign reports no community promotion. This endpoint concerns access to the shared fast path; it does not measure personal acceptance, use before promotion, or every intermediate lifecycle transition.

## Appendix B The Memory Interface in Practice 

This appendix restates the memory interface of Section 3.2 from a
systems perspective: the seven operations and the fixed alphabet of replies
the P-LLM can receive (Section B.1), the label lifecycle
(Section B.2), a step-by-step trace of a memory-poisoning episode
(Section B.3), and
the implementation of the Quarantined Reader (Section B.4). It is
informative: no rule reads it and no proof cites it. The normative transition schema and Rules R1–R11 are in Supplement S2; Section D.1 summarizes their role in the guarantees.
Throughout, $P$ denotes the P-LLM and $Q$ the Q-LLM.

## B.1 Operations and the reply alphabet

Every reply the interpreter returns to $P$ is drawn from a fixed alphabet
(Rule R10),

 $r\;::=\;\mathsf{ack}\;\mid\;h_{j}\;\mid\;\mathsf{fail}\;\mid\;\mathsf{dec}(\mathsf{accept})\;\mid\;\mathsf{dec}(\mathsf{reject})\;\mid\;\mathsf{bytes}(b),\ b\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathbb{E}},$ 

so the only readable text $P$ ever receives is the verbatim content of a
trusted block. The isolation half of the single-user argument
(Lemma D.1) is the check, operation by operation, that
untrusted bytes never appear in the last category; Table 12 makes
that check readable. Two further properties keep the alphabet uninformative
about untrusted memory: handle identifiers are fixed by $P$ ’s own operation
sequence and never encode store contents (Rule R1), and an empty search, a
reader failure, and an unoccupied identifier all return the same
operation-determined handle (Rules R6, R7).

Table 12: The P-facing memory interface. Trusted labels: $\mathsf{T}$ (typed),
 $\mathsf{C}$ (confirmed), $\mathsf{CC}$ (community-confirmed), $\mathsf{PC}$ 
(P-composed); $\mathsf{U}$ is untrusted. Transition names and rule numbers refer to Supplement S2. Untrusted bytes reach $P$ only through
 $\mathsf{confirm}$ .

Operation

Purpose

 $P$ receives

Store effect

Semantics

The seven memory operations (emitted by $P$ )

 $\mathsf{write}(b)$ 

store a P-composed note

ack

new $\mathsf{PC}$ block: readable; empty tool scope, so never fast-paths a
sensitive tool

W-P; R1(g)

 $\mathsf{recall}(id)$ 

read a block

bytes if $id$ resolves to a trusted block; otherwise an opaque
handle, identical for untrusted, unknown, and absent ids

—

Recall-E/O; R6, R10(b)

 $\mathsf{fill}(q)$ 

 $Q$ searches untrusted memory

opaque handle; $\leq c_{\mathrm{cand}}$ candidates bound interpreter-side,
empty result indistinguishable

## B.2 Label lifecycle

Blocks are immutable once written (Assumptions A1–A2); trust changes only by
the creation of a fresh block. Figure 6 shows every permitted
transition. The only path from $\mathsf{U}$ to a trusted label is $\mathsf{display}$ 
followed by an accepting $\mathsf{confirm}$ , which copies the displayed bytes
into a new $\mathsf{C}$ block and leaves the untrusted original in place.

trusted ( $\mathbb{E}$ -level): readable by $P$ ; fast path per tool scope $\mathsf{U}$ $\mathsf{C}$ $\mathsf{CC}$ $\mathsf{T}$ $\mathsf{PC}$ pastetool output $\mathsf{crystallize}$ typed input $\mathsf{write}$ $\mathsf{display}$ + accepting $\mathsf{confirm}$ (fresh copy; original stays $\mathsf{U}$ )communitypromotion

Figure 6: Label lifecycle. No other transition exists: no operation relabels a
block in place, and $\mathsf{CC}$ status lives in the shared tier rather than
in any local store.

lists everything $P$ ever receives.

Three facts about the trace carry the design. First, the payload never enters
 $P$ ’s context: steps 2–5 deliver only acknowledgements and handles, so $P$ ’s
plan in session 2 is what the user typed, not what the attacker wrote
(Reasoning Integrity, Section 3.2). Second, the payload cannot
reach a tool without being displayed verbatim at step 6 and accepted for that
exact tool (Action Integrity, Section 3.2). Third, everything
therefore rests on the decision at step 6—the single auditable event whose
occupants (oracle, self-verification, external LLM verifiers)
Table 1 evaluates.

## B.4 The Recursive Language Model as a Quarantined Reader

Our implementation uses an RLM (Zhang et al., 2025) because it supplies search, structured extraction, and crystallization within the quarantined reader ( $\mathsf{fill}$ and $\mathsf{crystallize}$ in Table 12). Conventional retrieval would need adaptation to provide the same interface while preserving the separation between untrusted content and the protected agent.

Why an RLM.

The reader must search an untrusted store that is
unbounded (convention (C7)) and routinely exceeds any context window, yet its
bytes must never be loaded into a trusted context. An RLM fits both
constraints by construction: the long input is not fed to the model but placed
as a variable in a persistent REPL environment, and the model writes code to
inspect, decompose, and recursively query sub-models over programmatic slices
of it (Zhang et al., 2025). The store is thus navigated by code,
symbolically, at any size.

How retrieval works.

The interpreter first restricts the reader’s
view to $\mathsf{U}$ -labelled blocks—endorsed content and interpreter state are
structurally invisible—and loads the visible blocks (identifier, content,
tags) as a list variable inside the REPL. The root model receives only the
retrieval instruction and constant-size metadata; it then iterates, printing
slices of the store, delegating oversized blocks to sub-model calls, and
accumulating its answer in a result variable, under a fixed iteration cap. The
instruction is fidelity-only: read the actual blocks, copy any
extracted value verbatim, cite the source block identifier, and report absence
as absence—the reader carries no safety obligation, because every safety
property is enforced downstream by the interpreter (Lemma D.1,
Rules R6, R7). Finally, the interpreter validates the
reply deterministically: the value must satisfy its declared schema and every
citation must name a block genuinely present in the view; any failure, reader
error, or uncited summary collapses to the null-handle outcome the semantics
already define (Rule R6). An accepted value reaches $P$ only as an
opaque handle, resolved at a confirmation gate; an accepted summary is written
back at level $\mathsf{U}$ (Fill, Cryst, Figure 6).
Algorithm 1 summarizes the procedure.

Algorithm 1 RLM-based retrieval for one reader operation
( $\mathsf{fill}$ or $\mathsf{crystallize}$ )

 1:

 query $q$ , schema $s$ , memory store $\mathcal{M}$ ; iteration cap $T$ 

 2:

 3:

 4:

 $\mathcal{E}\leftarrow\textsc{InitREPL}(\texttt{context}\leftarrow[(\mathrm{id}(b),\mathrm{bytes}(b)):b\in V])$

with constant-size metadata of context

 6:

 for $t=1,\dots,T$ do

 7:

    $c\leftarrow Q(\text{history})$ ;  $(\mathcal{E},\mathrm{out})\leftarrow\textsc{REPL}(\mathcal{E},c)$ 
 $\triangleright$ $Q$ reads slices; sub-calls for oversized blocks

   if $\mathcal{E}[\texttt{answer}]$ is set then

     $(v,C)\leftarrow\textsc{Parse}(\mathcal{E}[\texttt{answer}])$ ,
disambiguated against the true identifier set of $V$

 $C\subseteq\{\mathrm{id}(b):b\in V\}$ and

Utility of the complete pipeline.

In every PerMIT cell of
Tables 1 and 4, the RLM reader is the
retrieval path for stored untrusted content. With the oracle gate,
PerMIT has higher AM than undefended RAG on four of seven backbones,
trails by at most 1.5 AM points on Gemma-4-31B-it and GPT-5.5,
and by about ten AM points on GPT-5.4.
These cross-backend comparisons assess the complete mediated pipeline,
not a gate-induced improvement in retrieval quality. The oracle measures
retained capability under correct endorsement, while comparisons among
PerMIT’s gate configurations keep the reader and interface fixed and
characterize decision-dependent utility costs. Isolating mediation
overhead from reader quality would require a matched no-gate reader
control; measuring human confirmation effort requires a separate study.

## Appendix C Concurrent Work: Origin-Bound Memory Authority

TMA-NM (Louck, 2026) studies origin-bound authorization of
consequential actions. PerMIT combines observation mediation at a finite
memory interface, exact-value and tool-scoped endorsement, a
transcript-conditioned integrity theorem, and federated trust
accumulation. The comparison below distinguishes the policies, proved
properties, and verification scopes. Each guarantee depends on the
assumptions and enforcement rules of its own model.

Independent development.

PerMIT was developed independently of, and concurrently with, TMA-NM.
We became aware of the earlier public preprint while preparing this
manuscript and include this comparison to clarify the relationship
between the two approaches.

endorsed content may influence responses
