## C.1 The observation boundary

TMA-NM permits untrusted memory to inform responses while constraining
consequential actions. PerMIT additionally mediates what the protected
agent can observe. Under Lemma D.1, untrusted sources
are represented by opaque handles until endorsement releases their
contents. This prevents readable delivery of unendorsed memory through
the modeled interface; it does not prevent all possible influence from
malicious content after endorsement.

Theorem D.5 states the precise comparison. For matched
initial configurations, identical user-typed inputs, coupled randomness,
the same schedule, and a common horizon, equal complete display
transcripts imply equal observable protected-agent behavior. An
attacker can nevertheless change a displayed preview, influence an
endorsement decision, or supply harmful content that a verifier accepts.
Such changes may alter the transcript and subsequent behavior. The
theorem establishes no additional influence beyond the transcript under
its matching conditions; it does not establish factual correctness,
unbiased recommendations, or the soundness of an automated verifier
(Appendix D.2.2).

Isolation likewise blocks in-context reconstruction from fragments
while they remain unavailable to the protected agent as unendorsed
content. Once fragments have been endorsed and released, the agent can
combine them. Sensitive calls remain subject to exact-value and
tool-scope checks, but separate endorsements do not establish the safety
of a combined transaction or its suitability in a changed context
(Appendix D.2.1).

## C.2 Relational versus reachability properties

The single-execution invariants reported for TMA-NM do not establish
PerMIT’s two-execution, transcript-conditioned property. This is a
difference in the statements proved, not an expressiveness limitation
of model checking. Relational safety properties can be reduced to
safety properties over self-composed or product systems representing
multiple executions (Shemer et al., 2019). Establishing an
analogous result would require an appropriate relational model and a
separate proof; enlarging an existing single-execution model alone
does not supply that proof.

PerMIT’s Lean development establishes its integrity properties for
arbitrarily long executions of the mechanized single-user core, under
the modeled interface and assumptions. Appendix E
distinguishes this checked core from the paper-only extensions and
federated analysis. A proof about either formal model does not by itself
establish that a deployed harness implements that model faithfully.

## C.3 The federated tier

PerMIT also studies shared memory contributed and reviewed by a
population of attested identities. Its analysis addresses promotion,
disputes, wrongful eviction, and confirmation cost under explicit
identity budgets, reviewer conditions, and protocol schedules
(§3.3).

These guarantees are conditional and probabilistic. Attestation and
first-decision accounting constrain how much weight adversarial
identities can supply, but honest reviewers can still accept poison.
Equation 1 bounds that residual promotion risk.
Under the demand, coverage, and threshold schedules of Supplementary
Theorem S6.13, the risk bounds decrease with community
size while the per-user confirmation burden for covered benign shared
demand does not increase. This is not an unconditional guarantee of
zero poison promotion or decreasing review effort for every workload.

## C.4 Deployment without institutional corroborators

PerMIT’s single-user tier operates with the user as endorser and does
not require a federation. Once an exact value has been approved for a
named tool, retaining the endorsed copy and its in-scope authorization
permits reuse without another mandatory approval for that same pair.
This does not bound the number of rejected review requests, and deleting
the local endorsed copy can remove the reuse path. The guarantee also
does not establish how much useful content a human or automated verifier
will approve.

Reuse is an explicit authorization-policy choice. PerMIT’s endorsement
does not bind argument position, task, purpose, time, or the other
arguments of a call. Community disputes suspend community reuse while
preserving prior personal confirmations. Thus the confirmation-cost
claim should be read together with the retained-copy assumption and
the contextual-authorization limits in Appendix D.2.1.

## C.5 Laundering channels

PerMIT addresses laundering through the modeled interface rules.
Reader-generated summaries remain untrusted when written by
 $\mathsf{crystallize}$ , so summarization alone grants no authority.
All tool outputs are captured as untrusted, preventing a tool’s name or
status from automatically making its output trusted. These rules do not
make subsequently endorsed content harmless or protect against a
compromised enforcement harness.

Repeated content does not itself constitute independent endorsement.
Distinct attested identities and first-decision accounting restrict
manufactured voting weight. Where the promotion quorum exceeds the
certified adversarial identity budget, adversarial endorsements alone
cannot satisfy it; erroneous honest endorsements can still promote
poison, with risk bounded under the assumptions of
Equation 1.

The capability_ifc baseline evaluated by
Louck (2026) treats retrieved memory as trusted. Its
reported failures concern that evaluated configuration and do not
establish that every deployment of CaMeL, FIDES, or capability-based
information-flow control fails under the same attacks.

## Appendix D Formal Guarantees and Proof Sketches

This appendix summarizes the memory-interface model and single-user integrity guarantees cited in the main text. Part III (Supplement S) gives the complete semantics and integrity proofs (S2–S3), federated analysis and confirmation-cost results with their assumptions (S4–S6), security discussion (S7), probability tools and assumption audit (S8), and detailed Lean correspondence (S9).

## D.1 Interface and trust assumptions

The Protected LLM $P$ is an arbitrary agent strategy over its observations, interaction history, and random coins. It chooses operations and can re-plan on endorsed feedback. The harness mediates the memory interface and tool boundary; it does not inspect or taint-track the agent’s internal reasoning. The Quarantined Reader $Q$ may behave adversarially and holds no tool capability. Appendix B.1 gives the operation-level view; Supplement S2 gives the normative semantics.

Stored values and authority.

Each immutable block has bytes, a source-based label, provenance, and a tool scope. The labels are $\mathsf{T}$ (user-typed), $\mathsf{U}$ (untrusted), $\mathsf{C}$ (personally confirmed), $\mathsf{PC}$ (agent-composed), and, in the federated extension, $\mathsf{CC}$ (community-confirmed). The readable store $\mathcal{M}_{\mathbb{E}}$ consists of non- $\mathsf{U}$ blocks. Readability alone does not authorize a sensitive call: a literal must byte-match a $\mathsf{T}$ block or a confirmed block scoped to that tool. A $\mathsf{PC}$ block has no independent endorsement authority. A confirmation for the reasoning target makes its bytes readable but grants no sensitive-tool scope.

The trusted command channel creates $\mathsf{T}$ blocks. Pasted content and all tool outputs, including errors and status text, enter $\mathsf{U}$ . Reader-generated summaries also enter $\mathsf{U}$ . The agent receives an opaque handle, not those bytes. Accepting confirmation creates a fresh $\mathsf{C}$ copy; it never relabels the untrusted source in place. Endorsed and untrusted identifiers use separate allocation streams, so an attacker cannot influence a later endorsed identifier by adding untrusted blocks.

Mediation rules.

The harness enforces the following properties (complete rules: Supplement S2.3).

• 

Every handle-expressed tool argument gates, even if its hidden bytes equal an already trusted value. The gate resolves the handle, displays the exact bytes and target, and binds an acceptance to those same bytes. Rejection or failed resolution prevents the flow.

• 

Sensitive-tool literals use only the typed or exact-tool-confirmed fast paths. The baseline permits agent-composed literals at data tools. Consequently, endorsement for one purpose does not imply a confidentiality guarantee for all later data-tool uses; Option O2 adds exact-tool checks there.

• 

Reader failure, empty retrieval, and unoccupied identifiers have the same opaque reply. Handle structure follows operations rather than hidden content, size, or success. The reply alphabet contains only fixed acknowledgements, handles, fixed failures, decisions, and verbatim readable-store bytes. Trusted retrieval sees $\mathcal{M}_{\mathbb{E}}$ alone.

• 

All attacker-influenceable user-facing content is recorded in the display transcript. Authenticated facts and untrusted source claims occupy separate zones. Selection previews carry no authority; authorization uses the resolved value in the decision pane.

• 

A batch displays every value that would flow, records its pages and decisions, and executes only after all required approvals. Accepted values remain endorsed even if a later review aborts the invocation. Full batch semantics and the corresponding determinacy argument are in Supplement S2–S3.

Assumptions and boundaries.

Both integrity results assume source-based labels, immutable stored blocks, and faithful displays (A1–A3 in Supplement S2.4), together with the enforced interface above. Action Integrity additionally assumes sound atomic endorsement: acceptance means that the user intends the exact displayed value at the named tool, separately for each reviewed value, including accepted positions of a transaction that later aborts (A4). An LLM verifier can violate this assumption; the theorem does not establish its accuracy. Reasoning Integrity instead assumes a common exogenous typed-input stream and matched scheduling and coins (A6 and conventions C1, C9); it does not require endorsements to be semantically correct. Timing, termination, and compromised harness components are outside these guarantees.

Lemma D.1 (Protected-agent isolation).

In every interface-conformant execution, each bytes-carrying reply delivered to $P$ is the verbatim content of a readable-store block at delivery. Untrusted sources reach $P$ only through opaque handles until endorsement. This is a statement about the source of a delivered value, not disjointness of byte strings across stores.

Proof sketch.

Inspect the reply constructors. Tool capture, untrusted recall, reader search, and crystallization return handles or fixed tokens. Only trusted recall and retrieval return bytes, and both read $\mathcal{M}_{\mathbb{E}}$ . Input ingestion and each operation preserve this property, so induction extends it to arbitrary run length. Full proof: Supplement, Lemma S3.12.
∎

## D.2 Integrity Proofs

D.2.1 Action Integrity

Definition D.2 (Personal Action Integrity).

A sensitive sink event at tool $t$ is personally authorized when each argument value $v$ byte-matches user-typed content, or this user has accepted the exact bytes $v$ for $t$ , either at that call or in a retained in-scope confirmation. For a batch, acceptance covers each displayed element separately. Action Integrity requires this property for every sensitive sink event of every execution.

Theorem D.3 (Action Integrity).

Under the interface and A1–A4 above, every execution starting from a well-formed configuration has personal Action Integrity, for arbitrary agent strategies, attacks, and reader behavior. The empty configuration is well formed; a seeded configuration must carry a valid endorsement record. The invariant requires immutable source-correct blocks, valid endorsement ancestry and history, interface-conformant state, and authorization of the accumulated sink log (complete definition: Supplement, Definition S3.3).

Proof sketch.

The empty state satisfies the invariant. For each transition, new untrusted blocks confer no authority; agent writes confer readability but no new authorization; and an accepted confirmation creates a fresh copy justified by the exact display and sound endorsement. A sensitive call therefore has only three admissible cases: a typed literal, an in-scope confirmed literal, or fresh accepting confirmation. Every handle takes the third case, and a batch applies it to every flowed element. Failed reviews prevent the call, while already accepted values remain individually authorized. These cases preserve the invariant and establish sink safety by induction. The single-user interpreter has no $\mathsf{CC}$ creation path. Full statement and proof: Supplement, Theorem S3.21.
∎

Corollary D.4 (No silent resolution).

Under the same conditions, never-endorsed untrusted bytes cross a tool boundary, including a data-tool boundary, only through acceptance of those exact bytes for that tool in that call. After endorsement at any target, their readable copy may be used as a literal at baseline data tools. Sensitive-tool scope checks and the rule that handles always gate still apply.

Proof sketch.

Isolation leaves never-endorsed bytes available only through handles, and every handle resolution gates. Endorsement subsequently creates a readable copy, enabling the stated literal path. This distinguishes exact-byte authorization from control influence carried by a logged decision. Full proof and Option O2’s closure of the later data-tool path: Supplement, Corollary S3.23.
∎

Reuse and contextual authorization.

The policy authorizes exact values for tools, not complete transactions. Separately approved values may be combined without joint review, and a changed task context may make their reuse inappropriate. Shared disputes suspend community reuse but preserve prior personal confirmations. Candidate expiry retires dormant or inactive shared entries (Supplement, Rule R14); it is not a universal authorization lifetime. Local scope leases are a future extension outside the proved model (Option O3). Context-bound or transaction-level approval would require extended semantics and revised confirmation-cost accounting.

Future direction: combined-memory review.

Human or agent memory managers could assemble related memories with their provenance and intended use, then review their joint meaning. The combined item remains untrusted until confirmation; its components’ approvals do not automatically endorse it. If subsequently published, it must gather fresh endorsements of its exact content under the community-promotion rules. Revisions require their own review. This proposed workflow builds on crystallization and endorsement but has not been evaluated and does not authorize every later transaction using the combined memory. Future experiments should compare it with per-item review under matched attack-search and review budgets, measuring harmful endorsement, delayed-combination success, benign utility, and review burden.

D.2.2 Reasoning Integrity modulo the display transcript

The protected view is $\mathsf{obs}_{P}(c)=(\mathcal{M}_{\mathbb{E}},H,x,h)$ : the readable store, observable handle structure, typed input, and decision history. Matched configurations $c_{1}\approx c_{2}$ agree on this view, the agent’s interaction history and consumed coins, endorsed allocation state, and initial ghost display and sink logs. Their untrusted stores and private handle resolutions may differ arbitrarily. A common endorsed seed with empty histories is a canonical matched start.

The complete display transcript $\mathsf{DT}(\rho)$ records selection surfaces and previews, exact decision-pane values and provenance zones, targets, selections, accepts and rejects, and batch pages and aggregated decisions, including reviews in aborted transactions. Its event kinds are interpreter-assigned. The observable behavior $\mathcal{B}(\rho)$ comprises protected-agent operations, tool invocations, the reply stream, final protected view, and the ungated sink projection. The latter omits arguments flowed by fresh confirmations, which are already recorded in the transcript.

Theorem D.5 (Reasoning Integrity modulo the display transcript).

Under A1–A3 and the input and scheduling conditions above, fix matched starts, any two attacks, and a coupled coin sequence. For runs $\rho_{1},\rho_{2}$ of a common horizon,

 $\mathsf{DT}(\rho_{1})=\mathsf{DT}(\rho_{2})\quad\Longrightarrow\quad\mathcal{B}(\rho_{1})=\mathcal{B}(\rho_{2}).$ 

Paste presence and content, reader outputs, and tool outputs need not agree across the attacks. The statement is trace-wise for every coupled coin sequence. In plain terms, the two runs may differ in whether pasted input arrives, its contents, and reader and tool outputs. The guarantee holds for every fixed sequence of random choices used identically in both runs.

Proof sketch.

Maintain matched protected views, histories, coin positions, and transcript prefixes. The strategy then chooses the same next operation. A display-free step returns only a constant token, an operation-determined handle, or matched readable bytes, preserving the invariant. At a display step, the append-only transcripts and equality of the complete transcripts make the newly appended segments prefix-comparable. The interface’s display-segment determinacy lemma forces those segments to be equal, including page structure, decisions, and released bytes. The two steps therefore create the same endorsed copies and replies. Induction gives the claimed equality; sink annotations follow the same mediation cases as Action Integrity. Full proof, including the segment lemma and batch cases: Supplement, Lemma S3.20 and Theorem S3.25.
∎

Corollary D.6 (Attack-surface confinement).

Under the same comparison conditions, a difference in protected-agent operations, tool invocations, or ungated sink events entails a difference in the complete display transcript.

Proof sketch.

Take the contrapositive of Theorem D.5.
∎

This is a conditional noninterference guarantee. An attacker can affect a preview or an endorsement decision, and thereby the transcript and later behavior. The theorem excludes additional influence that bypasses that recorded release surface; it does not establish reasoning correctness or make displayed attacks harmless.

## Appendix E Scope of the Lean 4 Verification

The Lean artifact is available upon request and will be released with the code when the paper is published. The artifact contains the executable single-user interpreter, proofs, build assertions, dependency manifest, and axiom report. It pins Lean 4 and Mathlib to v4.31.0 (Mathlib revision fabf563a); lake exe cache get && lake build reproduces the check. Supplement S9 gives the complete correspondence table, ten modeling deltas, and the specification corrections discovered during mechanization.

What is checked.

action_integrity and action_integrity_from establish the single-user action guarantee from the empty endorsed store and from any state satisfying the mechanized invariant. reasoning_integrity and reasoning_integrity_from prove the transcript-conditioned comparison from empty or shared endorsed seeds, for arbitrary agent strategies and independently chosen attacks. Their conclusions include equal sink logs, operations, replies, and final protected views. Paste presence and content can differ. Supporting results check reply isolation, mandatory handle gating, and attack-surface confinement. A standalone batch layer checks displayed-element flow, rejection behavior, and transcript determinacy; executable assertions cover the baseline counterexample and eight scenarios.

What remains a paper proof.

The run-level integration of batched iteration, durable endorsements from accepted positions of an aborted invocation, arbitrary mid-run matched starting states, and the reduction from reactive actors to event-indexed oracles are proved in the supplement, outside the checked interpreter. Options O1–O2 are checked at the gate level only; their wider integration, Option O3, the federated protocol, probability bounds, and confirmation-budget results are not mechanized. The checked interpreter returns one selected element at a direct collection-handle call and records additional transcript-determined selection information. These representation differences and their correspondence arguments are explicit in S9.

Trusted base and artifact.

The artifact’s clean-build axiom report lists only propext and Quot.sound for the top-level theorems, with no sorryAx; the executable assertions additionally trust Lean’s evaluator. Kernel checking certifies the mechanized statements, not the correspondence between that model and a deployed harness or the reliability of a verifier. The artifact includes the full source, CORRESPONDENCE.md, pinned build instructions, the build log, and source checksums. Thus the concise statements here remain auditable against both the complete paper proofs and the precise mechanized scope.

statements remain in the shared source files; the Lean artifact is available
upon request.

## Detailed contents of the formal development

## Appendix S1 Structure of the Proof, Notation, and Conventions

## S1.1 Organisation and register conventions

Sections S1–S8 contain the formal
development for PerMIT, with a machine-checked single-user core
(§S9) and register-separated normative, proof,
and rationale layers; the empirical appendices are indexed in the guide
in Appendix A of the main paper.
The material is organised into three strictly separated registers.

Normative register (Appendices S2, S4).

The memory-interface semantics, the interpreter and protocol rules
(R1–R14), and the assumptions (A1–A9; the budget-model assumptions
A10–A13 are stated with the model they govern). Every rule clause states what
the harness does, tersely; no rule contains its own justification. A rule
clause states normative behaviour tersely, relying only on the
normative definitions and conventions it cites; rationale and
empirical interpretation are never needed to implement it.

Proof register (Appendices S3, S5,
S6).

Definitions, lemmas, and theorems. Proof-register text
cites the normative register and the named invariants of
§S1.4; it never cites the non-normative register.
The single-user results of Appendix S3 are additionally
machine-checked in Lean 4 over the map-free operation algebra, with the
batch-confirmation surface of Rule R9 mechanized as a
standalone layer; the store-level integration of $\mathsf{map}$ into the
run-level interpreter and Option O3 remain outside the mechanized
surface, under the disclosed deltas recorded in §S9.

Non-normative register (Appendix S7 and commentary
blocks).

Design rationale (Notes N1–N18), the small-type commentary
blocks defined below, and a security and robustness analysis
(Analyses S1–S18): an indexed catalogue of
attack vectors and failure modes, each with its disposition
by formal fact, and its status: disposed (under stated
assumptions; a formal fact disposes
of it) or residual (a genuine limitation, disclosed); and
either entering only the budget constants or not used in
any proof. Four rationale remarks whose content adjoins specific results
are set as numbered Remarks adjacent to the results they annotate,
Remarks S4.7,
S5.19, S5.5, and S5.26. Nothing normative
lives in this register; no proof depends on it.

Commentary blocks. Throughout the normative and proof
registers, short passages set in small type under the heading
Commentary (non-normative) carry the rationale, epistemic
status, and validation pointers for the material they adjoin, so that a
rule or assumption and its justification can be read together without
the justification entering the register. Commentary blocks belong to
the non-normative register in function: no rule reads them, no proof cites
them, and a reader who skips every commentary block loses no
hypothesis and no step of any proof.

Statement discipline. A theorem, lemma, proposition,
corollary, definition, or invariant environment contains
hypotheses, the conclusion, and bare pointers (“established by”,
“see Remark”); interpretation, comparison, special cases, and design
justification are set in a numbered remark adjacent to the result they
concern.

Cross-referencing convention. The formal development
may point
into the non-normative register only by a parenthetical key, e.g. “(rationale:
N2; analysis: S11)”; the non-normative register may cite the
formal development freely. Consequently the formal development is self-contained: a reader who
skips Appendix S7 entirely loses no hypothesis and no
step of any proof. A reader with a specific concern should consult
the attack and limitation index (Table S3), which
maps concerns to analysis entries and to the formal facts that govern
them.

Map of the formal development
(Sections S1–S8).

Dependency structure.

facts are visible directly from the figure and are relied on throughout: (i) the
single-user theorems and the pathwise cap
(Thm. S6.2(i)) depend only on the trust axioms A1–A6 and
the harness rules, never on any federated or budget modelling

Corollary S6.3; (ii) no

edge); (iii) the behavioural parameters $q,p_{b},\rho_{\mathrm{seat}}$ and the demand
assumptions A10–A13 enter only the probabilistic-refinement and budget
layers, each of which degrades to the layer below it when its
hypotheses fail (§S7.5).

## S1.2 Notation

The principal symbols of these appendices are collected here; locally
scoped symbols are defined at first use. Unless otherwise marked,
symbols denote fixed deployment configuration belonging to the
trusted computing base (TCB): the fixed
components (interpreter, channels, rendering and logging paths, and
configuration) that the guarantees assume correct and that no model
output can alter (inventory: §S2.5 single-user,
§S4.3 federated). Marks:
(emp) empirical/behavioural parameter, (sch) population schedule,
(rv) random variable.

(Appendix S2)

state, Reader state, decision history.

memory store; restrictions to level- $\mathsf{U}$ /
level- $\mathbb{E}$ blocks.

block: identifier (two-sorted, operation-determined,
Rule R1(g)), bytes, label, interpreter-only provenance,
tool scope.

 $\ell(b)\in\{\mathsf{U},\mathsf{T},\mathsf{C},\mathsf{CC},\mathsf{PC}\}$ 

label: untrusted, trusted (user-typed),
confirmed, community-confirmed, $P$ -composed.

 $\mathrm{lvl};\ \mathbb{E}$ 

level map ( $\mathrm{lvl}(\mathsf{U})=\mathsf{U}$ , else $\mathbb{E}$ ); endorsed level.

 $P,\ Q,\ R_{\mathcal{E}}$ 

Protected LLM; Quarantined Reader; trusted-store
retriever (selector).

 $\mathsf{Tools}_{\mathrm{sens}},\ \mathsf{Tools}_{\mathrm{data}}$ 

sensitive (effectful) / data tools; fixed partition,
canonical identifiers.

Disambiguation. $h$ (decision history) vs. $h_{j}$ 
(handles): subscripted occurrences are always handles; $H(c)$ (handle
structure), $H_{\mathrm{acc}}$ (honest acceptors), and $H_{M}$ (Zipf
head set) are unrelated to one another and to $h$ . $\mathsf{C}$ (label) vs. $C_{u},C_{v},\bar{C}_{M}$ (confirmation counts): the label is sans-serif and
unsubscripted. $Z$ (Zipf normaliser) vs. $Z_{i}$ (decision indicators):
subscripted occurrences are always indicators. $\pi_{\mathrm{ord}},\pi_{\mathrm{pan}},\pi_{\mathrm{blast}}$ 
(blast-schedule promotion probabilities, §S8.2) vs. $\pi(b)$ (interpreter-only block provenance,
Definition S2.2): the former are always subscripted and take
a reach argument, the latter never is; no bare $\pi(\cdot)$ denotes a
probability anywhere in this document. $S(J)$ (blast generating sum) is
unrelated to $S_{\mathrm{anc}}$ (anchored attempts,
Cor. S5.27), to $S_{\mathrm{sh}}(L)$ (shared-tier replay,
Definition S4.1), and to the bare $S$ of
A5 (a crystallized summary, which takes no argument).

## S1.3 Standing conventions

(C1) 

Determinism modulo coins. A single coin sequence $\omega$ 
jointly covers the sampling randomness of $P$ and of $R_{\mathcal{E}}$ ; given
 $\omega$ , both are deterministic functions of their inputs. All two-run
comparisons (Thm. S3.25, Prop. S5.10,
Prop. S8.20) couple $\omega$ , the user’s typed stream
(A6), and (in the federated setting) the exogenous federation-log prefix (A8; local suffixes derived equal, Def. S4.2),
identically across the compared runs.

(C2) 

Probability space and adversary. All federated-protocol randomness
lives on the filtration $(\mathcal{F}_{s})$ generated by logged protocol
events and display events; the adversary’s strategy (presentation,
submission, stopping, self-dispute) is predictable with respect to it.
The construction, and the two structural constraints on adaptivity it
implies, are given once in §S8.1 and used everywhere.

(C3) 

Log order. The federation maintains a single linearizable,
append-only log of protocol events: a TCB log service (a trusted
sequencer or a consensus layer) supplies the total order, and its history
is materialized as a Certificate-Transparency-style Merkle tree for
tamper-evident audit — the tree authenticates the history; the
sequencing service, assumed correct as TCB, provides the linearizability.
It supplies the total order on decisions, demand events, and verdicts
that Rules R12–R14 invoke.
“The federation log,” “the log,” and “log order” all refer to
this object and the order it induces. The hash-chained audit log that
materializes the display transcript (Definition S3.8)
is a distinct, per-deployment TCB component and is never called “the
log.”

(C4) 

Epochs, leases, rounds. Three multiplicities the
development keeps distinct.
(a) Epochs. An epoch is a fixed span of the
federation log (a fixed number of sequence numbers or a fixed calendar
period), common to all items and identities, readable off the log (TCB);
every renewable per-epoch quantity ( $\rho_{\mathrm{sub}}$ , $E_{\max}$ , notification
deduplication, the accountability allowances of Rule R13(v),
(vii)) is indexed to it.
(b) Leases. A lease is the deployment-selected local scope
lifetime of the requirements-level option O3 (O3; not
part of the formal semantics); $E_{\mathrm{lease}}=\mathcal{O}(1)$ counts lease
periods per (user, item) in the extension-level accounting of
O3(e).
(c) Rounds. A re-verification round re-presents a
community-confirmed item to inspectors with no prior decision on its
bytes; $E_{\mathrm{rv}}$ counts rounds.
(d) Permanence and boundaries. Decision freshness
(Def. S4.5) and verdict capacity
(Rule R13(iii)) are permanent and attach to none of the
three: they never renew. Promotion weight and banked credits persist
across epoch boundaries until the item retires. Finalisation windows are
contained within epochs: a window that would cross a boundary closes at
the boundary, banking its accrued endorsements to the item’s next entry
exactly as a dormant-interval decision would
(Def. S4.6); a boundary never finalises a promotion early
and never discards honest effort. A boundary-spanning dispute counts
toward Rule R14(i) in the epoch in which it opened.

(C5) 

Byte identity. Items, freshness, disputes, and panels are
keyed to exact byte strings. Paraphrase variants are distinct items
(consequences: S1).

(C6) 

Confirmations. A confirmation is a user approval—an accepting gate
decision; it is durable (its fresh endorsed copy is written even when
another review aborts the enclosing invocation, Rule R2)
and is counted once per (value, tool) pair per gate transaction
(Definition S2.5). Rejecting decisions, abandoned
dialogs, batch-element page approvals, and repeated within-transaction
reviews of an already-accepted pair are attention cost, counted
separately (N18). Required, voluntary, and
redundant confirmations are defined in §S6.1:
the confirmation cap (Thm. S6.2) counts required
pair-confirmations, the ledger (Lem. S6.7) required and
voluntary; redundant confirmations are protocol-inert
(Rule R13(i)) and excluded from both. Here, required means
needed for the user’s intended use, not an obligation to accept.

(C7) 

Store idealization. The store is idealized as unbounded: no
operation’s defined behaviour depends on aggregate store volume. A
deployment enforcing quotas must apply them to the untrusted tier by
silent eviction, never by an error visible to $P$ (otherwise store
exhaustion reopens exactly the channel Rule R7 closes; S7).

(C8) 

Constants vs. schedules. $N,k,m,W,D_{\mathrm{pool}},T_{\mathrm{win}},T_{\mathrm{exp}}$ ,
 $E_{\max},\rho_{\mathrm{sub}},r_{\max},c_{\mathrm{ceil}},c_{\mathrm{batch}},c_{\mathrm{cand}},c_{\mathrm{p}}$ are fixed TCB
configuration at any deployment; Appendix S6 additionally
studies schedules $N(M),k(M),\dots$ along a growing population,
and every scheduled claim is confined to the corridor stated there.
All caps and multiplicity constants are positive integers where
integral; in particular $E_{\max}\geq 1$ and $c_{\mathrm{tool}}\geq 1$ (used by
Lemma S8.18).

(C9) 

Environment scheduling. An interpreter step has a fixed
slot structure: first the channel slots deliver at most one typed event
(W-Typed) and at most one paste event (W-Paste), in
that order; then $P$ emits exactly one operation, whose environment
interaction (tool returns, reader outputs, retriever selection, gate
decisions) occurs synchronously within that operation’s atomic
transition. Two-run comparisons (Theorem S3.25,
Proposition S5.10) couple only this slot skeleton and
the typed stream (A6): paste presence and content are
uncoupled and may differ arbitrarily across the compared attacks, as
may every tool output and reader output. Channel-slot transitions
acknowledge the channel, never $P$ : nothing is appended to $P$ ’s reply
stream by W-Typed or W-Paste
(Figure S3, convention (vi)). Two-run comparisons are always taken at a common horizon: the
compared runs consist of the same number of interpreter slots, and
transcript equality means equality of the two full transcripts at that
horizon.

## S1.4 Named invariants and structural properties

The following six named facts are cited constantly and are collected here;
together they are the structural core of the design. They are
heterogeneous in kind, and each entry declares its kind: rule
property (a direct consequence of the cited rule and definition clauses);
conditional inductive invariant (preserved by every transition,
under the named first-party exceptions); protocol property (a fact
of the counting and pacing rules); bound metaproperty (a property
of the bounds of the cited theorems, holding under those theorems’
hypotheses). None is an axiom: each is established at the location cited,
and nothing beyond the cited hypotheses is assumed. Invariants
INV-1–INV-6 are stated in the vocabulary of the
federated protocol, defined in Appendix S4.

Invariant INV-1 (Byte-permanence of freshness).

An attested identity’s first logged decision on a byte string
— its first decision taken while the bytes have a shared-tier
presence (Definition S4.5) — is its only weight-bearing
inspection of those bytes, ever: every later accepting decision by
that identity (at any tool, in any pipeline entry, in any epoch, in
any later shared-tier presence of the same bytes) carries no
promotion weight and no verdict capacity. Freshness is decidable
from the federation log (convention (C3)) and never renews; personal
decisions outside any shared-tier presence are not logged and are
outside this guarantee (Definition S4.5).
Kind: rule property. Established by Definition S4.5 and the counting rule
Definition S4.6. (Byte-keyed: paraphrase variants are
distinct items, (C5), S1.)

Invariant INV-2 (Rejection asymmetry).

A rejecting decision can freeze promotion, corroborate an open
dispute, or trigger post-promotion review; it can never promote and
never vindicate. A repeat acceptance carries no promotion
weight and no verdict (INV-1). Hence re-exposure of bytes
to a previously logged-decided inspector can only add
rejection opportunities; exposure to a log-fresh inspector adds both
a rejection opportunity and a possible weight-bearing acceptance,
priced by the hypotheses of Theorem S5.13.
Kind: rule property. Established by
Rule R13(i)–(iii) with Definition S4.5.

Invariant INV-3 (Single-approval cap, per (user, value, tool)).

No user is ever required to confirm a given (value, tool) pair
more than once over the deployment’s lifetime: a user’s first
accepting review of value $v$ at tool $t$ — at any gate transaction,
including one whose enclosing invocation aborts (Rule R2)
— durably writes the local $\mathsf{C}$ copy with $t\in\mathsf{scope}$ , which
covers all their later use of $v$ at $t$ and survives every
third-party protocol event; and within one transaction, confirmations
are counted once per pair (Definition S2.5). A first use
of the same bytes at a different tool $t^{\prime}$ is a distinct required
confirmation, of the pair $(v,t^{\prime})$ , itself once ever; the pairs per
item are bounded by A12 in the budget model. The disclosed exception within the formal semantics is first-party:
the owner’s own agent’s ungated $\mathsf{evict}$ (N13); a
deployment adopting a conforming instantiation of the
requirements-level Option O3 additionally admits lease
expiry, an extension outside this document’s semantics
(O3). Kind: conditional inductive invariant (exception:
first-party evict, N13; extension-level: conforming-O3
lease expiry, O3). Established by
Lemma S6.1 (from Rules R2,
R4, A1–A2, and
Lemma S5.8); reasoning pairs covered per the reasoning-coverage clause of Rule R13(i); consumed by Theorem S6.2
and Lemma S6.7(i).

Invariant INV-4 (Banking safety).

A banked credit (a first decision logged during dormancy or during a
freeze with no open entry) raises only the starting weight of the
item’s next pipeline entry. Nothing finalises promotion except an open
entry’s window end, so banking never bypasses a window’s rejection
opportunity and never accelerates finalisation past a window boundary; and
each banked decision remains its identity’s single fresh inspection of the
bytes, so successive entries’ weight-bearing sets stay identity-disjoint
(Lemma S5.3). Banked credits expire if the item retires
first. Every banked decision is logged after its attempt’s anchor (Definition S5.1), so banking never lets a credited decision precede the conditioning point of Theorem S5.13. Kind: protocol property. Established by Definition S4.6 with
Rule R13(iv).

Invariant INV-5 (Verdict non-renewal).

An identity casts at most one verdict on a given dispute instance, for
the instance’s entire life, across any number of epoch boundaries.
Verdict capacity is consumed only by cast verdicts (abandonment
consumes nothing) and, like freshness, never renews.
Kind: rule property. Established by Rule R13(iii).

Invariant INV-6 (Reach-freeness).

The federated safety bounds depend only on protocol constants
( $N,k,m,D_{\mathrm{pool}}$ ) and the adversary’s resources ( $f,f_{\mathrm{s}},W_{\mathrm{adv}}$ ), never on
the number of honest identities to whom a poisoned item is presented:
the same stated upper bound applies at every reach. (No
monotonicity in reach is claimed: presenting the bytes to log-fresh
inspectors creates both rejection opportunities and possible
weight-bearing acceptances, INV-2; what is proved is
that the bound does not degrade with reach.) Reach-freeness is relative to the stated parameters: the bounds’
form is reach-independent, while the behavioural constants
 $q_{\mathrm{pre}},q_{\mathrm{pan}}$ are weakest-target infima over all cohorts the adversary
can reach (Remark S5.19), so widening reach can lower the applicable
constants without changing the bound’s form. Kind: bound metaproperty. The property is
Definition S4.8; it is a property of the stated bounds of Theorems S5.11 and S5.13, holding under their hypotheses, not a property of reachable configurations; contrast with the
reach-dependent no-dispute baseline, Theorem S8.9.

## Appendix S2 Formal Specification of the Memory Interface

PerMIT places no restriction on which tools the Protected LLM may invoke
or on how it reasons; the harness (the fixed
interpreter code of Figures S2–S3 together
with the display and logging paths) bounds two things only: what $P$ can
observe, and what may cross a tool boundary. The bounded
surface is the memory interface: the $P$ -facing operation
surface

 $\displaystyle\Sigma_{P}\;=\;\Sigma\;\cup\;\{\mathsf{tool}(\bar{a}):\ \mathsf{tool}\in\mathsf{Tools}_{\mathrm{sens}}\cup\mathsf{Tools}_{\mathrm{data}}\}\;\cup\;\{\mathsf{retrieve}\},$ 

 $\displaystyle\Sigma=\{\mathsf{write}_{P},\mathsf{recall},\mathsf{fill},\mathsf{crystallize},\mathsf{display},\mathsf{confirm},\mathsf{evict}\},$ 

( $\mathsf{write}$ , where it appears in prose, abbreviates the sole
 $P$ -emitted write operation $\mathsf{write}_{P}$ .)
together with one interpreter-internal derived form ( $\mathsf{map}$ ).
Every completeness claim below (Lemmas S3.17, S3.18)
enumerates over $\Sigma_{P}$ , which coincides arm-for-arm with the mechanized
operation type (POp, §S9).
Because this surface is finite, its interface transition schema
is specified clause-complete at the interface alphabet: every operation of $\Sigma_{P}$ 
and channel event has its
store effect, reply, transcript events, and sanitized-history events fixed by Figures S2–S3 with these rules, with
totality per Rule R10(b); the full run state these transitions
carry is fixed by Definition S2.6. What the figures deliberately do not formalise is
 $P$ ’s internal computation (a strategy, §S2.1) and the
UI-side rendering internals of Rule R8; the channel
analysis of Appendix S3 needs only the interface schema,
and is verified clause by clause against it, with the totality clause of Rule R10 ensuring
that no invocation has undefined or distinguishing behaviour.
(Model lineage and design rationale: N1.)

## S2.1 Configurations, labels, and roles

Definition S2.1 (Trust lattice and labels).

The integrity lattice has two levels, $\mathsf{U}\sqsubset\mathbb{E}$ . Each block carries
a label $\ell(b)\in\{\mathsf{U},\mathsf{T},\mathsf{C},\mathsf{CC},\mathsf{PC}\}$ ; the level map $\mathrm{lvl}$ sends
 $\mathrm{lvl}(\mathsf{U})=\mathsf{U}$ and $\mathrm{lvl}(\ell)=\mathbb{E}$ for
 $\ell\in\{\mathsf{T},\mathsf{C},\mathsf{CC},\mathsf{PC}\}$ . The four $\mathbb{E}$ -level labels are
provenance-and-reuse tags, not a finer trust ordering: $\mathsf{T}$ (user-typed;
reusable by its owner), $\mathsf{C}$ (single-user confirmed; admissible only to
the tools in its scope, reusable without re-confirmation only by its
owner), $\mathsf{CC}$ (community-confirmed, Appendix S4;
admissible only to the exact targets endorsed (tools, or the reasoning pseudo-target, §S4.1), reusable by anyone), $\mathsf{PC}$ 
( $P$ -composed; empty tool scope, hence never eligible for the sensitive
fast path of Rule R4). Label combination is the meet on
levels: an operation combining inputs yields level $\mathsf{U}$ if any input is
at level $\mathsf{U}$ , else level $\mathbb{E}$ ; no operation in $\Sigma$ raises an
untrusted value to level $\mathbb{E}$ by combination.

Definition S2.2 (Store and configuration).

A block is $b=(\mathrm{id},\mathrm{bytes}(b),\ell(b),\pi(b),\mathsf{scope}(b))$ 
with interpreter-only provenance
 $\pi(b)\in\{\mathsf{typed},\mathsf{pasted},\mathsf{ingress},\mathsf{cryst},\mathsf{pcomposed},\mathsf{confirmed},\mathsf{cache}\}$ ,
assigned only by the interpreter (provenance is interpreter-private
and not part of $\mathsf{obs}_{P}$ ), and, for endorsed blocks, a tool
scope $\mathsf{scope}(b)\subseteq\mathsf{Tools}_{\mathrm{sens}}\cup\mathsf{Tools}_{\mathrm{data}}$ (a set of individual tool
identifiers). Every block-creating transition of
Figures S2–S3 instantiates one of the total
constructors

 $\displaystyle\operatorname{newT}(v)$ 
 $\displaystyle:=(\mathrm{id}^{\mathbb{E}}_{\bullet},v,\mathsf{T},\mathsf{typed},\varnothing),$ 
 $\displaystyle\operatorname{newPC}(v)$ 
 $\displaystyle:=(\mathrm{id}^{\mathbb{E}}_{\bullet},v,\mathsf{PC},\mathsf{pcomposed},\varnothing),$ 

 $\displaystyle\operatorname{newC}(v,t)$ 
 $\displaystyle:=(\mathrm{id}^{\mathbb{E}}_{\bullet},v,\mathsf{C},\mathsf{confirmed},\sigma(t)),$ 
 $\displaystyle\operatorname{newCache}(v,t)$ 
 $\displaystyle:=(\mathrm{id}^{\mathbb{E}}_{\bullet},v,\mathsf{C},\mathsf{cache},\sigma(t)),$ 

 $\displaystyle\operatorname{newU}(v,\pi)$ 
 $\displaystyle:=(\mathrm{id}^{\mathsf{U}}_{\bullet},v,\mathsf{U},\pi,\varnothing),$ 

where $\mathrm{id}^{\mathbb{E}}_{\bullet}$ (resp. $\mathrm{id}^{\mathsf{U}}_{\bullet}$ )
is the next identifier of the endorsed (resp. untrusted) sort
(Rule R1(g)) and $\sigma(t)=\{t\}$ for a tool target,
 $\sigma(\mathrm{reasoning})=\varnothing$ ; the cache confirmations of
Options O1–O2 use $\operatorname{newCache}$ , all
other accepting confirmations $\operatorname{newC}$ . Tool identifiers
are canonical, fixed TCB configuration, so scope membership is a
decidable syntactic test. Block identifiers are two-sorted and
operation-determined (Rule R1(g)); the sorts separate the
tiers: an endorsed-sort identifier resolves only to an $\mathbb{E}$ -level
block, an untrusted-sort identifier only to a $\mathsf{U}$ -block.

A configuration is $c=(\mathcal{M},\sigma_{P},\sigma_{Q},h)$ : the store, the
Protected-LLM state, the Reader state, and the interpreter’s sanitized
decision history $h$ : a sequence of (target, decision) records, one
appended at each reached gating review (one per reached gating
position; for a batch, one per gating non- $\diamond$ body position plus
one aggregate element decision) — never bytes, never a block
identifier of either sort, never a selection ordinal, never a candidate
multiplicity, collection cardinality, or page count; selections,
candidate lists, page renderings, and per-page decisions are recorded
in the display transcript only (Definition S3.8). A
null-handle abort appends the same (target, reject) record a user
rejection appends (Rule R6). Hence $h$ contains no
 $\mathsf{U}$ -bytes, exposes no untrusted-sort identifier
(Rule R1(g)), and carries no occupancy, cardinality, or
pagination information (Rules R6, R7); this is the
form machine-checked as HEvent (§S9).
 $\mathcal{M}_{\mathsf{U}}$ and $\mathcal{M}_{\mathbb{E}}$ denote the restrictions of $\mathcal{M}$ to levels
 $\mathsf{U}$ and $\mathbb{E}$ .

Endorsement unit.

The endorsement unit throughout is the (value, tool) pair: every
confirmation names an exact byte string and an exact tool, and there is
no endorsement transfer between tools, however similar.
(Exact-target discipline governs the sensitive fast path and all
endorsement, promotion, and counting; the baseline data-tool
path of Rule R4(b) is scope-blind,
Corollary S3.23(b), and Option O2 extends the
exact-target discipline to data tools.)
Combinations,
orderings, and timings of separately endorsed values are
ungated by design (rationale and residual: N2, S11;
hardening: Option O1).

Individuation of trusted blocks.

 $\mathsf{T}$ -blocks are individuated at write time: each field the user
explicitly supplies (or, absent field structure, the command as one
atomic unit) is one $\mathsf{T}$ -block. The individuating parser is fixed
interpreter code inside the TCB; the fast-path test of
Rule R4 is byte-equality against these stored blocks.

Two scopes and one selector.

The agent is a transition system over configurations whose steps are
tool invocations, control flow, and the operations of
Figures S2–S3, executed in one of two scopes: the
privileged scope $P$ may invoke any tool and reaches memory only
through $\Sigma$ . Throughout the formal development $P$ is modelled as a
strategy: a function from its interaction history, its own
emitted operations, the reply stream of Rule R10, its view
 $\mathsf{obs}_{P}(c)$ , and the coins $\omega$ (convention (C1)), to its next
operation. Its internal state $\sigma_{P}$ is therefore an abbreviation
for that history and carries no independent degrees of freedom: two
runs agreeing on the history agree on $\sigma_{P}$ by definition. This is
not a restriction on $P$ (the strategy is arbitrary, adversarial
included) but the standard determinism-modulo-coins reading of (C1),
and it is the form in which $P$ is machine-checked
(§S9).
The quarantined scope $Q$ has read-only access
to the whole store and no tool access. A third model role, the
trusted-store retriever $R_{\mathcal{E}}$ (Rule R11), is a
stateless selector over $\mathcal{M}_{\mathbb{E}}$ : it reads only the endorsed store
and returns only block identifiers, which the interpreter dereferences.
(The asymmetry between $Q$ ’s and $R_{\mathcal{E}}$ ’s vision is forced by the
theorems: N3.)

Definition S2.3 (Protected-LLM view).

For a configuration $c$ , let $H(c)$ denote the handle structure of
Rule R1 and $x(c)$ the trusted command input received so
far; $h$ is the decision history. The Protected-LLM view is

 $\mathsf{obs}_{P}(c)\;=\;\big(\mathcal{M}_{\mathbb{E}},\;H(c),\;x(c),\;h\big).$ 

By Figures S2–S3 and Rules R1–R11, $\mathsf{obs}_{P}(c)$ 
together with the reply stream of Rule R10 is the entirety
of what $P$ can observe (Lemma S3.17).

Remark S2.4 (Information interface vs. effect interface).

 $\Sigma$ is the interface of effects; $\mathsf{obs}_{P}(c)$ is the interface of
information. Granting $P$ direct sight of $\mathcal{M}_{\mathbb{E}}$ in its view is
a deliberate, conservative strengthening: every theorem quantifies over all
strategies of this better-informed $P$ , so it a fortiori covers any $P$ 
that must acquire the same data operationally, and the two-run theorem is
harder under it (a more informed $P$ has more ways to diverge).
 $\mathsf{recall}$ on endorsed identifiers is consequently informationally
redundant and is retained as the operational access path; the sentence
“ $P$ reaches memory only through $\Sigma$ ” concerns effects, not
information. The mechanization adopts exactly this reading
(§S9: obs is computed through
memEOf).

Definition S2.5 (Sensitive sinks, gate transactions, and confirmations).

A sensitive sink event is a call
 $\mathsf{tool}(a_{1},\dots,a_{n})$ with $\mathsf{tool}\in\mathsf{Tools}_{\mathrm{sens}}$ .

A gating position of a tool invocation is an argument position
that does not pass the fast path of Rule R4. The
invocation’s gating positions are processed left to right as one atomic
gate transaction (Rule R2): at each reached position
the interpreter resolves the argument, displays its bytes with zoned
provenance (Rule R8), and reads the user’s selection (if
among candidates) and decision $z\in\{\mathrm{accept},\mathrm{reject}\}$ ;
a null resolution renders the fixed no-value display of
Rule R6 and takes the standard reject outcome, consuming no
decision; a rejecting outcome aborts the review of the remaining
positions.

An accepting review of resolved bytes $v$ at target $t$ is a
confirmation event $\mathsf{confirm}(v,t)$ . Confirmations are
durable: at the close of the atomic transaction the interpreter
writes, for every accepted gating position, one fresh endorsed copy of
its displayed bytes scoped to the target
(Figure S3, convention (vii)), whether or not the
transaction as a whole succeeds. The tool itself executes iff every
gating position was accepted and none was null; on execution the bytes
that flow at each position are exactly the bytes displayed for it, and
on abort no argument flows.

Counting unit. For all accounting
(Appendices S4, S6), confirmations are
counted per (value, tool) pair per gate transaction: accepting
reviews of the same exact pair at several positions of one transaction
are one pair-confirmation; the repeated reviews themselves are
attention cost (N18).

A standalone $\mathsf{confirm}(v,t)$ with $t$ a tool or “reasoning” is the
single-position special case. The “publish” target is not a local
confirmation: it creates no local block and is defined by the
Publish transitions of Figure S3 and
Rule R12. Batch-element page approvals under
Rule R9 are call-local: they authorize the displayed
elements for that $\mathsf{map}$ instance only and create no durable
copy; gating non- $\diamond$ arguments of a map body are ordinary
durable confirmations (convention (vii′)).

## S2.2 Operational semantics

Figures S2–S3 give the interface transition
schema of the memory interface: complete at the level of the interface
alphabet (every operation’s store effect and reply is fixed here, with
totality per Rule R10(b)), with the remaining components of the
run state — fixed by Definition S2.6 — evolving by the
deterministic bookkeeping stated in the rules and conventions.
Every transition is fixed interpreter code; no model
participates in any label assignment or any reply. Replies to $P$ are
drawn from the grammar at the top of Figure S2 and nothing else
(Rule R10); the side conditions cite the Rules of
§S2.3, which constrain naming, atomicity, display, and
gating. Transitions triggered by the user or the environment (channel
events, gate decisions, tool returns) are marked with their trigger;
all others are $P$ -emitted.

Definition S2.6 (Extended configuration).

The full run state comprises: the store $\mathcal{M}$ ; the two identifier
allocators of Rule R1(g) (endorsed-sort and untrusted-sort); the
interpreter-private handle-resolution table (index $=$ handle identifier;
never read by any reply, Rule R7); $P$ ’s reply stream
(Rule R10); the decision history $h$ ; the trusted command input
 $x(c)$ ; the executed-tool, confirmation, and reader-event counters that
index the environment’s oracles; and three ghost logs used only by the
theorems — the display transcript $\mathsf{DT}$ 
(Definition S3.8), the sensitive-sink log
(Definition S3.7), and $P$ ’s emitted-operation log.
 $P$ -visible components are exactly $\mathsf{obs}_{P}(c)$ and the reply stream
(Lemma S3.17); all others are interpreter-private or ghost. A
block is readable iff it is at level $\mathbb{E}$ , i.e. dereferenceable to
bytes by $\mathsf{recall}$ (Recall-E); $\mathsf{U}$ -blocks are never
readable to $P$ . For the map-free fragment, the executable interpreter of
§S9 realizes this extended configuration verbatim
(IState), and the figures are its human-readable projection onto
the store and reply components.

Replies to $P$ (Rule R10)

 $\displaystyle r\;::=\;\mathsf{ack}\;\mid\;h_{j}\;\mid\;\mathsf{fail}\;\mid\;\mathsf{dec}(\mathrm{accept})\;\mid\;\mathsf{dec}(\mathrm{reject})\;\mid\;\mathsf{bytes}(b),\ b\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathbb{E}}$ 

Replies to $P$ form a stream; the grammar above
is its alphabet. A single transition may append more than one token
(e.g. Tool-Gate-Acc appends
 $\mathsf{dec}(\mathrm{accept})\cdot\mathsf{ack}$ ), per convention (iii).

Judgment.  $\mathcal{M}\xrightarrow{\;o\;\Downarrow\;r\;}\mathcal{M}^{\prime}$ : operation $o$ yields
reply $r$ ; components of $c$ other than $\mathcal{M}$ are suppressed when
unchanged; $h_{j}$ is the $j$ -th operation-determined handle
(Rule R1). Transitions triggered by the user or the
environment are marked by their trigger; all others are $P$ -emitted.
Conclusions suppress the operation label over the arrow where the
premises display it; the components of the extended configuration
(Definition S2.6) other than $\mathcal{M}$ evolve by the
deterministic bookkeeping stated in the rules and conventions, and the
figures are the store/reply projection of that full-state transition,
realized executably (for the map-free fragment) by the mechanization
(IState, §S9).

Tool invocation.

 $\frac{P\text{ emits }\mathsf{tool}(\bar{a})\quad\text{every }a_{i}\text{ passes the fast path of
Rule~\ref{S-r:whitelist}}\quad\text{environment returns }o\ \text{(attacker-chosen bytes and
count)}}{\mathcal{M}\xrightarrow{\;\mathsf{tool}(\bar{a})\,\Downarrow\,\mathsf{ack}\;}\mathcal{M}\cup\mathrm{capture}(o)}\quad\textsc{lab=Tool-Fast}\frac{P\text{ emits }\mathsf{tool}(\bar{a})\quad\text{some }a_{i}\text{ gate (Rule~\ref{S-r:whitelist})}\quad\text{every gating position is confirmed and accepted
(Rules~\ref{S-r:atomic},\,\ref{S-r:display})}\quad\text{environment returns }o\ \text{(attacker-chosen bytes and
count)}\quad\mathcal{M}^{\prime}=\mathcal{M}\cup\bigl\{\operatorname{newC}(\text{displayed }a_{i},\mathsf{tool}):\ \begin{subarray}{c}\text{per accepted gating}\quad\text{position (conv.~\hyperref@@ii[S-sos:vii]{(vii)})}\end{subarray}\bigr\}}{\mathcal{M}\xrightarrow{\;\mathsf{tool}(\bar{a})\,\Downarrow\,\mathsf{dec}(\mathrm{accept})\cdot\mathsf{ack}\;}\mathcal{M}^{\prime}\cup\mathrm{capture}(o)}\quad\textsc{lab=Tool-Gate-Acc,
right={atomic (\ref{S-r:atomic}); fresh ids (\ref{S-r:names}(g))}}\frac{P\text{ emits }\mathsf{tool}(\bar{a})\quad\text{gate fires; some gating position is rejected by the user, or
null-aborts (Rule~\ref{S-r:null})}\quad\mathcal{M}^{\prime}=\mathcal{M}\cup\bigl\{\operatorname{newC}(\text{displayed }a_{i},\mathsf{tool}):\ \begin{subarray}{c}\text{per gating position}\quad\text{accepted before the abort}\end{subarray}\bigr\}}{\mathcal{M}\xrightarrow{\;\mathsf{tool}(\bar{a})\,\Downarrow\,\mathsf{dec}(\mathrm{reject})\;}\mathcal{M}^{\prime}}\quad\textsc{lab=Tool-Gate-Rej,
right={\shortstack[l]{no invocation; accepted positions\\
endorsed (\ref{S-r:atomic}); $h$ extended}}}$ extended 

Writes.

 $\frac{\text{channel event: }\mathrm{typed}(x)\quad\mathrm{parse}_{\mathsf{T}}(x)=b_{1},\dots,b_{n}\ (\text{TCB})}{\mathcal{M}\xrightarrow{\;\Downarrow\,\mathsf{ack}\;}\mathcal{M}\cup\{\operatorname{newT}(b_{1}),\dots,\operatorname{newT}(b_{n})\}}\quad\textsc{lab=W-Typed}\frac{\text{channel event: }\mathrm{paste}(x)}{\mathcal{M}\xrightarrow{\;\Downarrow\,\mathsf{ack}\;}\mathcal{M}\cup\{\operatorname{newU}(b_{x},\mathsf{pasted})\}}\quad\textsc{lab=W-Paste}\frac{b\in\sigma_{P}}{\mathcal{M}\xrightarrow{\;\mathsf{write}_{P}(b)\,\Downarrow\,\mathsf{ack}\;}\mathcal{M}\cup\{\operatorname{newPC}(b)\}}\quad\textsc{lab=W-P}$ 

Memory reads and display.

 $\frac{\mathrm{id}\ \text{resolves to a readable }\mathbb{E}\text{-block }b}{\mathcal{M}\xrightarrow{\;\mathsf{recall}(\mathrm{id})\,\Downarrow\,\mathsf{bytes}(b)\;}\mathcal{M}}\quad\textsc{lab=Recall-E}\frac{\text{otherwise: }\mathsf{U}\text{-block, unknown, or nonexistent
(uniformly)}}{\mathcal{M}\xrightarrow{\;\mathsf{recall}(\mathrm{id})\,\Downarrow\,h_{j}\;}\mathcal{M}}\quad\textsc{lab=Recall-O}\frac{P\ \text{emits }\mathsf{retrieve}(q)\quad R_{\mathcal{E}}(q,\mathcal{M}_{\mathbb{E}},\omega)\text{ returns an id resolving to }b\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathbb{E}}}{\mathcal{M}\xrightarrow{\;\Downarrow\,\mathsf{bytes}(b)\;}\mathcal{M}}\quad\textsc{lab=Retrieve, right={verbatim dereference (\ref{S-r:retrieve})}}\frac{R_{\mathcal{E}}\text{ returns no id, or an id not resolving in }\mathcal{M}_{\mathbb{E}}}{\mathcal{M}\xrightarrow{\;\mathsf{retrieve}(q)\,\Downarrow\,\mathsf{fail}\;}\mathcal{M}}\quad\textsc{lab=Retrieve-F}\frac{\text{rendering per Rule~\ref{S-r:display}; nothing enters }\sigma_{P}}{\mathcal{M}\xrightarrow{\;\mathsf{display}(x)\,\Downarrow\,\mathsf{ack}\;}\mathcal{M}}\quad\textsc{lab=Display}$ 

Figure S2: Interface transition schema of the memory interface (1/2): tool
invocation, writes, memory reads, and display. Every transition is
fixed interpreter code; no model assigns any label or composes any
reply. Continued in Figure S3 (search, crystallization,
confirmation, eviction, iteration, and failure); conventions for both
figures follow Figure S3.

Search and crystallization.

 $\frac{Q\ \text{searches }\mathcal{M}_{\mathsf{U}}\quad\text{interpreter binds}\leq c_{\mathrm{cand}}\text{ candidates internally
(possibly none)}}{\mathcal{M}\xrightarrow{\;\mathsf{fill}(q)\,\Downarrow\,h_{j}\;}\mathcal{M}}\quad\textsc{lab=Fill,

Confirmation and eviction.

 $\frac{\text{gate }\mathsf{confirm}(v,t),\quad t\ \text{a tool or reasoning}\quad\text{user selects and accepts on the decision pane
(Rule~\ref{S-r:display})}}{\mathcal{M}\xrightarrow{\;\Downarrow\,\mathsf{dec}(\mathrm{accept})\;}\mathcal{M}\cup\bigl\{\operatorname{newC}(v,t)\bigr\}}\quad\textsc{lab=Conf-Acc,

right={bytes not returned; $h$ extended}}\frac{\text{gate }\mathsf{confirm}(v,\mathrm{publish})\quad v\ \text{byte-equals a local }\mathsf{C}\text{-block of the owner with

log}}\quad\textsc{lab=Publish-Acc,
right={shared-tier act; no local block, no endorsed id (\ref{S-r:share})}}\frac{\text{gate }\mathsf{confirm}(v,\mathrm{publish})\quad\text{owner rejects, or }v\ \text{is ineligible
(Rule~\ref{S-r:share}), or null (Rule~\ref{S-r:null})}}{\mathcal{M}\xrightarrow{\;\Downarrow\,\mathsf{dec}(\mathrm{reject})\;}\mathcal{M}}\quad\textsc{lab=Publish-Rej,
right={uniform reject; ineligibility, rejection, null alike
(\ref{S-r:nobit})}}\frac{P\ \text{emits }\mathsf{evict}(\mathrm{id})\quad\text{interpreter-side: if }\mathrm{id}\text{ resolves to a block
of the owner's local store,}\quad\text{that block is removed; otherwise the operation is a no-op}\quad\text{resolution status consumed interpreter-side
(Rules~\ref{S-r:nobit},\,\ref{S-r:iface}(b))}}{\mathcal{M}\xrightarrow{\;\mathsf{evict}(\mathrm{id})\,\Downarrow\,\mathsf{ack}\;}\mathcal{M}\ominus\mathrm{id}}\quad\textsc{lab=Evict,
right={uniform reply; no label change}}$ 

Iteration and failure.

 $\frac{P\ \text{emits }\mathsf{map}(\hat{o},H),\quad\hat{o}=\mathsf{tool}(\dots,\diamond,\dots)\ \text{well-formed (Rule~\ref{S-r:names}(f))}\quad\text{batch confirmation accepted on every page (Rule~\ref{S-r:batchgate})}\quad\text{environment returns }o_{1},\dots,o_{|H|}\ \text{per element

endorsements persist (\ref{S-r:atomic}); null collection\\
(\ref{S-r:names}(a),\,\ref{S-r:null})}}}\frac{P\ \text{emits }\mathsf{map}(\hat{o},H),\quad\hat{o}=\mathsf{crystallize}(\diamond)\quad Q\ \text{writes schema-bound summaries }s_{1},\dots,s_{|H|}\ \text{interpreter-side, one per element ({Cryst})}}{\mathcal{M}\xrightarrow{\;\Downarrow\,\mathsf{ack}\;}\mathcal{M}\cup\{s_{1},\dots,s_{|H|}:\ \ell=\mathsf{U}\}}\quad\textsc{lab=Map-Cryst, right={no decision; one handle, one ack (\ref{S-r:names}(c))}}\frac{P\ \text{emits well-formed }o\quad\text{a precondition fails, deterministically in }P\text{'s
emitted operations and }\mathcal{M}_{\mathbb{E}}\text{ alone
(Rule~\ref{S-r:nobit})}}{\mathcal{M}\xrightarrow{\;o\,\Downarrow\,\mathsf{fail}\;}\mathcal{M}}\quad\textsc{lab=Fail}$

Figure S3: Interface transition schema of the memory interface (2/2): search,
crystallization, confirmation, eviction, iteration, and failure,
continued from Figure S2. Every transition is fixed
interpreter code; no model assigns any label or composes any reply.
Conventions for both figures follow below.

Conventions (apply to Figures S2–S3).
(i) $\mathrm{capture}(o)$ stores the outputs $o$ as $\mathsf{U}$ -blocks
 $\operatorname{newU}(\cdot,\mathsf{ingress})$ bound
to a single fresh collection handle $h_{j}$ 
(Rules R1, R3); the identifier is
operation-determined, computable by $P$ , and never returned.
A call whose captured output count is zero binds its collection
handle with null resolution (Rule R6): a
zero-cardinality capture and an empty search result are the same
null-resolution case. The capture operation exposes no direct
cardinality or resolution bit to $P$ (Rule R7); any later
distinction surfaces only through the logged null-collection display
and its standard aggregated reject (Rule R9(b)).
(ii) Element calls inside Map are interpreter-iterated, not
 $P$ -emitted: individually neither handle-producing nor acknowledged.
If the argument collection has null resolution (an empty search
result or zero-output capture, convention (i)), the tool-free form
 $\mathsf{map}(\mathsf{crystallize}(\diamond),H)$ writes no
summaries, binds the map’s operation-determined output collection
handle with null resolution
(Rules R1(a),(c), R6), returns the ordinary
 $\mathsf{ack}$ , and appends no display or decision event; the
tool-bearing form takes the null-collection batch treatment of
Rule R9(b).
(iii) In Tool-Gate-Acc and Tool-Gate-Rej, gating
positions are reviewed left to right; one decision is recorded per
reached gating position, and the displayed bytes of an executed call
are exactly the bytes that flow; the entire step is atomic
(Rule R2). Every accepted position’s fresh $\mathsf{C}$ copy is
written at the close of the step, on the accepting and the aborting
branch alike; rejected and null positions never flow and write nothing.
The reply stream carries one decision token per gated
invocation, regardless of the number of gating positions; per-position
decisions are recorded in the transcript and in $h$ only
(Rule R10(a)(i),(iv)). Whether an invocation is gated at all
is a deterministic function of $P$ ’s own emitted argument list and
 $\mathcal{M}_{\mathbb{E}}$ (Rule R4), hence already $P$ -computable; the
token-shape difference between Tool-Fast,
Tool-Gate-Acc, and Tool-Gate-Rej therefore carries no
information beyond trusted data and the logged decisions
(Rule R7) (the $\mathsf{CC}$ branch’s live-entry bit is $\mathbb{E}$ -level
log data, coupled under A8 in two-run comparisons (exogenous prefix coupled; local suffixes derived equal, Def. S4.2)), and
within the reject class, user rejection and null-handle abort remain
indistinguishable in the reply and in $h$ (Rule R6).
(iv) Totality (Rule R10(b)): every well-formed
invocation with any argument is covered: unresolvable arguments take
Recall-O-style opaque-handle replies, failed deterministic
preconditions take Fail; no invocation is undefined and none
distinguishes attacker-influenced states.
(v) $\mathcal{M}\ominus\mathrm{id}$ denotes $\mathcal{M}\setminus\{\mathrm{id}\}$ 
when $\mathrm{id}$ resolves to a block of the owner’s local store,
and $\mathcal{M}$ otherwise. The reply is the constant $\mathsf{ack}$ in
every case; the realised outcome (removal of the owner’s block, at either level;
no-op on an identifier not resolving to a block of the owner’s local
store, including an unoccupied one) is
recorded in the audit log interpreter-side and never returned to
 $P$ . The effect on $\mathcal{M}_{\mathbb{E}}$ is $P$ -visible trusted data
(Rule R7); any effect on $\mathcal{M}_{\mathsf{U}}$ , and the absence of
any effect, are indistinguishable to $P$ . A handle argument
to $\mathsf{evict}$ is a deterministic malformation of $P$ ’s own
emission and takes the generic failure outcome
(Rule R10(a)(iii)).
(vi) Channel-triggered transitions (W-Typed, W-Paste)
acknowledge the channel; nothing is appended to $P$ ’s reply stream, so
the presence or absence of a paste event is not $P$ -observable
(Rule R7, convention (C9)).
(vii) Fresh-copy endorsement; block immutability. An accepting
confirmation never relabels a block in place: Conf-Acc and
Tool-Gate-Acc write, per accepted value, one fresh
 $\mathsf{C}$ -labelled block carrying exactly the displayed bytes, at the next
endorsed-sort identifier (Rule R1(g)), with scope
 $\sigma(t)=\{t\}$ for a tool target and
 $\sigma(\text{reasoning})=\varnothing$ (readable, but never eligible
for the sensitive fast path of Rule R4); the resolved
source block, where one exists, is unchanged and remains at its
level, and the publish target (Rule R12) is a shared-tier
act creating no local block. Endorsement is durable: the accepted positions’ fresh copies are
written at the close of the atomic step whether or not the enclosing
invocation executes (Rule R2). Consequently no transition of
Figures S2–S3 alters an existing block’s
bytes, label, or scope: blocks are immutable once written, and the
store changes only by creation of fresh blocks and by Evict.
(vii′) Batch acceptance is call-local for elements. An
accepting batch confirmation (Rule R9) authorizes each
 $\diamond$ -element’s bytes for its own element call of that
 $\mathsf{map}$ instance only: no persistent endorsed copy of any
element is created, and the elements remain $\mathsf{U}$ -labelled in
 $\mathcal{M}_{\mathsf{U}}$ . Fresh $\mathsf{C}$ -copies are written for the accepted
gating non- $\diamond$ arguments, one each, scoped
 $\{\mathsf{tool}\}$ , exactly as at Tool-Gate-Acc, and durably so: they persist
even when a later element page rejects and no element call executes
(Map-Tool-Rej). This is
forced by the identifier discipline of Rule R1(g):
per-element copies would allocate $|H|$ endorsed-sort identifiers
with $|H|$ attacker-chosen, exposing an attacker-dependent quantity
in $\mathcal{M}_{\mathbb{E}}$ ; the non- $\diamond$ count is fixed by $P$ ’s emitted
body. Convention (vii)’s per-accepted-value fresh copy, and the
endorsed-copy clause of Definition S2.5, are read with
this carve-out: they govern Conf-Acc and
Tool-Gate-Acc; batch-accepted elements flow under
Rule R2’s displayed-bytes binding without entering
 $\mathcal{M}_{\mathbb{E}}$ . Consequently re-processing the same elements in a later
 $\mathsf{map}$ is gated afresh — call-local authorization caches
nothing — and the single-approval cap (INV-3) does not
extend to map elements, whose confirmations are per-task cost
(Appendix S6, Scope).

Commentary
 (non-normative; the Map rules and the mechanization boundary). The split fixes, per body form, the decision token (none for the tool-free
form), the store effect ( $\mathrm{capture}$ of element outputs vs. schema-bound $\mathsf{U}$ -summaries $s_{1},\dots,s_{|H|}$ written as in Cryst),
and the endorsement effect (fresh $\mathsf{C}$ -copies for gating non- $\diamond$ 
arguments only; elements are authorized call-locally, convention (vii′)). In every
case, including rejection, exactly one collection-handle index is consumed,
on rejection with null resolution — so handle structure is independent of
the outcome (Rule R1(a)), closing the identifier channel a
skipped index would open. $\mathsf{map}$ is normative here; its batch-confirmation surface —
pagination, per-page decisions, the aggregated decision, all-or-nothing
displayed-bytes flow, and batch determinacy — is mechanized as a
standalone layer (BatchGate.lean, §S9),
while its store-level integration into the run-level interpreter remains
outside the mechanized surface (delta (6)).

## S2.3 Interface rules

The rules below constrain the transitions of
Figures S2–S3. Each clause is normative and
implementable as stated; all rationale lives in
Appendix S7 under the keys cited, or in the marked
commentary blocks of §S1.1.

Rule R1 (Operation-determined naming: handles and block identifiers).

(a) 

Handle identifiers (including collection-handle identifiers) are
drawn from the sequence of $P$ -emitted operations alone: the $j$ -th
handle-producing operation of a run binds identifier $h_{j}$ . Identifiers
never encode, count, or depend on store contents.

(b) 

Handle-producing operations: ingress capture (the triggering tool
invocation is $P$ -emitted; $P$ computes the collection handle’s
identifier from its own operation count; nothing is returned);
 $\mathsf{recall}$ returning a handle; $\mathsf{fill}$ ;
 $\mathsf{crystallize}$ ; $\mathsf{map}$ .

(c) 

A $\mathsf{map}$ invocation is exactly one handle-producing
operation regardless of collection cardinality: its per-element outputs
are captured as $\mathsf{U}$ -blocks bound to a single fresh collection handle
determined by the $\mathsf{map}$ ’s position in $P$ ’s operation sequence.
Element calls are interpreter-iterated, not $P$ -emitted, and are
individually neither handle-producing nor acknowledged.

(d) 

 $P$ reaches captured element outputs only through
 $\mathsf{fill}$ / $\mathsf{crystallize}$ searches. No handle identifier
depends on collection size.

(e) 

A collection handle admits exactly two uses: as the argument of
 $\mathsf{map}$ , or as a tool argument — the latter defined by
desugaring: an invocation $\mathsf{tool}(\dots,h,\dots)$ with a
collection-handle argument $h$ is the instance
 $\mathsf{map}(\mathsf{tool}(\dots,\diamond,\dots),h)$ , the desugaring
applied before the invocation matches any transition of
Figures S2–S3 (it never instantiates
Tool-Fast or Tool-Gate-*), batch-gated per
Rule R9 and subject to the body grammar of clause (f)
(in particular at most one collection handle per invocation and no
other handle argument; violations take the generic failure outcome,
deterministic in $P$ ’s own emission, Rule R7). A
collection handle exposes no length, no indexing, and no per-element
access.

(f) 

Map bodies. The body $\hat{o}$ of
 $\mathsf{map}(\hat{o},H)$ is a single operation template containing
exactly one occurrence of the distinguished element placeholder
 $\diamond$ , of one of exactly two forms: (i) one tool invocation
 $\mathsf{tool}(a_{1},\dots,a_{n})$ in which one argument is $\diamond$ 
and every other argument is a $P$ -supplied non-handle value, subject
per argument to the ordinary mediation of Rule R4
(fast-path values flow; a gating argument is confirmed once for the
whole batch, at the batch confirmation of Rule R9,
alongside the elements); or (ii) one tool-free
 $\mathsf{crystallize}(\diamond)$ template. The body may not contain
 $\mathsf{map}$ , $\mathsf{recall}$ , $\mathsf{fill}$ ,
 $\mathsf{evict}$ , $\mathsf{confirm}$ , $\mathsf{display}$ , the write operation $\mathsf{write}_{P}$ , or a
handle argument
(the interpreter-side captures of forms (i)–(ii) are transitions of
the interpreter, not body operations); nesting is not defined. Well-formedness is a
syntactic test on $P$ ’s emitted operation alone, and a body outside
this grammar takes the generic failure outcome, deterministic in
 $P$ ’s own emission (Rule R7). Multi-step per-element
pipelines are expressed as successive $\mathsf{map}$ s: each
 $\mathsf{map}$ yields a fresh collection handle (clause (c)), which
clause (e) admits as the argument of a further $\mathsf{map}$ , each
step batch-gated independently; the grammar restricts the shape of
one step, never the depth of a pipeline.

(g) 

Two-sorted block identifiers. Block identifiers are drawn
from two disjoint sorts, endorsed
( $\mathrm{id}^{\mathbb{E}}_{0},\mathrm{id}^{\mathbb{E}}_{1},\dots$ ) and untrusted
( $\mathrm{id}^{\mathsf{U}}_{0},\mathrm{id}^{\mathsf{U}}_{1},\dots$ ), each allocated in
sequence. Endorsed-sort identifiers are allocated by exactly the local
endorsed-block-creating transitions: W-Typed, W-P,
Conf-Acc, and, per accepted gating position,
Tool-Gate-Acc, Tool-Gate-Rej, Map-Tool-Acc,
and Map-Tool-Rej (for maps, gating non- $\diamond$ body
positions only — a count fixed by $P$ ’s emitted operation, never by
the collection cardinality, convention (vii′)). A publish decision
allocates no identifier of either sort (Publish-Acc). The
 $j$ -th endorsed block of a run sits at $\mathrm{id}^{\mathbb{E}}_{j}$ , a position
determined by the coupled event sequence (the typed stream, $P$ ’s
emitted operations, and the logged decisions) alone, hence computable
by $P$ and identical across runs whose coupled events agree.
Untrusted-sort identifiers are allocated by the untrusted-writing
transitions (W-Paste, ingress capture, Cryst) and may
depend on attacker-chosen multiplicities; no untrusted-sort identifier
is ever exposed to $P$ by any reply or view
(Rules R6, R7, R10(b)). An endorsed-sort
identifier never resolves to a $\mathsf{U}$ -block and an untrusted-sort
identifier never resolves to an $\mathbb{E}$ -block, so the sorts separate the
tiers syntactically. Like handle identifiers, block identifiers of
either sort never encode, count, or depend on store contents beyond
this allocation order.

Commentary
 (non-normative; role of the identifier discipline). Clause (g) closes the identifier channel that in-place endorsement
would open: identifiers visible to $P$ (those of $\mathcal{M}_{\mathbb{E}}$ ) depend
only on coupled events, while every attacker-dependent multiplicity
can influence only untrusted-sort identifiers, which no reply and no
view exposes. It is consumed by Theorem S3.25 (Step 4) and
is machine-checked as the two-sorted id space of the mechanization
(§S9).

Rule R2 (Atomic gating; durable per-value endorsement).

When confirmations gate a tool call, the reviews of all gating
positions, the writing of the accepted positions’ fresh endorsed
copies, and the invocation (or its abort) form one atomic interpreter
step: no step may interleave between display, decision, endorsement,
and flow. The bytes that flow at an executed call are the displayed
bytes, bound to that call instance. If any gating position is rejected
or null, the invocation aborts entire: no argument flows; the accepted
positions’ endorsed copies are nevertheless written, their accepting
decisions being recorded in the transcript like any confirmation. When
a batch confirmation gates a $\mathsf{map}$ (Rule R9),
the aggregated accepting decision and the batch of element invocations
form one atomic step, and the bytes that flow at each element call are
exactly the element bytes displayed for that call; accepted gating
non- $\diamond$ arguments are endorsed durably exactly as at a direct
call, whatever the element pages’ outcome.

Commentary
 (non-normative; severability and durable endorsement). Soundness of durable endorsement on an aborting transaction is the
severability clause of A4: intent is per displayed value,
severally. A commit-on-success variant — discarding accepted
positions’ copies on abort — would be safety-monotone but
sacrifices the once-per-pair cap (INV-3): a
transaction that accepts one pair and aborts on a later position
leaves no durable copy, so re-presentations of the same pair re-force
it without bound. Durability is adopted precisely because the cap is
load-bearing (Theorem S6.2).

Rule R3 (Ingress: all tool outputs captured).

Tools partition into $\mathsf{Tools}_{\mathrm{sens}}$ (sensitive, effectful) and $\mathsf{Tools}_{\mathrm{data}}$ (data
readers); the partition and the canonical tool identifiers are fixed
deployment configuration, per capability, inside the TCB. The
interpreter captures every tool output, including the status and
error responses of sensitive calls, to $\mathcal{M}_{\mathsf{U}}$ with label $\mathsf{U}$ , and
returns to $P$ only a single constant acknowledgement token per
invocation: never the bytes, never a success bit, never a count, size,
or identifier derived from captured content.

Rule R4 (Whitelist mediation at every tool boundary).

(a) 

A value $a$ passed to a tool in $\mathsf{Tools}_{\mathrm{sens}}$ flows without
confirmation iff $a$ consists of readable $\mathbb{E}$ -level bytes and
the interpreter verifies that $a$ byte-equals a stored $\mathsf{T}$ -block, or
byte-equals some $\mathsf{C}/\mathsf{CC}$ -block $b$ with
 $\mathsf{tool}\in\mathsf{scope}(b)$ (for $\mathsf{CC}$ , the matched block is the live
shared-tier entry, checked valid at call time; §S4.1). ( $\mathsf{PC}$ blocks have empty scope and never
pass this test.)

(b) 

A value passed to a tool in $\mathsf{Tools}_{\mathrm{data}}$ flows without confirmation
iff it consists of readable $\mathbb{E}$ -level bytes.

(c) 

A handle argument never flows silently to any tool: it always
triggers $\mathsf{confirm}(a,\mathsf{tool})$ , at data tools exactly as at
sensitive ones, regardless of whether its resolved bytes coincide with
stored endorsed content. There are no per-tool exceptions.

(d) 

The fast-path check is membership in $\mathcal{M}_{\mathbb{E}}$ plus a scope
test, not taint-tracking of $P$ ’s code.

Rule R5 (Readable local endorsement).

Every accepting confirmation with target
 $t\in\mathsf{Tools}_{\mathrm{sens}}\cup\mathsf{Tools}_{\mathrm{data}}\cup\{\mathrm{reasoning}\}$ writes a fresh block
 $\operatorname{newC}(v,t)$ (Options O1–O2:
 $\operatorname{newCache}(v,t)$ ) carrying exactly the displayed bytes,
labelled $\mathsf{C}$ (or, under community promotion, the shared tier carries
 $\mathsf{CC}$ status; Appendix S4), at the next endorsed-sort
identifier (Rule R1(g)); its bytes are readable by $P$ via
 $\mathsf{recall}$ , the identifier being computable by $P$ from its own
coupled event history. The originating untrusted block, where one
exists, is unchanged and remains untrusted. Before confirmation, a
crystallized summary is $\mathsf{U}$ -labelled, lives reader/interpreter-side,
