contain $(1+\eta)N/p_{b}$ demanders whenever
 $\bar{\gamma}\,n_{v}+s_{0}\geq(1+\eta)N/p_{b}$ , i.e. whenever
 $n_{v}\geq\big((1+\eta)N/p_{b}-s_{0}\big)/\bar{\gamma}$ ; $D_{0}(M)$ may therefore
be taken of order $N(M)/\bar{\gamma}(M)$ , and the product condition’s Heaps form
 $D_{0}(M)=o(M^{1-\beta})$ (Theorem S6.9(iii)) becomes
 $N(M)/\bar{\gamma}(M)=o(M^{1-\beta})$ , satisfied by every corridor schedule
(§S6.5).

Commentary
 (non-normative; scoping, representation dependence, and validation
of A11). Benign scoping is deliberate: poisoned items are excluded;
their promotion probability is bounded above by
Theorem S5.13, and their residual demand is charged at the
single-user cap by design, each such confirmation being a personal
gate inspection of an unpromoted or flagged item, exactly the
inspection the design intends. The operational (no-row) form is
load-bearing: an earlier draft keyed coverage to certificate
existence, under which a handle-expressed use of a live- $\mathsf{CC}$ pair
counted as covered while its gate — required under
Rule R4(c) — was charged to no row and to no
residual; the operational form charges every required confirmation
somewhere by construction (Lemma S6.7, charging
convention), and names exactly the demand the amortisation story must
cover. Representation dependence is the disclosed price:
community certification amortises the flows that can take the fast
path — literal-expressed reuse of promoted bytes, in particular
bytes obtained through trusted retrieval (Retrieve returns
live entries as readable $\mathbb{E}$ -bytes, Definition S4.1,
Rule R14(ii), which then byte-match the live entry,
Rule R4(a)) — and never the handle-expressed
arrivals, which gate by design (S18);
 $\varepsilon(M)$ must absorb the handle-arrival fraction of certified
demand, so the vanishing of the residual is a joint property of
publication timing, promotion speed, and arrival
representation. Failure is graceful and item-local: residual demand
is priced at the cap (Theorem S6.2(i)), and the epoch’s
shared required load never exceeds the standalone mass
 $d_{\mathrm{tool}}$ in any case (Theorem S6.2(ii)).
Incentive sensitivity is the disclosed residual S16.
Under permanent freshness the condition weakens in one direction and
is explicitly priced in the other: banked credits accumulate across
epoch boundaries toward the next entry, so qualifying demand need not
concentrate within one epoch; conversely, endorsers consumed by a
failed entry never renew, which the identity-disjoint counting
already prices (Lemma S5.3). Validation: log each
item’s realized residual fraction $U_{v,t}/n_{v,t}$ and the
fast-path-vs-gate split of live- $\mathsf{CC}$ demand (the handle-arrival
fraction), stratified on the most-demanded items, together with
per-item time-to-promotion against accumulated demand and the
per-epoch demanded-but-unpublished mass (N17).

Assumption A12 (Bounded tool-spread).

Each shareable item is demanded at no more than $c_{\mathrm{tool}}=\mathcal{O}(1)$ 
distinct tools over the epoch: at most $c_{\mathrm{tool}}$ tools have
 $n_{v,t}\geq 1$ , and hence $\sum_{t}n_{v,t}\leq c_{\mathrm{tool}}\,n_{v}$ 
(latent reference-model counts, §S6.1; the
realized counts of Definition S4.4 inherit the bound
pathwise: $n^{\mathrm{log}}_{v,t}\leq n_{v,t}$ places the realized
tool support inside the latent tool support, so at most $c_{\mathrm{tool}}$ 
tools have $n^{\mathrm{log}}_{v,t}\geq 1$ , and every realized
pair-demander is a realized item-demander, so
 $n^{\mathrm{log}}_{v,t}\leq n^{\mathrm{log}}_{v}$ 
(Definition S4.4); hence
 $\sum_{t}n^{\mathrm{log}}_{v,t}\leq c_{\mathrm{tool}}\,n^{\mathrm{log}}_{v}$ 
pathwise). In particular each user demands at
most $c_{\mathrm{tool}}$ pairs per item, and $d_{\mathrm{tool}}\leq c_{\mathrm{tool}}\,d$ 
(the $\leq c_{\mathrm{tool}}$ demanded targets per item include
 $\mathsf{reasoning}$ where demanded; constants unchanged).

Commentary
 (non-normative; reading and validation of A12). The bound formalises the observation that a published item typically
addresses the scenario its owner used it in: endorsement, promotion,
and scope are all per (value, tool); it licenses the per-tool split
in Theorem S6.9(iii). Validation: per-item distinct-tool
counts (N17). The byte-first density of a
demanded tool’s stream — the fraction of its demanders whose first
decision on the bytes occurs at that tool — is deliberately
not part of this assumption: no proof consumes it, and its
role is heuristic support for the promotion-coverage clause of
A11 (see the heuristic-sufficiency paragraph there).

Assumption A13 (Bounded per-user demand mass).

Along the population scaling, the single-epoch per-user demand mass
is uniformly bounded: $\sup_{M}d(M)<\infty$ , where
 $d(M)=\sum_{v}p_{v}=M^{-1}\sum_{u}\sum_{v}\mathbb{P}[\text{$u$ demands $v$}]$ is
the population-average number of distinct shareable items one user
demands per epoch (§S6.1); hence
 $\sup_{M}d_{\mathrm{tool}}(M)<\infty$ under A12,
and $d_{\mathrm{good}}\leq d$ throughout. Where A13 is
not adopted, every vanishing ( $\to 0$ ) budget conclusion below holds
verbatim with its rate condition read against the mass:
 $\bar{\gamma}(M)\,(1+\kappa(M))\,d_{\mathrm{good}}(M)\to 0$ in
Theorem S6.9(i)–(ii) and Theorem S6.13(iii),
and $\varepsilon(M)\,d_{\mathrm{good}}(M)\to 0$ in
Theorem S6.9(iii) (whose $D_{0}(M)V(M)/M$ term is
independent of the per-user mass and unchanged). The
relative bounds (cost at most the front-loading multiple
 $B_{\mathrm{fl}}\,\bar{\gamma}$ of the standalone mass, Theorem S6.9(i),
first clause) and the cap (Theorem S6.2) consume no
bound on $d$ in any case.

Commentary
 (non-normative; reading and validation of A13). The assumption is load-bearing and deliberately explicit: without
it, the vanishing conclusions would fail — a population-indexed
demand law with $d(M)\to\infty$ (each user demanding ever more
distinct shared items as the community grows) satisfies every
remaining hypothesis while the front-loading term
 $B_{\mathrm{fl}}\,\bar{\gamma}\,d$ grows — and pointwise finiteness of $d$ at each
 $M$ does not supply the uniform bound the limits consume. It is an
individual-usage property, not a population property: under any
fixed per-user demand law the masses are constants in $M$ and
A13 is automatic (the Zipf law of §S8.5
has $d=\sum_{r}p_{r}=1$ identically), and the Heaps form is standardly
read over a corpus accumulated from bounded individual streams
Lü et al. (2010), so the saturation hypothesis already carries
this reading. The owner-coupling convention cannot violate it:
coupled indicators number at most $\rho_{\mathrm{sub}}$ per user per epoch
(Rule R12). The disclosed failure mode is demand growth
induced by the shared tier itself — a richer library drawing each
user to more shared items per epoch — a network effect of the
same character as the incentive feedback of S16;
violation is graceful: the relative bounds and the pathwise
cap (Theorem S6.2(i)) are unaffected, and the general-form rate conditions of
A13 price the growth explicitly. Validation: log
per-user distinct shareable-item demand per epoch across deployment
growth (N17).

## S6.2 The single-user first-authorization cap

The cap’s core is a counting identity. Its pathwise clause,
Theorem S6.2(i), rests on the harness rules and
INV-3 alone: no ledger row, no behavioural parameter, no
demand model, and no assumption of this appendix. Its expected
clauses restate that identity against the reference demand model’s
masses (§S6.1), still using no assumption; the
single assumption-bearing statement in this subsection is the mass
comparison of Corollary S6.3
(A12). The cap is therefore stated first. Its
dependency chain is Lemma S6.1 $\to$ 
INV-3 $\to$ Theorem S6.2 $\to$ 
Lemma S6.7, strictly in that order.

Lemma S6.1 (Local scope persistence).

Fix a user $u$ , a value $v$ , and a tool $t$ , and suppose $u$ ’s own
accepting review of $(v,t)$ has occurred at any gate transaction —
including a transaction another of whose reviews aborted the enclosing
invocation (Rule R2: endorsement is durable). Then in
every later configuration, unless $u$ ’s own agent has evicted the
block (N13), $u$ ’s store contains a $\mathsf{C}$ -block with bytes $v$ and
 $t\in\mathsf{scope}$ , and every use of $v$ at $t$ by $u$ expressed as a
readable literal takes the fast path of Rule R4(a); a
use expressed through a handle gates (Rule R4(c)) as a
covered gate, protocol-inert and redundant (Rule R13(i),
§S6.1). In particular no later gate event of $u$ for
 $(v,t)$ is required. (Option O3 is requirements-level and absent from the
formal semantics; a conforming instantiation would leave the block
in place and read the authorization and coverage tests over
currently authorizing witnesses, O3(a),(c)–(d).) For the pseudo-target: once $u$ holds any local readable
 $\mathbb{E}$ -level copy of the bytes (via Recall-E,
Definition S2.6), a later $\mathsf{confirm}$ on those bytes at
 $\mathsf{reasoning}$ is a covered gate by the reasoning-coverage
clause of Rule R13(i), protocol-inert and redundant; hence
no later gate event of $u$ for $(v,\mathsf{reasoning})$ is required.

Proof.

By Rule R2 and Definition S2.5, every
accepting review of $(v,t)$ writes a fresh $\mathsf{C}$ -block with bytes $v$ 
and $t\in\mathsf{scope}$ at the close of its atomic transaction, on the
executing and the aborting branch alike (Conf-Acc,
Tool-Gate-Acc, Tool-Gate-Rej;
Figures S2–S3, convention (vii)). By
A1–A2 blocks are immutable once written, so
neither this block’s label nor its scope ever changes; local stores
mutate only through owner-invoked $\Sigma$ -operations and owner
confirmations, and no protocol event initiated by any other identity
alters or removes any block in $u$ ’s local store
(Lemma S5.8). The only removal path is first-party eviction (N13). Rule R4(a) then byte-matches
 $v$ against the stored block with $t\in\mathsf{scope}$ . For the pseudo-target: any local $\mathbb{E}$ -level copy of the bytes is
readable (Recall-E, Definition S2.6) and
persists by A1–A2 and
Lemma S5.8, the first-party eviction exception aside;
the reasoning-coverage clause of Rule R13(i) then classifies
every later reasoning-target gate on those bytes as covered. Its
owner-side test reads exactly the readability fact
Rule R5 establishes: readability is the entirety of
what an accepting reasoning confirmation buys, so the clause covers
precisely the gates whose acceptance would change nothing locally.
∎

Theorem S6.2 (Single-user first-authorization cap).

The cap counts required user approvals — first
authorizations of reusable (value, tool) pairs — and nothing else:
not rejecting decisions, abandoned or repeated dialogs, covered-gate
(redundant) acceptances, batch-page attention, or per-task
confirmations (classes: §S6.1; effort account:
N18). The superscript $\mathrm{forced}$ below denotes
this required-approval count.
Suppose the owner’s agent does not evict the relevant local blocks
(the disclosed first-party exception of INV-3);
Option O3 is requirements-level and absent from the
formal semantics (O3), so no lease expiry exists in the
proved system.

(i) 

(Pathwise lifetime invariant.) Pathwise and independent of every federated and demand-model assumption, subject to the stated first-party persistence exceptions: under any
dispute, rejection, revocation, or availability campaign whatsoever,
no user is required to confirm any given (value, tool) pair more than
once over the deployment’s lifetime:
 $C^{\mathrm{forced}}_{u,v,t}\leq 1$ pathwise, counting required
pair-approvals (Definition S2.5, counting unit).
This is INV-3, established by
Lemma S6.1. Rejecting decisions, abandoned dialogs,
repeated within-transaction reviews, redundant confirmations
(§S6.1), and inspection time are a distinct
resource, disclosed and partially bounded in N18, and are
not bounded by this clause.

(ii) 

(Expected cap, single epoch, reference model.) In the
independent reference model (§S6.1), the two
components are bounded on their own horizons:
 $\mathbb{E}[C^{\mathrm{forced},\infty}_{\mathrm{personal}}]\leq d_{\mathrm{priv}}$ 
(lifetime) and
 $\mathbb{E}[\bar{C}^{\mathrm{forced},(e)}_{\mathrm{share}}]\leq d_{\mathrm{tool}}$ 
(per epoch $e$ ); the componentwise sum $d_{\mathrm{priv}}+d_{\mathrm{tool}}$ is
a valid upper bound for their sum on any single epoch read jointly
with the lifetime personal term, not a homogeneous single-horizon
cost. The bound
 $d_{\mathrm{tool}}$ is exactly the expected shareable cost a single
user incurs in isolation, where every gate necessarily fires: no user
is ever pushed above the single-user cost. The comparison to the
item-level mass, $d_{\mathrm{tool}}\leq c_{\mathrm{tool}}\,d$ , is
Corollary S6.3 and is this theorem’s only
assumption-bearing companion.

(iii) 

(Expected cap, lifetime.) Over the deployment’s
lifetime, expected required shareable cost is at most
 $d^{\infty}_{\mathrm{tool}}=\sum_{v,t}p^{\infty}_{v,t}$ ; the
single-epoch mass bounds the lifetime cost only when it is itself read
as the lifetime mass (§S6.1).

(iv) 

(Monotonicity.) At a fixed realised workload with no
disputes, adding users never increases any individual’s required count:
promotions only advance, and a promotion of $(v,t)$ removes the gate
for every later demander of that pair (Rule R4). With
disputes, an added identity can suspend amortisation, forcing later
demanders to confirm personally; the pathwise invariant (i) still caps
every user at once per pair.

Proof.

(i) By Lemma S6.1: $u$ ’s first accepting review of
 $(v,t)$ writes the covering local copy durably, whatever the enclosing
transaction’s outcome, so no later gate event of $u$ for $(v,t)$ is
required (a literal-expressed use fast-paths; a handle-expressed use is
a covered gate, redundant by definition); within that first
transaction the pair is counted once (Definition S2.5);
declining voluntary invitations costs nothing
(Definition S4.6). (ii) By (i), each required confirmation
of $(v,t)$ belongs to a distinct demander of the pair (realized, hence latent, §S8.1), so the
population’s required total for $(v,t)$ is at most $n_{v,t}$ , with
 $\mathbb{E}[n_{v,t}]=Mp_{v,t}$ in the reference model; summing over pairs and dividing by $M$ gives
 $d_{\mathrm{tool}}$ ; personal pairs add $d_{\mathrm{priv}}$ , once each (INV-3). (iii) The
pathwise clause (i) is lifetime; taking expectations over lifetime
pair-demand indicators gives $d^{\infty}_{\mathrm{tool}}$ . (iv)
Immediate from Rule R4 and (i).
∎

Corollary S6.3 (The cap under bounded tool-spread).

Under A12, $d_{\mathrm{tool}}\leq c_{\mathrm{tool}}\,d$ ; hence
the shareable cap of Theorem S6.2(ii) is at most
 $c_{\mathrm{tool}}\,d$ per epoch (componentwise sum $d_{\mathrm{priv}}+c_{\mathrm{tool}}\,d$ , horizons per Theorem S6.2(ii)). The lifetime
analogue, $d^{\infty}_{\mathrm{tool}}\leq c_{\mathrm{tool}}\,d^{\infty}$ with
 $d^{\infty}:=\sum_{v}p^{\infty}_{v}$ , holds when A12
is read over lifetime demand (at most $c_{\mathrm{tool}}$ distinct tools per
item ever), a strictly stronger reading than the per-epoch form and
asserted only here. This corollary restates the cap against the
coarser item-level mass; no other result consumes it.

Proof.

A user demanding the pair $(v,t)$ demands the item $v$ 
(Definition S4.4), and under A12 at
most $c_{\mathrm{tool}}$ tools per item carry demand, so
 $\sum_{t}p_{v,t}\leq c_{\mathrm{tool}}\,p_{v}$ per item; sum over items. The lifetime
form is identical under the lifetime reading.
∎

Remark S6.4 (Voluntary effort and the cap).

The cap is stated over required confirmations. Voluntary community
effort (a repeat re-inspection after a dispute or re-entry
notification) is declinable at zero personal cost, carries no
promotion weight (INV-1), and is counted in the budget
(Lemma S6.8), never in the cap.

Corollary S6.5 (The cap under revocation).

Post-promotion suspensions and re-verification (N16) do not
multiply required cost: a user who relied on ungated $\mathsf{CC}$ status at $t$ 
and loses it on demotion or suspension owes at most their first-ever confirmation of
 $(v,t)$ , already counted inside $d^{\infty}_{\mathrm{tool}}$ , after
which their local $\mathsf{C}$ copy covers all reuse at $t$ for the deployment’s
lifetime (Lemma S6.1). Hence expected total required
cost is at most $d_{\mathrm{priv}}+d^{\infty}_{\mathrm{tool}}$ , lifetime,
independent of the number of epochs. A multiplicative factor $E_{\mathrm{lease}}$ would arise only under a
conforming instantiation of the requirements-level
Option O3 — an extension-level claim, stated and to be
proved with that extension (O3(e)), outside this
corollary.

Proof.

Immediate from Theorem S6.2(i),(iii): required confirmations
are once per (user, value, tool) over the lifetime; demotion,
suspension, and re-verification never touch local $\mathsf{C}$ copies
(Lemma S5.8). Option O3 is absent from the formal semantics
(O3).
∎

Corollary S6.6 (Budget under Options O1–O2).

Option-induced cache confirmations
(Options O1–O2) belong to no pipeline ledger row
and to no residual (Lemma S6.7, exclusions): they are
accept-inert for the shared tier
(Definition S4.6(iii)) and are accounted in two
classes. (Per-task.) O2’s confirmations of freshly
composed data-tool arguments lie in the per-task stream, outside the
reusable-memory accounting entirely, by the scope hypothesis of
Proposition S3.28. (Floor-charged.) All remaining
option-induced cache confirmations — O1’s first-use
sensitive-sink confirmations of $\mathsf{T}$ -values and O2’s
confirmations of endorsed-value reuse at data tools — are required
pair-confirmations of the confirming user, charged to the floor
component: each is at most one per (user, value, sensitive sink)
resp. (user, value, data tool) (scope-caching,
Lemma S6.1, INV-3), an $\mathcal{O}(1)$ 
enlargement of the per-item pair count under the sink-spread and
data-tool-spread hypotheses of Proposition S3.28 —
folded exactly as A12 folds tool pairs. The
exhaustiveness of the charging convention (Lemma S6.7)
is unaffected: option-induced confirmations are charged to the
per-task stream or the floor, never to any pipeline row or residual.

Proof.

Immediate from Proposition S3.28(iii),
Definition S4.6(iii), and INV-3: the fresh
cache copy scope-caches (Lemma S6.1), so each
(user, value, target) triple is required at most once; the spread
hypotheses bound the targets per value by $\mathcal{O}(1)$ ; and the
freshly-composed class is per-task by hypothesis.
∎

## S6.3 The per-item confirmation ledger

Lemma S6.7 (Per-item confirmation ledger, $\mathcal{O}$ -form).

Fix an item $v$ and an epoch; adopt the default bars of
Rule R13(iv), A9, A10,
A12, and, for the bucket rows, that each
honest inspection, fresh or repeat, accepts a benign item with
conditional probability at least $p_{b}$ given all prior protocol events
(for a repeat inspection of bytes the inspector previously accepted, the
bound is if anything the more plausible).
Adopt also the standing exception of Theorem S6.2 (no
owner eviction of the relevant local blocks; Option O3 is
requirements-level and absent from the formal semantics,
O3).
Option-induced cache confirmations (Options O1–O2)
are excluded throughout — from $C^{\mathrm{pipe}}_{v}$ , from the
residual of A11, and from the pathwise count $C_{v}$ of
clause (iv): they are charged to the floor component, with the
 $\mathcal{O}(1)$ spread factors of Corollary S6.6.
Let $E_{\star}:=E_{\max}+1$ .
Presence taxonomy. Within the epoch, the item’s shared-tier
presence partitions into four classes of pieces: entry windows
(at most $E_{\max}$ , by the per-item entry cap of
Rule R14(i), counting promoted, evicted, and unpromoted
entries alike; windows are epoch-contained, convention (C4));
freeze/suspension intervals (the current-epoch disputes and
fast-track reviews, plus at most one carry-in instance — the
byte-level freeze is exclusive, Rule R13(ii); a carried-in
instance is counted toward Rule R14(i) in its opening
epoch, convention (C4), but its current-epoch demanders are charged
here, via the carry-in term of $\bar{D}_{v}$ , and it issues no new
prompts, Rule R13(vi)); candidate-dormancy intervals
(no open window, no live promotion at any tool, unfrozen); and
stable promoted intervals (a live, unsuspended community entry
at some tool, no open window, unfrozen). Inter-window spans — each
entry is followed by at most one, ending at the next entry,
retirement, or the epoch boundary, so at most $E_{\star}$ begin in or
carry into the epoch — have promotion status fixed at the preceding
window’s close, so each span is candidate-dormant or stable-promoted
throughout, with its freeze/suspension pieces carved out;
A10’s dormant-interval clause is applied per
candidate-dormancy span, freeze-time demanders within a span being
charged to the freeze bucket. The first three classes are the
ledger-charged pieces; stable promoted intervals are
deliberately not bucketed: a matching literal fast-paths there
at the promoted tool (Rule R4(a)) at zero cost, while
the gates that do fire there — handle-expressed uses of the
certified pair (Rule R4(c)) and uses at unpromoted
tools — are charged to the ledger residual below, bounded by
Theorem S6.9(iii) under A11. (A stable promoted
interval is “window closed, not yet resubmitted or retired” in the
sense of Definition S4.6(b): first decisions logged
there bank toward the item’s next entry exactly as in candidate
dormancy; the taxonomy separates the classes for cost attribution
only and changes no protocol rule.) We use the common conservative
bound $E_{\star}$ for the window- and dormancy-bucket piece counts.
(The attempt structure of Definition S5.1 partitions the
same presence; the ledger’s buckets and bounds are unchanged by it.)
Write

 $\bar{D}_{v}\ :=\ 2(1-p_{b})\,E_{\star}\,c_{\mathrm{tool}}\big(\bar{\gamma}\,n_{v}+s_{0}\big)\;+\;(1-p_{b})\,E_{\max}c_{\mathrm{tool}}N^{+}\;+\;c_{\mathrm{acc}}f\;+\;2E_{\max}\;+\;1\,,$ 

the final $+1$ is the carry-in instance; the invited-reconsideration
term keeps $E_{\max}$ because invitations are epoch-scoped
(Definition S4.6). $\bar{D}_{v}$ is a function of the
realised demand $n_{v}$ : the bucket rows bound conditional expectations
given the demand realisation, and the split in (iii) follows by taking
the expectation over demand last, licensed by the demand construction
of §S8.1: the behavioural bounds are asserted uniformly
over demand realisations, so Lemma S8.2 applies under
the demand-conditional measure. For poisoned items no $p_{b}$ bound is
asserted: their entries are counted by Corollary S5.27
and their residual demand is charged at the single-user cap
(Theorem S6.2).

Pipeline component and ledger residual. The rows cover the
pipeline component $C^{\mathrm{pipe}}_{v}$ : every confirmation of
a pair of $v$ charged to a ledger row under the convention below —
credited weight-bearing endorsements, bucketed required confirmations
(window, candidate-dormancy, and freeze/suspension time, carry-in
included), and all invited-repeat, prompt, and publish confirmations
of $v$ . Required confirmations charged to no row are the
ledger residual: those of demanders whose epoch-first
item-demand falls in a stable promoted interval (in particular
handle-expressed uses of a live- $\mathsf{CC}$ pair, which gate under
Rule R4(c) despite the certificate) or outside every
shared-tier presence (before first publication, after retirement, or
after demotion). The residual is bounded by
Theorem S6.9(iii) under A11; any overlap between
the two accountings only over-counts.

Charging convention (retrospective). At the close of the
logging epoch every confirmation of $v$ — a durable accepting
pair-confirmation, counted once per pair per transaction
(Definition S2.5) — is assigned to exactly one row or
to the ledger residual; the ledger is a hindsight accounting device,
like A10, never protocol state. The ledger rows charge protocol-bearing decisions and invited
and publish events (Definition S4.6); the
residual is defined over all required confirmations of the bytes,
protocol-bearing or not — in particular outside-presence
confirmations, which are unlogged as candidate decisions — so that
every required confirmation in this lemma’s accounting universe (option-induced cache confirmations excluded, above) is charged somewhere, to a row or to the
residual, by construction. (Definition S4.4’s retirement
clause governs demand counting, not the confirmation universe
of this convention.) Non-covered reasoning-target confirmations
satisfying Definition S4.6 are protocol-bearing
(§S4.1) and are charged to the pair
 $(v,\mathsf{reasoning})$ exactly as tool pairs are; covered reasoning
gates are inert (Rule R13(i)) and excluded like any
redundant confirmation. Credited
weight-bearing endorsements (in-entry, banked-and-credited within the
epoch, or vindication-credited; required or invited alike) are assigned
to the weight row — including a banked first decision taken in a
stable promoted interval, so a residual-class demander’s credited
acceptance is weight-row-charged, only shrinking the residual;
uncredited invited confirmations to their invitation row
(re-entry or prompt; invitations are epoch-scoped, so no invitation
row carries over); publish confirmations to the publish row;
every other required confirmation to the ledger-charged piece
(window, candidate-dormancy, or freeze/suspension — current-epoch
instances and the at most one carry-in instance) containing its
demander’s first demand of the item in the epoch, at most
 $c_{\mathrm{tool}}$ confirmations per demander (one per pair, INV-3,
A12), when that first item-demand falls in a
ledger-charged piece, and to the ledger residual otherwise
(first item-demand in a stable promoted interval or outside every
presence). Cross-epoch crediting of an already-logged event
(Definition S4.6(b), Rule R13(iv)) relabels a
prior epoch’s event and adds nothing to any epoch. The four presence
classes partition the item’s shared-tier presence within the epoch,
so every demander’s epoch-first item-demand falls in exactly one
piece or outside every presence; the assignment classes are disjoint
and exhaustive over required, voluntary, and publish confirmations
(redundant confirmations excluded throughout,
§S6.1) — in particular every required
confirmation is charged somewhere, to a bucket row or to the
residual, by construction — and each row bound is an upper bound
for its assigned class. Then:

(i) 

(Once, ever, per pair.) No user’s required count for any
pair $(v,t)$ exceeds one over the deployment’s lifetime
(INV-3; exceptions per Theorem S6.2), a fortiori
one per epoch; required confirmations of $(v,t)$ belong to distinct
demanders of the pair.

(ii) 

(Row bounds.) Every pipeline-component confirmation is
assigned to exactly one row of Table S2 under the
retrospective charging convention above, with the stated per-epoch
bounds; cross-epoch crediting of an already-logged event
(Rule R13(iv), Definition S4.6(b)) is a
relabel adding no event to any epoch.

(iii) 

(Split.) Summing rows, there are $A(M)$ and $B_{\mathrm{fl}}$ 
depending only on protocol constants, with

 $A(M)=\mathcal{O}\!\big(E_{\max}\,c_{\mathrm{tool}}^{2}\,(N+c_{\mathrm{acc}}f)(1+\kappa)\big)\ \text{(via $N^{+}<N+D_{\mathrm{pool}}\leq 2N$)},$ 

 $B_{\mathrm{fl}}=\mathcal{O}\!\big(E_{\max}\,c_{\mathrm{tool}}^{2}(1+(1-p_{b})\,\kappa)\big),$ 

such that
 $\mathbb{E}[C^{\mathrm{pipe}}_{v}]\ \leq\ A(M)\,\mathbb{P}[n_{v}\geq 1]\;+\;B_{\mathrm{fl}}\,\bar{\gamma}\,\mathbb{E}[n_{v}]$ ; the pipeline required total obeys the same bound. Explicit
constants: Lemma S8.18 (§S8.3).

(iv) 

(Pathwise, all baseline-counted confirmations.) Let $C_{v}$ count all
required, voluntary, and publish confirmations of $v$ ’s exact bytes in
the epoch, wherever they fall — ledger-charged and residual alike;
redundant confirmations and option-induced cache confirmations
excluded (§S6.1;
Corollary S6.6). In the single-epoch model, with
 $K:=E_{\max}(c_{\mathrm{tool}}N^{+}+c_{\mathrm{p}}k+1)$ ,

 $C_{v}\ \leq\ c_{\mathrm{tool}}\,n_{v}+K\,\mathbf{1}\{n_{v}\geq 1\}:$ 

every required confirmation of a pair of $v$ belongs to a demander of
the pair — its confirmer’s own attempted use fires the gate, hence
realizes the latent indicator (required-approval clause,
§S6.1), inside or outside any shared-tier presence
— at most $c_{\mathrm{tool}}$ pairs per demander (one per pair,
INV-3, A12); voluntary
confirmations exist only at invitation surfaces, by definition
(§S6.1), and invitations per epoch are
deterministically capped at $E_{\max}c_{\mathrm{tool}}N^{+}$ re-entry notifications
plus $E_{\max}c_{\mathrm{p}}k$ suspension prompts, independently of
current-epoch demand — notified endorsers and prompted adopters
need not be current-epoch demanders — with joint deduplication
(Definition S4.6, Rule R13(vi)); the owner
contributes at most $E_{\max}$ publish confirmations
(one accepting publish per opened entry: a cap-exhausted submission is ineligible and records no accepting confirmation; Rules R12, R14(i)). Every term vanishes on $\{n_{v}=0\}$ , by the event-based vanishing clause of the owner-coupling convention and the
required-approval clause (§S6.1): no latent demander
means no attempted use, hence no required confirmation, and no entry, review, invitation, or publish confirmation occurs in the epoch, whatever presence is carried in.

Table S2: The per-item confirmation ledger. “Req.” and “vol.” denote required and
voluntary confirmations (§S6.1). Bucket rows use the
conservative $E_{\star}=E_{\max}+1$ (Lemma S6.7); windows need
only $E_{\max}$ . Stable promoted intervals are charged to no row; their
required confirmations form the ledger residual, bounded by
Theorem S6.9(iii) under A11.

Proof.

(i) After a user’s own accepting confirmation of $(v,t)$ , the
local in-scope $\mathsf{C}$ copy covers all their reuse at $t$ for the
deployment’s lifetime (Lemma S6.1) and no third
party can disturb it (Lemma S5.8); this is
INV-3.

(ii) Weight-bearing: within an entry, credited
endorsements per tool number at most $N^{+}$ by the credit caps
(Definition S4.6(f)), banked credits filling slots the
same caps govern; each credited endorsement is a distinct demander’s
first protocol-bearing decision, so successive entries’ credited sets
are disjoint (Lemma S5.3), entries per epoch at most
 $E_{\max}$ (the entry cap of Rule R14(i), republications and post-eviction republications included).
Buckets: within the epoch, the item’s shared-tier presence
partitions into the four classes of the presence taxonomy: at most
 $E_{\max}$ windows, at most $E_{\star}$ candidate-dormancy pieces (one
possibly carried in), the freeze/suspension intervals of the
current-epoch disputes and reviews plus at most one carry-in
instance, and the stable promoted intervals; every demander of $v$ 
has exactly one epoch-first item-demand, in exactly one piece or
outside every presence. A demander whose first item-demand falls in a
window or candidate-dormancy piece has a pair-first-demand there for
at least one of the item’s $\leq c_{\mathrm{tool}}$ pairs, so A10’s
demand clause (scoped to exactly these two piece types) bounds
window/candidate-dormancy occupancy by $c_{\mathrm{tool}}(\bar{\gamma}\,n_{v}+s_{0})$ 
per piece; each such demander is charged at most $c_{\mathrm{tool}}$ required
pair-confirmations (once per pair per transaction,
Definition S2.5; once ever required, INV-3),
giving the two bucket rows with the factor $E_{\star}$ .
Lemma S5.6 bounds each freeze or review’s arriving demanders
by $\kappa$ in expectation while unresolved — for the carry-in
instance, counting only its current-epoch arrivals, a sub-count of
the same dominated arrival sequence — each charged at most
 $c_{\mathrm{tool}}$ ; the freeze-bucket bound $c_{\mathrm{tool}}\,\bar{D}_{v}\,\kappa$ follows
by the tower property under the demand-conditional measure
(§S8.1). Demanders whose first item-demand falls in a
stable promoted interval, or outside every presence, are charged to
no bucket: their required confirmations are the ledger residual,
bounded by Theorem S6.9(iii) under A11 (their
credited acceptances, where any, are weight-row events already
counted).
Disputes and reviews ( $\bar{D}_{v}$ ): disputes on $v$ open only at
protocol-bearing dispute-initiating rejections logged while $v$ is
unfrozen and unpromoted at every tool (freeze-time rejections
corroborate, Rule R13(iii); a qualifying rejection on bytes
holding $\mathsf{CC}$ status at any tool is a post-promotion rejection routed
to clause (vi), Rule R13(ii), counted below within the
review cap) — hence only during window or candidate-dormancy pieces
— at most one initiation per identity per item per epoch
(Rule R13(i)). An honest identity’s initiating rejection is the rejecting branch of
its first audit-recorded decision on that pair in the epoch,
at a non-covered gate (cache gates included: both branches are
audit-recorded, §S8.1) with tool
or reasoning target while $v$ is
unfrozen (an earlier audit-recorded accepting decision on the
pair would have created durable coverage, Lemma S6.1, making the gate
covered, hence inert; an earlier rejecting decision — federation-logged in every case,
Definition S4.6(iii) — would make the
present one non-initiating; abandonment records nothing). The trial is indexed at
the pre-outcome header atom of an audit-recorded first pair-decision,
so its occurrence never selects the outcome, and the occurrences are
capped by A10’s first-pair-decision clause: at most
 $\bar{\gamma}\,n_{v}+s_{0}$ per pair per piece, $\leq c_{\mathrm{tool}}$ pairs, over at
most $E_{\star}$ window pieces and $E_{\star}$ candidate-dormancy pieces
— conservatively $2E_{\star}c_{\mathrm{tool}}(\bar{\gamma}\,n_{v}+s_{0})$ candidate
occurrences, each rejecting a benign item with conditional
probability at most $1-p_{b}$ at its header atom (the $p_{b}$ hypothesis
covers every honest inspection, cache gates included;
Lemma S8.1(ii), Lemma S8.2(iii)),
contributing the first term.
Invited reconsiderations are epoch-scoped
(Definition S4.6): at most $E_{\max}c_{\mathrm{tool}}N^{+}$ per
epoch, each rejecting with conditional probability at most $1-p_{b}$ 
(repeat form of the hypothesis), contributing the second term.
Adversarial initiations that take effect are either eventually
charged, at most $c_{\mathrm{acc}}$ per identity per epoch
(Lemma S5.4), $\leq c_{\mathrm{acc}}f$ in total; or terminal (eviction
or expiry), retiring that presence, at most one per presence and
 $\leq E_{\max}$ presences per epoch. Post-promotion reviews —
which absorb every rejection logged during a stable promoted
interval, honest or adversarial — are capped at $E_{\max}$ per item
per epoch (Rule R13(vi)). The carry-in instance adds $1$ .
Summing gives $\bar{D}_{v}$ .
Voluntary and publish rows: Definition S4.6,
Rule R13(vi), Rule R12.

(iii) Summing the row bounds (with $E_{\star}$ in the bucket rows and the carry-in term of $\bar{D}_{v}$ ) and collecting the coefficient of
 $\bar{\gamma}\,n_{v}$ gives the split; every row’s pathwise count vanishes on
 $\{n_{v}=0\}$ (the event-based vanishing clause of the owner-coupling convention, §S6.1: no entries, reviews, invitations, or demanders on $\{n_{v}=0\}$ , whatever presence is carried in), so
each demand-free term is carried with the indicator
 $\mathbf{1}\{n_{v}\geq 1\}$ before taking expectations (the freeze
bucket via Lemma S8.2(iii) applied to the
indicator-stopped counts), multiplying it by $\mathbb{P}[n_{v}\geq 1]$ ; the
explicit algebra, with the stated
 $\mathcal{O}$ -forms verified, is Lemma S8.18. Each required
confirmation belongs to a distinct demander, so the required total obeys
the same bound.

(iv) With $K:=E_{\max}c_{\mathrm{tool}}N^{+}+E_{\max}c_{\mathrm{p}}k+E_{\max}$ :

 $C_{v}\ \leq\ c_{\mathrm{tool}}\,n_{v}+K\,\mathbf{1}\{n_{v}\geq 1\}.$ 

Required: by the required-approval clause (§S6.1) every
user making a required approval of a pair of $v$ is a latent demander of $v$ ,
contributing at most $c_{\mathrm{tool}}$ pair-confirmations, once per pair per
transaction and once ever required (INV-3,
A12); voluntary: confirmations exist only at invitation
surfaces
(§S6.1), which are epoch-scoped and deterministically
capped at $E_{\max}c_{\mathrm{tool}}N^{+}$ re-entry notifications plus $E_{\max}c_{\mathrm{p}}k$ 
suspension prompts, with joint deduplication and boundary expiry
(Definition S4.6, Rule R13(vi)), independently
of current-epoch demand; publish: at most $E_{\max}$ 
(one per opened entry; Rules R12, R14(i)). Every term vanishes
on $\{n_{v}=0\}$ : no latent demander means no attempted use, hence no
required confirmation (required-approval clause); an entry requires a
current-epoch submission (owner demand); a review or dispute opens at
a latent demander’s protocol-bearing decision; invitations require a
current-epoch entry or review, and a carried-in review issues no new
prompts (Rule R13(vi)); a carried-in freeze with no
current-epoch arrivals charges nothing.
∎

## S6.4 Saturation

Lemma S6.8 (Distinct-demand bound, two-term form).

Let $\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{good}}\subseteq\mathcal{V}$ be the benign
(Definition S4.10), assumption-conforming items — those to
which the $p_{b}$ , A10, A9, and
A12 hypotheses of Lemma S6.7 apply — and
write $V_{\mathrm{good}}(M):=\mathbb{E}|\{v\in\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{good}}:n_{v}\geq 1\}|\leq V(M)$ and
 $d_{\mathrm{good}}:=\sum_{v\in\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{good}}}p_{v}\leq d$ . Then
Under A10, A9, A12, and the competence bound of
Lemma S6.7, with $A(M)$ , $B_{\mathrm{fl}}$ , $\kappa$ as there,

 $\mathbb{E}[\bar{C}^{\mathrm{pipe,good}}_{M}]\ \leq\ A(M)\,\frac{V_{\mathrm{good}}(M)}{M}\;+\;B_{\mathrm{fl}}\,\bar{\gamma}\,d_{\mathrm{good}},$ 

where $\bar{C}^{\mathrm{pipe,good}}_{M}$ counts all pipeline-component shareable
confirmations, required and voluntary (Lemma S6.7), over $\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{good}}$ ; the
ledger residual (Lemma S6.7, charging convention) is
bounded separately by Theorem S6.9(iii) and added in
Corollary S6.10. The first term is carried by the number of distinct demanded
items and vanishes under saturation; the second is the
front-loading term: item popularity has cancelled out of it,
leaving only the front-loading multiple $\bar{\gamma}$ against the
standalone single-user shareable mass $d$ . Demand on the complement
is charged at the single-user cap and enters the residual
 $r_{\mathrm{bad}}$ of Corollary S6.10.

Proof.

By Lemma S6.7(iii),
 $\mathbb{E}[C^{\mathrm{pipe}}_{v}]\leq A(M)\,\mathbb{P}[n_{v}\geq 1]+B_{\mathrm{fl}}\,\bar{\gamma}\,\mathbb{E}[n_{v}]$ . Summing
over $v\in\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{good}}$ : $\sum_{v\in\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{good}}}\mathbb{P}[n_{v}\geq 1]=\mathbb{E}|\{v\in\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{good}}:n_{v}\geq 1\}|=V_{\mathrm{good}}(M)$ and
 $\sum_{v\in\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{good}}}\mathbb{E}[n_{v}]=Md_{\mathrm{good}}$ ; divide by $M$ . ( $E_{\max}$ inside $A(M)$ is a
deterministic per-epoch entry bound, Rule R14(i); no expectation over
entry counts is needed.)
∎

Theorem S6.9 (Confirmation cost under saturation).

Under A10, A9, A12, the
competence bound of Lemma S6.7, and — consumed only by
the limit ( $\to 0$ ) clauses below — A13:

(i) 

if $V(M)=o(M)$ and $A(M)\,V(M)=o(M)$ , then
 $\mathbb{E}[\bar{C}^{\mathrm{pipe,good}}_{M}]\leq o(1)+B_{\mathrm{fl}}\,\bar{\gamma}\,d_{\mathrm{good}}$ : the ledger-counted shareable
cost falls to at most the front-loading multiple $B_{\mathrm{fl}}\,\bar{\gamma}$ of the
standalone single-user shareable mass, independently of item popularity; if in
addition $\bar{\gamma}(M)(1+\kappa(M))\to 0$ , then $\mathbb{E}[\bar{C}^{\mathrm{pipe,good}}_{M}]\to 0$ (an upper-bound statement: the displayed bounds vanish);

(ii) 

with $V(M)=\Theta(M^{\beta})$ , $\beta\in(0,1)$ , the protocol
constants fixed, and additionally $f(M)=\mathcal{O}(1)$ and
 $\inf_{M}\rho_{\mathrm{seat}}(M)>0$ — hence $\kappa(M)=\mathcal{O}(1)$ , so
 $A(M)=\mathcal{O}(1)$ and $B_{\mathrm{fl}}(M)=\mathcal{O}(1)$ ; the added hypotheses are not
implied by fixing the protocol constants, $f$ being an adversary
resource and $\rho_{\mathrm{seat}}$ a behavioural parameter — (i)’s hypothesis
holds automatically:
 $\mathbb{E}[\bar{C}^{\mathrm{pipe,good}}_{M}]\leq\mathcal{O}(M^{\beta-1})+B_{\mathrm{fl}}\,\bar{\gamma}\,d_{\mathrm{good}}$ , vanishing whenever
 $\bar{\gamma}(M)\to 0$ (under these hypotheses equivalent to (i)’s condition
 $\bar{\gamma}(M)(1+\kappa(M))\to 0$ ). This is the fixed-adversary,
fixed-constants regime; the growing-adversary regime is the corridor
(§S6.5, Theorem S6.13);

(iii) 

(Ledger residual, benign conforming component.) under
A11 and A12, the expected per-user required
cost charged to no ledger row over the benign,
A11-conforming items — the ledger residual of
Lemma S6.7: required confirmations of demanders whose
epoch-first item-demand falls in a stable promoted interval (in
particular handle-expressed use of a live- $\mathsf{CC}$ pair, which gates
under Rule R4(c)) or outside every shared-tier
presence (before first publication, after retirement, or after
demotion); residual demand on poisoned or
A11-violating pairs enters $r^{(e)}_{\mathrm{bad}}$ 
(Corollary S6.10) instead — is at most
 $c_{\mathrm{tool}}\big(D_{0}(M)\,V(M)/M+\varepsilon(M)\,d_{\mathrm{good}}(M)\big)$ 
( $d_{\mathrm{good}}\leq d$ , $V_{\mathrm{good}}(M)\leq V(M)$ as in
Lemma S6.8). This bound vanishes if, in addition,
 $D_{0}(M)\,V(M)=o(M)$ and $\varepsilon(M)\,d_{\mathrm{good}}(M)\to 0$ ;
under A13 the latter follows from
 $\varepsilon(M)\to 0$ . In particular, under the Heaps form
 $V(M)=\Theta(M^{\beta})$ of clause (ii), $D_{0}(M)=o(M^{1-\beta})$ 
supplies the product condition. The product condition is an
additional rate hypothesis of this vanishing conclusion: neither
A11 nor clause (i)’s hypotheses alone implies it.

Proof.

(i)–(ii) Immediate from Lemma S6.8:
 $A(M)V(M)/M=o(1)$ by hypothesis (under Heaps’ form with the hypotheses of (ii) — protocol constants fixed, $f(M)=\mathcal{O}(1)$ , $\rho_{\mathrm{seat}}$ bounded below — $A(M)=\mathcal{O}(1)$ , $\kappa(M)=\mathcal{O}(1)$ , and $V(M)/M=\mathcal{O}(M^{\beta-1})$ ), and
 $B_{\mathrm{fl}}=\mathcal{O}(E_{\max}c_{\mathrm{tool}}^{2}(1+\kappa))=\mathcal{O}(E_{\max}(1+\kappa))$ ( $c_{\mathrm{tool}}=\mathcal{O}(1)$ absorbed), so the second term is
 $\mathcal{O}(E_{\max}(1+\kappa)\bar{\gamma})\,d_{\mathrm{good}}$ , vanishing whenever
 $\bar{\gamma}(M)(1+\kappa(M))\to 0$ , the mass factor being uniformly bounded
( $d_{\mathrm{good}}\leq d$ , A13) (a sufficient
condition: the displayed bound is an upper bound, and degenerate
parameter regimes, e.g. $p_{b}=1$ , can shrink it further).
(iii) Fix a benign, A11-conforming item $v$ and a target
 $t$ . Every required confirmation of $(v,t)$ belongs to a distinct
demander of the pair (INV-3). Under the charging
convention of Lemma S6.7, each is charged to a bucket
row (its demander’s epoch-first item-demand lying in a
ledger-charged piece), to the weight row (credited), or to the
ledger residual; nothing is uncharged. The residual required
confirmations of $(v,t)$ number exactly $U_{v,t}$ 
(A11), with $U_{v,t}\leq n_{v,t}$ pathwise; conditioning
on $n_{v,t}$ and applying A11 (whose bound holds
trivially on $\{n_{v,t}=0\}$ , where $U_{v,t}=0$ ),

 $\mathbb{E}[U_{v,t}]\ \leq\ D_{0}(M)\,\mathbb{P}[n_{v,t}\geq 1]+\varepsilon(M)\,\mathbb{E}[n_{v,t}].$ 

Now sum over all targets $t$ , using A12
pathwise on each term:

 $\sum_{t}\mathbb{P}[n_{v,t}\geq 1]=\mathbb{E}\big[\#\{t:n_{v,t}\geq 1\}\big]\ \leq\ c_{\mathrm{tool}}\,\mathbb{P}[n_{v}\geq 1],$ 

 $\sum_{t}\mathbb{E}[n_{v,t}]=\mathbb{E}\Big[\sum_{t}n_{v,t}\Big]\ \leq\ c_{\mathrm{tool}}\,\mathbb{E}[n_{v}],$ 

the first because at most $c_{\mathrm{tool}}$ tools carry demand and none does
on $\{n_{v}=0\}$ ( $n_{v,t}\leq n_{v}$ ), so
 $\#\{t:n_{v,t}\geq 1\}\leq c_{\mathrm{tool}}\,\mathbf{1}\{n_{v}\geq 1\}$ pathwise; the
second by $\sum_{t}n_{v,t}\leq c_{\mathrm{tool}}\,n_{v}$ . Summing over the benign,
A11-conforming items
( $\sum_{v\in\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{good}}}\mathbb{P}[n_{v}\geq 1]=V_{\mathrm{good}}(M)\leq V(M)$ ,
 $\sum_{v\in\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{good}}}\mathbb{E}[n_{v}]=M\,d_{\mathrm{good}}(M)$ ,
Lemma S6.8) and dividing by $M$ gives
 $c_{\mathrm{tool}}\big(D_{0}(M)V(M)/M+\varepsilon(M)\,d_{\mathrm{good}}(M)\big)$ ,
which vanishes under the additional rate hypotheses of the statement
( $D_{0}(M)V(M)=o(M)$ ; $\varepsilon(M)d_{\mathrm{good}}(M)\to 0$ ,
supplied under A13 by $\varepsilon(M)\to 0$ ), with
 $c_{\mathrm{tool}}=\mathcal{O}(1)$ ; under the Heaps form of clause (ii),
 $D_{0}(M)=o(M^{1-\beta})$ supplies the product condition. (Beyond
A11, the pair-to-item aggregation consumes only the
bounded tool-spread of A12; the vanishing limit
additionally consumes the stated rate hypotheses and, where
 $\varepsilon(M)\to 0$ is invoked, A13. Demand at
pairs where A11 fails enters
 $r^{(e)}_{\mathrm{bad}}$ , Corollary S6.10.)
∎

Corollary S6.10 (Per-epoch shared cost; lifetime reading).

Fix an analyzed epoch $e$ satisfying the hypotheses of
Theorem S6.9(i)–(iii) including the additional rate
conditions of clause (iii), read per epoch:
 $D_{0,e}(M)\,V_{e}(M)=o(M)$ and
 $\varepsilon_{e}(M)\,d_{\mathrm{good},e}(M)\to 0$ (under
A13, from $\varepsilon_{e}(M)\to 0$ ); let
 $r^{(e)}_{\mathrm{bad}}=r^{(e)}_{\mathrm{bad}}(M)$ be
the expected per-user demand mass in epoch $e$ on $\mathsf{Bad}$ 
(Definition S4.10) or A11-violating pairs (each such
pair charged at most once ever, Theorem S6.2(i)). Then

 $\limsup_{M\to\infty}\ \mathbb{E}\big[\bar{C}^{(e)}_{M,\mathrm{share}}\big]\;\leq\;\limsup_{M\to\infty}\Big(B_{\mathrm{fl}}(M)\,\bar{\gamma}(M)\,d_{\mathrm{good}}\;+\;r^{(e)}_{\mathrm{bad}}(M)\Big),$ 

and under a shrinking-front-loading schedule with bounded mass (§S6.5, A13)
the first term vanishes. The personal component is a lifetime
inventory: at most $d_{\mathrm{priv}}$ expected required pair-confirmations over
the deployment’s life (Theorem S6.2(ii)). The headline
reading “lifetime personal inventory plus current-epoch shared
load” therefore satisfies $\limsup_{M\to\infty}\big(\text{lifetime personal}+\text{epoch-$e$ shared}\big)\leq d_{\mathrm{priv}}+\limsup_{M\to\infty}r^{(e)}_{\mathrm{bad}}(M)$ .
This is not a bound on the sum of shared costs over an
unbounded number of epochs: the present accounting yields a lifetime
shared upper bound whenever the following series of per-epoch
upper bounds is summable — a sufficient condition only: the terms
are loose upper bounds, so divergence of this series implies nothing
about the true lifetime cost — with the coverage parameters read
per epoch ( $D_{0,e},\varepsilon_{e}$ ; a deployment asserting
A11 with epoch-uniform parameters drops the subscripts),

 $\sum_{e}\Big(A_{e}(M)\,\tfrac{V_{e}(M)}{M}+B_{\mathrm{fl}}^{(e)}(M)\,\bar{\gamma}_{e}(M)\,d_{\mathrm{good},e}\\
+c_{\mathrm{tool}}\big(D_{0,e}(M)\tfrac{V_{e}(M)}{M}+\varepsilon_{e}(M)\,d_{\mathrm{good},e}\big)+r^{(e)}_{\mathrm{bad}}\Big)<\infty,$ 

in which case lifetime expected total cost is at most $d_{\mathrm{priv}}$ plus
that sum; the lifetime shareable mass comparison of
Theorem S6.2(iii) ( $d^{\infty}_{\mathrm{tool}}$ ) bounds the
required component unconditionally in any case. These are upper bounds;
no matching lower bound is claimed.

Proof.

Combine Theorem S6.9(i)–(iii) over epoch $e$ with Theorem S6.2(i)–(iii); the summable case is termwise.
∎

Remark S6.11 (Reading of Theorem S6.9).

Four observations govern the interpretation of
Theorem S6.9. First, the residual charges are the design operating as intended:
each residual poisoned-demand confirmation is a personal gate
inspection of an unpromoted or flagged item, and each residual
handle-arrival confirmation is a personal gate inspection of bytes
that reached this user through an untrusted channel — the
certificate amortises trusted-channel reuse, never untrusted-channel
arrival (Rule R4(c), S18); both are
exactly the inspections the design intends (cf. A11). Second, the
front-loading term is the price of certifying popular items rather
than an overhead: each within-window confirmation is a
fresh honest inspection before finalisation: exactly the
pre-promotion scrutiny the exponent $N-f$ of
Theorem S5.13 counts. Third, the
bound is informative when $\bar{\gamma}\lesssim 1/B_{\mathrm{fl}}$ ; for larger $\bar{\gamma}$ 
it remains valid but loose, a bounded multiple of $d$ ,
with Theorem S6.2 the binding guarantee throughout.
Fourth, these are conditional accounting statements.
Clauses (i)–(ii) consume the ledger, smoothness, competence,
juror-supply, and demand-shape hypotheses; clause (iii) additionally consumes A11 — the
assumption carrying publication, promotion, and
arrival-representation coverage jointly — together with the
product condition $D_{0}(M)V(M)=o(M)$ and the remaining vanishing
conditions of Theorem S6.9(iii). The
shrinking-front-loading conditions describe changing time geometry,
never a consequence of population growth alone
(Remark S6.12); no clause derives coverage or shrinking
front-loading from the protocol. The assumption-free protocol
guarantee is the pathwise cap of Theorem S6.2(i), with
clause (ii) giving its reference-model standalone-mass ceiling.

## S6.5 Conditional co-scaling

Status of the corridor.

The corridor collects, in one place, every constraint the co-scaling
result uses; each row exists elsewhere and none is new. Two of its rows
are schedules of protocol constants ( $g_{N},g_{k}$ ), which a
deployment controls; the rest are empirical or behavioural properties
( $\beta$ , $\bar{\gamma}$ , $\rho_{\mathrm{seat}}$ , $q+p_{b}$ , $d$ , the coverage law
 $(D_{0},\varepsilon)$ of A11, the Sybil bounds), which a
deployment measures (N17) and cannot set. The
co-scaling theorem is therefore a compatibility statement: if
the measured environment satisfies its rows, then schedules
exist under which security, resolution correctness, and budget improve
together; and the floor of Theorem S6.2 holds outside the
corridor unconditionally.

Constraint

Source

Role

Example schedule

 $V(M)=\Theta(M^{\beta})$ , $\beta\in(0,1)$

Remark S6.12 (Operational reading of the shrinking-front-loading
rows).

For steady demand over an epoch of length $L$ with fixed window
 $T_{\mathrm{win}}$ , $\bar{\gamma}\approx T_{\mathrm{win}}/L$ independently of $M$ 
(A10, commentary): population growth alone does not shrink
 $\bar{\gamma}$ . The rows $\bar{\gamma}(M)(1+\kappa(M))=o(1)$ and $\bar{\gamma}(M)k(M)\to 0$ 
therefore describe deployments whose time geometry changes with
scale — finalisation windows shrinking relative to the epoch, or
demand growing more diffuse as the population grows — and are to be
read as measured properties of that geometry, not consequences of
 $M\to\infty$ . Where they fail, the fixed- $\bar{\gamma}$ reading of
Theorem S6.9 is the operating point: pipeline cost falls to
the front-loading multiple $B_{\mathrm{fl}}\,\bar{\gamma}\,d_{\mathrm{good}}$ of the standalone single-user shareable mass,
with Theorem S6.2 binding throughout
(Remark S6.11).

A concrete schedule satisfying every row simultaneously:
 $f_{\mathrm{s}}=\mathcal{O}(1)$ , $f(M)=\log M$ , $N(M)=2\log M$ , $k(M)=f_{\mathrm{s}}+\log^{2}M$ ,
against measured $\bar{\gamma}(M)=\log^{-3}M$ and bounded measured $d$ (A13); then with $\rho_{\mathrm{seat}}$ bounded
below, $\kappa=\Theta(\log^{2}M)$ , so
 $\bar{\gamma}(1+\kappa)=\Theta(\log^{-1}M)\to 0$ ;
for an illustrative measured coverage law with
 $D_{0}\asymp N/\bar{\gamma}=\Theta(\log^{4}M)$ and empirically validated
 $\varepsilon(M)\to 0$ , $D_{0}V=\Theta(M^{\beta}\log^{4}M)=o(M)$ ; and
 $Nk=\Theta(\log^{3}M)=o(M^{1-\beta})$ .

Theorem S6.13 (Joint scaling of safety, wrongful-eviction risk, and cost).

Adopt Rule R13 with the default bars, A9, A10,
A11, A12, A13, and saturation
 $V(M)=\Theta(M^{\beta})$ , $\beta\in(0,1)$ . Assume also the
coverage-rate condition of Theorem S6.9(iii),
 $D_{0}(M)\,V(M)=o(M)$ (under the Heaps form, equivalently
 $D_{0}(M)=o(M^{1-\beta})$ ; $\varepsilon(M)\to 0$ per A11). Suppose
 $f(M)=o(M^{1-\beta})$ and $q+p_{b}>1$ ; fix $\gamma_{0}\in(0,q+p_{b}-1)$ .
Choose margin schedules $g_{N}(M),g_{k}(M)\to\infty$ with
 $g_{N},g_{k}=o(M^{1-\beta})$ and $f_{\mathrm{s}}(M)=o(g_{k}(M))$ , and set
 $N(M)=f(M)+g_{N}(M)$ , $k(M)=f_{\mathrm{s}}(M)+g_{k}(M)$ ,
 $m(M)=f_{\mathrm{s}}(M)+\lceil(1-q+\gamma_{0})g_{k}(M)\rceil$ ,
 $m_{\mathrm{rej}}(M)=k(M)+1-m(M)$ (so every panel that gathers $k$ 
verdicts resolves decisively and $m_{\mathrm{rej}}>f_{\mathrm{s}}$ for all large
 $M$ ). All protocol caps and multiplicity parameters not explicitly
scheduled here are uniformly $\mathcal{O}(1)$ in $M$ (fixed TCB
configuration along the scaling, convention (C8)); in particular
 $E_{\max},\rho_{\mathrm{sub}},r_{\max},c_{\mathrm{ceil}},c_{\mathrm{p}},s_{0},D_{\mathrm{pool}},c_{\mathrm{batch}},c_{\mathrm{cand}}=\mathcal{O}(1)$ , hence $c_{\mathrm{acc}}=c_{\mathrm{ceil}}+r_{\max}=\mathcal{O}(1)$ , while
 $c_{\mathrm{tool}}=\mathcal{O}(1)$ by A12. This is load-bearing: the
conclusions
 $A(M)=\mathcal{O}\!\big(E_{\max}c_{\mathrm{tool}}^{2}(N+c_{\mathrm{acc}}f)(1+\kappa)\big)=o(M^{1-\beta})$ and $K(M)=o(M^{1-\beta})$ consume it.
Assume further the cross-term condition
 $N(M)\,k(M)=o(M^{1-\beta})$ (which, since
 $N>f$ , implies $f(M)k(M)=o(M^{1-\beta})$ ) and the shrinking front-loading condition
 $\bar{\gamma}(M)\,k(M)\to 0$ ( $\kappa=\Theta(k)$ for $\rho_{\mathrm{seat}}$ bounded below);
both are achievable by choosing $g_{k}$ to grow slowly against the
measured front-loading of the demand stream. Then:

(i) 

(Safety, per poisoned entry.)
 $\mathbb{P}[\text{promotion of a fixed poisoned entry}]\leq(1-q)^{g_{N}(M)}+e^{-2\gamma_{0}^{2}g_{k}(M)}\to 0$ (Theorem S5.13), against adaptive
presentation and at any reach (INV-6). (System-wide,
per anchor epoch.) If additionally
 $2f(M)\,\rho_{\mathrm{sub}}\big((1-q)^{g_{N}(M)}+e^{-2\gamma_{0}^{2}g_{k}(M)}\big)\to 0$ , the
per-anchor-epoch bound of Corollary S5.27 over all
adversary-published attempts anchored in the epoch also vanishes; the example schedule satisfies
this: with $f=\log M$ , $g_{N}=\log M$ (natural logarithm throughout; a fixed
change of logarithm base only rescales the schedule by a constant and
leaves every limit below unaffected),
 $2f\rho_{\mathrm{sub}}(1-q)^{g_{N}}=2\rho_{\mathrm{sub}}\log M\cdot M^{\ln(1-q)}\to 0$ , and
 $2f\rho_{\mathrm{sub}}\,e^{-2\gamma_{0}^{2}\log^{2}M}\to 0$ a fortiori;

(ii) 

(Wrongful eviction.) with $\delta_{b}:=\tfrac{q+p_{b}-1-\gamma_{0}}{2}>0$ , for all sufficiently
large $M$ a benign item is evicted, penalising its
publisher, with probability at most $e^{-2\delta_{b}^{2}g_{k}(M)}\to 0$ 
(Propositions S5.20, S5.22); equivalently
 $\mathbb{P}[\text{vindication}]\geq\mathbb{P}[\text{resolution}]-e^{-2\delta_{b}^{2}g_{k}(M)}$ , resolution being the demand-side event of
Remark S5.21;

(iii) 

(Budget, per analyzed epoch.) with $\rho_{\mathrm{seat}}$ bounded
below, $\mathbb{E}[\bar{C}^{\mathrm{pipe,good}}_{M}]\leq A(M)\,\mathcal{O}(M^{\beta-1})+B_{\mathrm{fl}}\,\bar{\gamma}(M)\,d_{\mathrm{good}}=o(1)$ , and
the benign ledger residual is $o(1)$ 
(Theorem S6.9(iii)): the analyzed epoch’s shared
confirmation load vanishes, while the lifetime personal inventory
remains at most $d_{\mathrm{priv}}$ (Theorem S6.2) and demand on
 $\mathsf{Bad}$ or assumption-violating pairs adds that epoch’s
residual $r^{(e)}_{\mathrm{bad}}$ , bounded pathwise by
Theorem S6.2(i). No claim is made about the sum over an
unbounded number of epochs without the summability condition of
Corollary S6.10.

All three hold with no assumption on how widely a poison spreads.

Proof.

(i) $N-f=g_{N}$ , and
 $\delta=\frac{m-f_{\mathrm{s}}}{k-f_{\mathrm{s}}}-(1-q)\geq\gamma_{0}$ by the choice of $m$ , so
Theorem S5.13 gives the bound with exponents $g_{N}$ and
 $2\gamma_{0}^{2}g_{k}$ .
(ii) $m/(k-f_{\mathrm{s}})\leq(1-q+\gamma_{0})+(f_{\mathrm{s}}+1)/g_{k}(M)$ ; since
 $1-q+\gamma_{0}=p_{b}-2\delta_{b}$ by the definition of $\delta_{b}$ (using
 $\gamma_{0}<q+p_{b}-1$ , so $\delta_{b}>0$ ), and $(f_{\mathrm{s}}+1)/g_{k}(M)\to 0$ is
eventually at most $\delta_{b}$ , we get
 $m/(k-f_{\mathrm{s}})\leq p_{b}-\delta_{b}$ for all large $M$ , and
Proposition S5.20 applies;
Proposition S5.22(ii) restates the same event as the
publisher penalty.
(iii) By Lemma S6.8:
 $A(M)=\mathcal{O}(E_{\max}c_{\mathrm{tool}}^{2}(N(M)+c_{\mathrm{acc}}f(M))(1+\kappa(M)))=o(M^{1-\beta})$ 
by the margin schedules, the cross-term condition, and $\kappa=\Theta(k)$ , so the first term
is $o(M^{1-\beta})\cdot\mathcal{O}(M^{\beta-1})=o(1)$ ; the second term is
 $\mathcal{O}(E_{\max}\,\kappa(M)\,\bar{\gamma}(M))\,d=o(1)$ by the shrinking
front-loading condition and the bounded mass (A13);
the residual clause of Theorem S6.9(iii) applies by the
coverage-rate hypothesis $D_{0}(M)\,V(M)=o(M)$ assumed in the
statement, with $\varepsilon(M)\,d_{\mathrm{good}}(M)\to 0$ from
 $\varepsilon(M)\to 0$ (A11) and A13.
∎

Remark S6.14 (Role of the saturation exponent).

Smaller $\beta$ is uniformly favourable: faster budget decay
 $M^{-(1-\beta)}$ and a weaker Sybil requirement
 $f(M)=o(M^{1-\beta})$ . There is no design trade-off along $\beta$ : it
is not a design parameter but an empirical property of the community’s demand
stream, to be measured (N17);
Theorem S5.11, which references neither $\beta$ nor the demand
model, remains available at any
 $\beta$ under its own hypotheses (Remark S5.12). A worked Zipf instantiation, including the tail, is
§S8.5.

(§S1.1). §S7.2 collects design rationale;

and residuals; §S7.5 summarises the composed guarantees.

## S7.2 Design rationale

Four rationale remarks whose content adjoins specific formal
results (the freshness asymmetry, the reading of $q$ , the
accountability calibration, and the roles of the protocol
clocks) are set as numbered Remarks adjacent to their points of use
(Remarks S4.7, S5.19, S5.5,
S5.26) and are indexed here for completeness. The notes below
record the remaining design rationale.

Note N1 (Model lineage).

The target property is an integrity property: content from an untrusted
source must not drive a sensitive action unless the user explicitly
authorised the exact value involved. Integrity is the lattice dual of
Denning’s confidentiality flow Denning (1976); Denning & Denning (1977), made
precise by Biba Biba (1977); the behavioural definition is
noninterference Goguen & Meseguer (1982), proved by the unwinding method
Goguen & Meseguer (1984) transported to a small-step operational semantics
Volpano et al. (1996); Sabelfeld & Myers (2003). Two refinements distinguish PerMIT. First, PerMIT
does permit untrusted-derived values to reach sensitive actions,
but only after a user confirmation; a confirmation is an
endorsement Myers et al. (2006), and the danger that an attacker
controls what is endorsed is ruled out by robustness
Zdancewic & Myers (2005); Myers et al. (2006). Second, PerMIT lets verified crystallized knowledge
inform the Protected LLM’s reasoning; the right noninterference
statement is therefore one modulo the release history, in the
delimited- and gradual-release tradition
Sabelfeld & Sands (2009); Sabelfeld & Myers (2004); Askarov & Sabelfeld (2007), with the display transcript
playing the role of the release-event history. The carve-out is the
full transcript: candidate previews as well as decision-pane
displays, rejections as well as acceptances
(Definition S3.8). The substrate, including the
provenance-tracking interpreter and the separation of a trusted
orchestrating model from a quarantined reader, is CaMeL Debenedetti et al. (2025);
we name these the Protected LLM ( $P$ ) and the Quarantined Reader LLM
( $Q$ ), realizing $Q$ as a Recursive Language Model Zhang et al. (2025). Related
efforts: AgentDojo Debenedetti et al. (2024), information-flow control for
agents Costa et al. (2025), memory-poisoning defenses Wei et al. (2025),
collaborative memory Rezazadeh et al. (2025). CaMeL leaves formal verification as future work (Debenedetti et al., 2025, §10). PerMIT leaves planning and tool choice to the agent while bounding its observations and data flows through the finite interface of Figures S2–S3, including tool-output capture and argument mediation. The channel analysis (Lemmas S3.17–S3.18) checks this written specification clause by clause.

Note N2 (The endorsement unit).

Endorsement is per (value, tool) and cached indefinitely while the relevant local copy is retained. A conforming instantiation of the requirements-level Option O3 would bound its scope-checked tool-authorization lifetime, but is outside the proved model. Combinations, orderings, and timings of separately endorsed values are ungated. The policy does not bind argument position, task, purpose, or the complete argument tuple. Reasoning Integrity excludes additional hidden influence when the complete display transcript is fixed; it does not certify the safety of later combinations (S10, S11). Options O1–O2 refine tool-scoped checks, not complete-transaction approval.

Note N3 (The reader asymmetry).

 $Q$ may read the whole store because its output is always untrusted or
handle-hidden; $R_{\mathcal{E}}$ may read only the endorsed store because its
output flows to $P$ as readable endorsed bytes. Neither can be relaxed
without breaking a theorem: a $\mathsf{U}$ -reading $R_{\mathcal{E}}$ violates
Theorem S3.25; an $\mathbb{E}$ -emitting $Q$ violates
Lemma S3.14.

Note N4 (Failure indistinguishability, operationally).

Under Rule R7, the agent cannot distinguish “the untrusted
store contained nothing useful” from “the reader failed while
looking”: both worlds return the same operation-determined handle, so
neither the contents nor the volume of untrusted memory can signal
through the failure path. Likewise, whether an identifier is occupied
by a $\mathsf{U}$ -block is a fact the attacker influences through the number of
blocks captured per call, which is why occupancy is never observable
(Rule R10(b)). The store-quota policy of
convention (C7) exists because a volume-dependent error would reopen
exactly this channel (S7).

Note N5 (The human channel).

A6 idealizes the user’s typing as exogenous. In reality,
displayed content can steer what the user subsequently types, which
enters as trusted input. Influence of displayed content on typing is
accounted for exactly insofar as that content appears in the display
transcript, which, by Rule R8(e), records every
attacker-influenceable rendering the user sees, on both surfaces. The
design therefore discharges the idealization’s practical content by
logging both surfaces: the release history covers every surface through
which such influence can pass, so Corollary S3.26 and
N7 remain faithful even where A6 frays. The
residual is disclosed at S9 and
§S7.4.

Note N6 (The batch gate is all-or-nothing).

The selected batch design is all-or-nothing: a per-page decision
returned to $P$ would leak the attacker-influenceable page count
(Rule R9(c)), and the aggregate decision avoids that
channel at the cost that one poisoned element forces rejection of the
whole batch — an availability and attention cost, not a safety gap.
Sufficiency of this design is what is established; no impossibility of
alternative fixed-shape or padded designs is claimed. The user may reject at any page,
aborting the remainder (Rule R9(d)), so
attacker-imposed attention is bounded by the user’s own patience, never
compelled. Both facts argue for sizing $c_{\mathrm{batch}}$ conservatively
(S12).

Note N7 (The transcript is recorded, not prevented).

The harness records every display event, selection renderings
included, in a hash-chained audit log, a tamper-evident
materialization of $\mathsf{DT}$ . Corollary S3.26 therefore has operational force: the
transcript supplies a complete record of the modeled release
surfaces relevant to the protected behaviours — ungated sinks,
Protected-LLM operations, tool invocations
(Definition S3.11) — missing no surface on which
attacker-shaped content was ever rendered; it does not by itself
reconstruct causation, timing, unreleased tool outputs, or attacker
identity. This is not a proof that the user cannot be manipulated;
it is a proof that any attacker influence on those behaviours must
pass through the logged display transcript or an explicitly
accepted endorsement. Logging makes influence visible and
attributable after the fact — attributable to a recorded release
or decision, not necessarily to an attacker identity; the
prevention half of the design is Theorem S3.21’s gate,
which stops any value that is neither user-typed nor endorsed
(personally, or, under delegation, by a qualifying community
quorum) for the exact sensitive tool from ever crossing that
boundary (argument-value integrity: the decision to invoke a tool,
and zero-argument sensitive calls, are scoped at
§S7.4(X)).

Note N8 (What previews can and cannot do).

An adversarial reader can decorate a malicious candidate with a
benign-looking preview. Within the formal model, previews are inert:
they never enter $\sigma_{P}$ (Lemma S3.12), and
endorsement binds to the true resolved bytes on the decision pane
(A3–A4), so a preview can never forge an
endorsement. Outside the formal model, a preview can influence the
human: what the user chooses to examine, and, through
A6’s idealization, what the user subsequently types. This is
precisely why previews are included in the display transcript and the
audit log (S9).

Note N9 (What the gate does to the detection task).

The confirmation gate reshapes the detection task an honest inspector
faces, in three architectural ways. First, stealth is eliminated by
construction: Rule R8 renders the exact bytes that will
flow, in full, on every gated invocation, so no gated content can
reach a sensitive action unseen — there is no channel by which
poison avoids display altogether. Second, hijacking-style content —
bytes written to be read as instructions rather than data — is
surfaced on the decision pane out of the narrative context it was
designed to exploit, isolated against a fixed provenance zone
(A3–A4); this plausibly raises $q_{\mathrm{pre}}$ for
that class of poison, but it is offered as motivation for the
parameter, never as a proof of it: $q_{\mathrm{pre}}$ remains an empirical,
weakest-target quantity (Remark S5.19), estimated by logging, not
derived from the display design. Third, because every gate decision
is logged (Definition S3.8), $q$ is made
log-measurable rather than merely posited: the validation protocol of
N17 is only possible because the architecture records
the events it needs. None of this touches semantic poison —
content that is simply false or misleading, with no injection
mechanism for display to expose — which gains nothing from being
shown out of context and remains exactly the disclosed residual of
S14, consumed by no proof in this document.

Note N10 (Federated lineage).

The federated tier is built on machinery network security has already
made rigorous: threshold endorsement from the PGP web of trust
Maurer (1996); transparency and revocation from Certificate
Transparency Laurie et al. (2013); Laurie et al. (2021); the identity-cost requirement of the
Sybil literature Douceur (2002); Dwork & Naor (2007) , the precise
reason the scaling results are conditional on an attested or
cost-bounded setting. The panel’s competence condition is the classical
jury condition Grofman et al. (1983); Allen et al. (2021); Martel et al. (2024).

Note N11 (What pool diversity buys).

The requirement $D_{\mathrm{pool}}$ caps per-pool verdicts and mixes the
fresh-inspector population, attacking correlated blind spots and
demand-side selection bias among panelists. Diversity bounds
correlation, not the minimum: since the adversary chooses
whom to present the item to (§S8.1), $q$ remains a uniform
lower bound over the honest identities the adversary can target, and
the pool caps of Definition S4.3 force those targets to span
 $D_{\mathrm{pool}}$ pools rather than one cluster, raising the effective bound
from the most fallible cluster toward the most fallible identity per
pool, never to a population average. The per-person correlation across
logged re-inspections is discharged structurally by
fresh-inspection counting (INV-1); the residual across
unlogged personal inspections is a calibration matter
(Remark S5.19, Definition S4.5, S13).

Note N12 (Reputation farming).

 $f$ , $W_{\mathrm{adv}}$ , and $f_{\mathrm{s}}$ are lifetime totals over every identity the
adversary ever controls (§S4.1), capped by the
attestation layer’s issuance; farming weight before spending it
changes when the capacity exists, never its total, so no bound
stated against the lifetime totals can be outrun. Publisher penalties
(Rule R13(vii)) only shrink the adversary’s capacity below
these totals, so every bound stated against them remains valid. The
economic deterrent of burning accrued reputation is real but
deliberately unused in any proof: the proofs assume the attacker
spends everything (S3).

Note N13 (The $\mathsf{evict}$ residual).

 $\mathsf{evict}$ is owner-scoped but $P$ -invocable and ungated, so an
erring (not attacker-steered, which
Lemmas S3.12–S3.18 exclude) Protected LLM can
remove its owner’s endorsed blocks without a confirmation; the
no-deletion guarantee of Lemma S5.8 is a third-party
guarantee only. Deployments wanting first-party cover may gate
 $\mathsf{evict}$ on $\mathbb{E}$ -labelled blocks like any sensitive call, a
further monotone hardening in the sense of
Proposition S3.28. The uniform- $\mathsf{ack}$ totalisation of $\mathsf{evict}$ 
(Figure S3, convention (v)) changes only the reply
surface, never this capability: the residual is unchanged, and the
realised outcome remains visible to the owner in the audit log.
The residual includes evictions $P$ performs in reaction to
legitimate federation events (notifications are $\mathbb{E}$ -level log data): a
third party can occasion such a reaction but not steer it —
Lemmas S3.12–S3.18 exclude any $\mathsf{U}$ -derived
influence on the choice — so this is erring-agent behaviour, outside the
threat model (§S7.4(V)), not an attacker channel.

Note N14 (Availability degrades gracefully, never destructively).

All federated guarantees are safety guarantees; availability is
explicitly not guaranteed, but its loss is bounded in harm (in required confirmations) per
dispute: under A9, at most $\kappa=(k{+}1)/\rho_{\mathrm{seat}}$ affected
demander arrivals in expectation, with exponential tail
(Lemma S5.6); if A12 is additionally read
over the entire unresolved-dispute demand horizon, at most
 $c_{\mathrm{tool}}\,\kappa$ additional required confirmations in expectation,
with the corresponding tail at thresholds scaled by $c_{\mathrm{tool}}$ ; under
the stated per-epoch reading of A12, only the
corresponding per-epoch conversion is asserted, while the pathwise
per-user cap remains valid for a freeze of any duration
(Lemma S5.24(ii)); pathwise, independent of every federated and demand assumption (persistence exceptions per INV-3), no affected
user exceeds the single-user cap, regardless of the freeze’s
duration (Lemma S5.24(ii)); recoverable (low wrongful-eviction
risk, Proposition S5.20); accountable on both sides
(Rule R13(v),(vii)); and never destructive
(Lemma S5.8): in the worst case the shared tier
contributes nothing and each user verifies personally, within the single-user cap of
Theorem S6.2. The per-epoch free charge renews, so the
adversary retains a per-epoch throughput bounded in the three
currencies of Lemma S5.24(iv); what it buys is bounded
required-confirmation harm and bounded per-epoch harassment throughput
(Lemma S5.24(ii),(iv)), never deletion — the
wall-clock duration of an unresolved freeze is deliberately
unbounded, resolution being demand-paced with no deadline
(Remark S5.21); its harm, not its duration, is what
is capped. A promoted benign item can be
harassed only through Suspended-state fast-track panels, at most
 $E_{\max}$ per item per epoch (Rule R13(vi)) and
penalty-escalating. An unpromoted benign candidate
cannot be retired by sequential vindicated disputes
(Rule R14(i)(b) exempts them, N15): each
adversarial rejection buys one freeze, resolved at the item’s own
demand rate, while occupying a rejection slot and, beyond the first
per identity per epoch, escalating the rejecter’s accountability; the
adversary’s per-epoch freeze throughput against any item being bounded
(Lem. S5.24(iv)), what it buys is bounded
required-confirmation harm within the single-user cap — of
unbounded wall-clock duration, the freeze being demand-paced —
never retirement, and the single-initiation
clause caps any identity, adversarial or honest, at one freeze per
item per epoch, so per-item harassment throughput is at most $f$ 
freezes per epoch deterministically. The entry cap of Rule R14(i) is likewise
availability-only: an item whose per-epoch entries are exhausted
(e.g. through repeated wrongful evictions, each already
exponentially rare, Proposition S5.20) waits for the epoch
boundary with its banked credits and any live promotion status
intact (convention (C4)), each demander meanwhile paying at most the
cap (Theorem S6.2(i)). Under this invalidating semantics, a suspension additionally converts its
demanders’ ungated reuse into at most one required confirmation each —
the cap confirmation they would in any case owe on demotion
(Corollary S6.5) — after which the pair is permanently
covered by a local $\mathsf{C}$ copy; this is priced once per demander and never
renewed.

Note N15 (The vindication exemption).

Rule R14(i)(b) counts only non-vindicated disputes
toward expiry. The exemption is what prevents both an adversary’s
wrongful rejections (each vindicated, each feeding the accountability
schedule of Rule R13(v)) and the honest wrongful-rejection
noise, which arrives at rate $1-p_{b}$ per benign fresh inspection and
would otherwise, at the co-scaling thresholds $N(M)\to\infty$ of
Theorem S6.13, retire every item before promotion
became arithmetically reachable, from terminating a benign item. Under
the default bars every resolved dispute ends in vindication or eviction
and every unresolved one ends in expiry, so this trigger fires only
through the optional neither-bar outcome of
Rule R13(iv); it remains availability-only and conservative
(Lemma S5.9).

Note N16 (Revocation, re-verification, and grandfathering).

Post-promotion suspensions and re-verification add panel and
re-inspection events but never multiply any user’s required cost
(Corollary S6.5): local endorsements are permanent, so
revocation campaigns tax the shared tier’s voluntary and verdict
effort, bounded per suspension by Rule R13(vi) and the rate
limits; a prior $\mathsf{CC}$ -only user additionally owes their first-ever
required confirmation of the pair — a cost within the cap,
already counted inside $d^{\infty}_{\mathrm{tool}}$ 
(Corollary S6.5), never beyond it. Re-verification draws its inspectors from
identities with no prior logged decision on the bytes (log-fresh,
Definition S4.5; a cohort that can include previously
exposed but never-logged inspectors, Remark S5.19), so the probability of a
uniformly convincing poison remaining uncaught across $E_{\mathrm{rv}}$ 
re-verification rounds, each reaching a cohort of new inspectors, is at
most $(1-q_{c})^{E_{\mathrm{rv}}}\to 0$ , with $q_{c}$ the correlated catch rate of
S13; the mechanism’s strength scales with the
supply of never-decided inspectors (population growth and new demand),
not with epoch renewal. Grandfathering: items promoted at lower
historical thresholds retain $\mathsf{CC}$ but enter the re-verification queue
until their endorsement meets the current bar. Within the formal semantics, first-party eviction aside
(N13), nothing re-forces a confirmed pair’s authorization;
a conforming instantiation of the requirements-level
Option O3 would re-force exactly the scope-checked tool
authorization on lease expiry — never reasoning readability —
an explicit trade stated and to be priced with that extension
(O3).

Note N17 (Validation without a user study).

Theorem S6.2 and the counting cores of
Lemmas S6.7, S6.8 are identities. Saturation
( $V(M)=o(M)$ ), fractional temporal smoothness (A10: log
each item’s realised front-loading fraction $\gamma_{v}$ , validated
specifically on the most-demanded items, where population averages
would hide spikes), coverage sufficiency (A11: log each item’s realized
residual fraction $U_{v,t}/n_{v,t}$ and the
fast-path-vs-gate split of live- $\mathsf{CC}$ demand — the handle-arrival
fraction, S18 — stratified on the most-demanded
items, with per-item time-to-promotion against accumulated demand), tool-spread
(A12: log per-item distinct-tool counts), bounded demand mass (A13: log per-user distinct
shareable-item demand per epoch across deployment growth), and the
verdict rate (A9: log verdict-eligibility rates on
disputed items) are modelling assumptions checked by logging in the
prototype and by replaying the promotion and demand-paced panel
mechanics in simulation while sweeping the growth exponent, a replay
of an algorithm, not an experiment on people. The replay’s own fidelity is checked independently of any behavioural parameter by the closed form of §S8.2, whose support-level
consequences are byte-identity properties of the implementation rather than statistical agreements.
One caveat governs the behavioural parameters specifically: $q_{\mathrm{pre}}$ , $q_{\mathrm{pan}}$ , $p_{b}$ ,
and $\rho_{\mathrm{seat}}$ are uniform conditional lower bounds, each validated on its own stratum (gate inspections vs. eligible verdicts), on the
prior-atom $\sigma$ -algebras of §S8.1; aggregate log rates do not
directly estimate such bounds. Their validation therefore reports
stratified rates (by identity class, presentation context, and flag
state) rather than population averages, uses lower confidence bounds rather than point
estimates, corrects for adaptive selection of inspection targets, and
monitors nonstationarity across epochs; the estimand is the infimum
over strata, and the deployed constants ( $N,k,m$ margins,
 $\theta_{\mathrm{acc}}$ , $c_{\mathrm{ceil}}$ ) are set against that infimum with margin, never against
a mean.
These stratified, lower-confidence-bound estimates are falsification
and calibration evidence: they can refute an assumed cap — a stratum
whose observed rate falls below the claimed bound — and can inform
recalibration, but they do not certify the uniform
infimum-over-history bounds $q_{\mathrm{pre}},q_{\mathrm{pan}},p_{b}$ require. Sparse or
unobserved strata (rare identity classes, rare presentation contexts) are
not covered by any finite sample, and the uniform bounds remain modelling
hypotheses, not measured quantities.
One further caveat: the classification $\mathsf{Bad}$ is exogenous
(Definition S4.10), so the log alone cannot label a trial as
poisoned or benign; estimating $q_{\mathrm{pre}}$ , $q_{\mathrm{pan}}$ , and $p_{b}$ therefore
requires an exogenously labeled corpus — red-team–injected
known-poison items and expert-adjudicated benign samples — with the
stratified lower-confidence-bound machinery applied to the labeled
trials. Unlabeled log rates validate the protocol mechanics only
( $\rho_{\mathrm{seat}}$ , realised $\gamma_{v}$ , $U_{v,t}/n_{v,t}$ , tool spread,
demand mass).

Note N18 (Total effort, including rejections).

The ledger of Lemma S6.7 covers every counted (required or voluntary) durable accepting pair-confirmation of shareable items, redundant covered-gate acceptances and the personal, per-task, and option-induced streams excluded or separately charged (§S6.1, Corollary S6.6); rejection and attention effort is a
distinct resource with a two-sided account. Under the durable
semantics of Rule R2 there are no
accepted-but-uncommitted confirmations: every accepting review
commits its copy. What remains uncounted is attention: rejected
