dialogs, abandonment, batch-page review, repeated reviews of an
already-accepted pair within one transaction (counted once,
Definition S2.5), and redundant covered-gate decisions.
Protocol-effective rejections are tightly capped: one
dispute-initiation per identity per byte string per epoch
(Rule R13(i)), at most $r_{\max}$ open slots, and the
accountability schedule of Rule R13(v). Rejection
dialogs, by contrast, recur: a user who rejects an uncovered pair holds
no local $\mathsf{C}$ copy, so each later attempted use of that pair fires the
gate again, and the later rejections are logged as corroboration or
advisory flags (Rule R13(i)) — the protocol does not
bound a user’s total rejection dialogs on one value. What bounds them is
the user’s own use stream and the exposure caps: per retrieval at most
 $c_{\mathrm{cand}}$ candidates are surfaced (Rule R14(ii)), disputed
items are flagged and ordered last, paraphrase spam consumes the
submitter’s rate limit per byte-distinct variant (Rule R12,
Corollary S5.27), and a $P$ -emitted $\mathsf{confirm}$ or handle-expressed tool call on a
covered pair fires a covered gate, protocol-inert on both
branches (Rule R13(i)), reasoning-target repetition on locally readable bytes included (reasoning coverage, Rule R13(i)): the resulting redundant
confirmations and rejections have no shared-tier effect and are
excluded from every ledger count (§S6.1), so agent
repetition on covered values costs the user attention only, disclosed
here — and $P$ can re-emit $\mathsf{confirm}$ on a repeatedly-rejected,
uncovered handle every step: covered-gate inertness neutralises
covered values only, by design. Repeated
dialogs arising from the user’s own repeated attempts at an
uncovered pair are a first-party cost of declining, of the same
character as the $\mathsf{evict}$ residual (N13): attacker-steered
repetition is excluded (Lemmas S3.12–S3.18), but
the model places no constant on it. Accordingly, every “bounded-harm”
statement in this document is a statement about required user approvals (Theorem S6.2), with inspection effort disclosed
here.
One deployment device reduces this attention cost without touching
the semantics: the decision pane may visibly badge a covered gate —
the resolved bytes byte-match a local in-scope copy, an owner-side
deterministic fact (Rule R13(i)) — and offer a one-tap
accept; the gate, its display, and its logged decision are unchanged.
Interpreter-level rate-limiting of repeated uncovered gate requests is
a candidate further hardening, monotone in the sense of
Proposition S3.28; it is an operational-semantics change
requiring its own transition, transcript, and two-run treatment, and
is not specified in this document.

## S7.3 Security and robustness analysis

Each entry states an attack vector or failure mode in its strongest
form (Vector), the facts that govern it (Disposition),
and its Status per §S7.1. Entries are grouped by
attack surface.

S7.3.1 Attacks on the endorsement mechanism

Analysis S1 (Paraphrase-variant families).

Vector. Freshness is byte-keyed
(INV-1). An adversary submits paraphrase variants of the
same poison as distinct byte strings; every inspector, including one
already fooled by a sibling variant, is fresh again on each variant,
so the per-person discharge of correlation is byte-scoped, not
content-scoped.
Disposition. The discharge of per-person correlation is indeed
per byte identity (convention (C5)), and the arithmetic is
unchanged: each variant is a distinct item consuming the submitter’s
rate limit and its own union-bound term (Rule R12,
Corollary S5.27), and per-retrieval exposure is capped
at $c_{\mathrm{cand}}$ (Rule R14(ii), N18). What survives
is an interpretation obligation on the deployer: $q$ inside
each union-bound term must be calibrated as the catch rate against
the variant family, not against a single presentation; an
inspector fooled by one variant is plausibly likelier to be fooled by
its siblings. Deployments may additionally surface an advisory-only
similarity flag (“similar disputed or evicted items exist”)
alongside disputed items, of the zero-authority advisory class of
Rule R13(i): it informs inspectors without granting the
model computing similarity any protocol effect.
Status. Mechanism disposed; calibration duty disclosed. Not
used in any proof.

Analysis S2 (Self-dispute).

Vector. Reject one’s own item; fill the panel with one’s own
verdicts; sustain the dispute across epochs via one’s own demand events.
Disposition. The case-(b) bound of Theorem S5.13 does not
condition on the rejecter’s identity, so self-dispute gains only the panel
term already counted, while consuming a rejection slot and accountability
exposure; verdict capacity never renews (INV-5), capping the
adversary’s tally at $f_{\mathrm{s}}$ for the dispute’s life; self-initiations are
capped at one per identity per item per epoch (Rule R13(i)).
Status. Disposed (under stated assumptions; Theorem S5.13, INV-5). Not
used in any proof.

Analysis S3 (Reputation farming).

Vector. Farm attestation weight and reputation over time; spend it
in one campaign.
Disposition. $f$ , $W_{\mathrm{adv}}$ , $f_{\mathrm{s}}$ are lifetime totals over every
identity the adversary ever controls (§S4.1); every bound is
stated against the totals, so farming shifts timing, never capacity
(N12). The economic deterrent of burning reputation is real but
deliberately unused: the proofs assume the attacker spends everything.
Status. Disposed (under stated assumptions; lifetime-total definition). Not used in any proof.

Analysis S4 (Tool-targeted poison).

Vector. Disputes are byte-level but harm is tool-level: a poison
dangerous at tool $A$ and innocuous at tool $B$ is sincerely vindicated by
a panel drawn from $B$ -demanders, contradicting honest $A$ -rejecters; the
freeze lifts and the interrupted entry resumes.
Disposition. The two promotion paths behave differently.
Ordinary path at $A$ : disposed. Promotion at $A$ still requires $N$ 
credited fresh endorsements at $A$ itself (Lemma S3.14,
Definition S4.6), where inspectors face the harmful use, so
the case-(a) term of Theorem S5.13 applies with $q_{\mathrm{pre}}$ read at
 $A$ . Panel path: residual. The panel term is not bounded by
 $A$ -context competence: $q_{\mathrm{pan}}$ is uniform over the jurors’ own tool
contexts (Theorem S5.13(c)), and for $A$ -targeted, $B$ -benign
poison it may be near zero — sincere $B$ -juror vindication is then
likely, the freeze lifts, and the entry resumes toward its $A$ -threshold.
What remains against $A$ -promotion is the ordinary-path term, plus
renewable re-freezes: each epoch, each honest identity’s first rejecting
decision on the bytes — in particular each new $A$ -context detection —
re-freezes promotion at every tool (Rule R13(i)–(ii)).
Accountability erosion. Honest $A$ -rejecters contradicted by
 $B$ -vindications feed Rule R13(v); mitigation is threefold: the
free first charge per epoch, the per-fresh-inspection rate trigger
calibrated above the honest baseline (Remark S5.5), and conversion to
zero-authority advisory flags at the ceiling. We deliberately do not
exempt rejecters whose vindicating jurors sat at a different tool: because
a rejection at any tool freezes all tools (Rule R13(ii)), such an
exemption would let an adversary file every rejection “at” an obscure
tool no juror will ever demand, harvesting freezes with zero accountability
exposure and breaking the throughput bounds of Lemma S5.24(iv).
The per-pair scoping variant of Remark S4.9 removes this residual; it is a deployment choice, consumed by no theorem.
Status. Ordinary-path promotion at the targeted tool disposed;
panel-path vindication under cross-tool juries residual, disclosed;
accountability erosion residual, mitigated, availability-only. Not used in
any proof.

Analysis S5 (Panel-lapse exploitation and slot economics).

Vector. Reject a low-demand benign item; let the panel lapse; tax
the owner’s rate limit on each forced re-entry.
Disposition. The attack presupposes a deadline-based design; there
is no panel lapse and no forced re-entry (Lemma S5.24(iii)):
the only re-entry paths are owner-initiated, and slots free on resolution
or expiry. Roles of the clocks: Remark S5.26.
Status. Disposed (under stated assumptions; Lemma S5.24(iii)). Not used in any proof.

Analysis S6 (Juror exhaustion by wide pre-exposure).

Vector. Pre-expose the poison so widely that few verdict-eligible
(fresh, seat-weighted) demanders remain; the panel never fills.
Disposition. The item stalls frozen: promotion is blocked at
every tool with no deadline to exploit (Rule R13(ii)–(iii));
the cost is availability, never safety, borne by the adversary’s own
submission, and the benign-item variant is bounded at the cap
(Remark S5.25, Lemma S5.24(ii)). Pre-exposure
also feeds the defence: each additional honest rejection opportunity can freeze promotion (INV-2), and consumed
honest identities are never re-usable for later entries of the same bytes
(Corollary S5.27).
Status. Safety disposed $+$ availability residual (under
Theorem S5.13’s hypotheses; deterministic failure mode of
A9, disclosed at its commentary). Not used in any proof.

S7.3.2 Side channels and covert channels

Analysis S7 (Store-exhaustion side channel).

Vector. The store is idealized as unbounded; a real deployment
has quotas, and quota errors visible to $P$ leak the attacker-chosen
volume of captured blocks.
Disposition. Correct, which is why the quota policy is a
stated convention rather than an afterthought: deployments must apply
quotas to the untrusted tier by silent eviction, never by an error
visible to $P$ (convention (C7)); otherwise store exhaustion reopens
exactly the channel Rule R7 closes (N4). Under
the convention, no operation’s defined behaviour depends on aggregate
store volume, and Lemma S3.18(f) stands.
Status. Disposed (under stated assumptions), conditional on the stated deployment
policy (C7). Not used in any proof.

Analysis S8 (Timing channels).

Vector. The bisimulation matches $P$ -operations; wall-clock
timing of the harness is unmodelled and could leak.
Disposition. Conceded: timing and termination are out of scope
(§S7.4). The interface returns no clock or step-count
value, so no timing value exists in $\sigma_{P}$ to branch on
(Lemma S3.18(e)); a clock consulted through a tool
is already inside the model: its reading is captured to $\mathcal{M}_{\mathsf{U}}$ 
like any tool output (Rule R3) and never reaches
 $\sigma_{P}$ . The exclusion concerns only implicit timing: wall-clock
latency of the harness observed by an outside party.
Status. Residual, precisely scoped
(§S7.4(IV)). Not used in any proof.

S7.3.3 The human factor

Analysis S9 (Preview manipulation).

Vector. An adversarial reader decorates a malicious candidate
with a benign-looking preview and steers the user.
Disposition. Within the formal model, previews are inert: they
never enter $\sigma_{P}$ (Lemma S3.12), and endorsement
binds to the true resolved bytes on the decision pane
(A3–A4), so a preview can never forge an
endorsement: the wrong value is displayed truthfully and rejected
under A4. Outside the formal model, a preview can influence
what the user chooses to examine and, through A6’s
idealization, what the user subsequently types. That residual is
covered by design rather than assumed away: previews are part of the
display transcript and the audit log
(Definition S3.8, Rule R8(e)), so the
release history covers every surface through which such influence can
pass, and Corollary S3.26 remains faithful even where
A6 frays (N5, N8).
Status. Disposed (under stated assumptions) within the model; residual at the human
channel, disclosed. Not used in any proof.

Analysis S10 (Careless endorsement and the post-endorsement channel).

Vector. After one careless narrative confirmation (a false acceptance in the sense of Definition S4.10; a failure of A5), the
endorsed summary’s bytes are readable and flow, literal-expressed,
to data tools ungated (handle-expressed use gates regardless,
Rule R4(c));
a poisoned summary can both mislead reasoning and direct data-tool
egress.
Disposition. Correct, and no theorem is violated: the
influence entered through a transcript event the user accepted. The
compound shape is: one careless narrative endorsement (a failure of
A5) simultaneously (i) admits attacker-shaped content into
 $P$ ’s readable state and (ii) opens ungated literal-expressed data-tool egress of
endorsed and typed bytes (Rule R4(b); handle-expressed
use gates regardless, Rule R4(c)); the misled $P$ 
may then recombine separately endorsed values at sensitive tools
without further gates (S11). Containment is threefold:
the influence never crosses a sensitive boundary with bytes neither
user-typed nor endorsed for that tool (Theorem S3.21);
its triggering endorsement is a logged transcript event, and any
divergence it induces in the protected behaviours is
transcript-carried (Corollary S3.26); and the
community mechanism reduces the frequency of the triggering
endorsement for shareable content (Appendix S4). In
the federated tier the residual carries a further, deliberate cost:
an honest user who carelessly confirms and then publishes
attacker-crafted content will, on that item’s panel eviction, incur
the publisher penalty of Rule R13(vii), a corrective on
the publish gate; the penalty never touches any local store
(Lemma S5.8, Proposition S5.22).
Option O2 removes the ungated data-tool egress of
out-of-scope endorsed bytes (each such flow then gates once per pair,
Corollary S3.23(b)); Option O3, if instantiated conformingly, would limit
only scope-checked local tool authorization; data-tool enforcement
requires Option O2, and readable reasoning state is
unaffected (O3).
Status. Residual, disclosed, contained, and priced. Not used
in any proof.

Analysis S11 (Recombination of endorsed values).

Vector. Individually authorized values can form a harmful transaction without violating Action Integrity: approval of a recipient and a body separately for an email tool does not record approval of sending that body to that recipient. Order, timing, and task context can also change the meaning of reuse.
Disposition. Recombination is ungated by design. Theorem S3.25 excludes additional hidden influence for matched runs with equal complete display transcripts. It does not exclude influence through changed displays, incorrect endorsements, or inappropriate reuse in a later context. Option O1 narrows typed-value authority by sensitive tool, and Option O2 extends exact-tool checks to data tools; neither binds the complete transaction. Human or agent memory managers could review combined content and seek fresh endorsements before it becomes trusted, as proposed in the main paper (Appendix D.2.1). This is untested future work, not a guarantee for all later combinations. Context-bound or transaction-level authorization would require extended semantics and revised confirmation-cost accounting.
Status. Policy boundary and endorsement residual disclosed. Not used in any proof.

Analysis S12 (Batch-gate attention).

Vector. The batch gate displays every element’s bytes, but
displayed-in-full is not reviewed-in-full; A4 is weakest
here.
Disposition. Conceded: Definition S3.10 holds
literally at the batch gate (every element’s bytes are displayed,
Rule R9(b)), but the human premise A4 is at
its weakest, and deployments should size $c_{\mathrm{batch}}$ conservatively.
The selected batch design is all-or-nothing: a per-page decision
returned to $P$ would leak the attacker-influenceable page
count, and the aggregate decision avoids that channel at the cost that one poisoned element forces rejection of the whole batch:
an availability and attention cost, not a safety gap (no impossibility of alternative fixed-shape or padded designs is claimed); and the user
may reject at any page, aborting the remainder, so attacker-imposed
attention is bounded by the user’s own patience, never compelled
(Rule R9(c),(d), N6). Deployments may additionally cap total batch cardinality (Rule R9(b)), converting oversize collections into a fixed, decision-free reject; the cap lies outside the mechanized batch layer (§S9).
Status. Mechanism disposed; residual at the human premise,
disclosed. Not used in any proof.

S7.3.4 Adverse parameter regimes

Analysis S13 (Cross-person correlated carelessness).

Vector. A uniformly convincing poison lowers every fresh
inspector’s catch rate together; the exponential bounds assume
independently varying carelessness and collapse toward $1-q_{c}$ under
engineered correlation.
Disposition. The premise of the vector is corrected first: the
bounds of Theorem S5.13 assume no independence across
inspectors; they assume a uniform conditional lower bound $q$ 
on each fresh inspection given the entire past (§S8.1).
What engineered cross-person correlation attacks is that bound itself:
a uniformly convincing poison is one for which the honest conditional
catch rate, after conditioning on earlier misses, is some
 $q_{c}<q$ . Three facts limit the damage: the theorem remains valid with
 $q$ read as $q_{c}$ , the bound degrading toward $1-q_{c}$ transparently,
never silently; the diversity requirement $D_{\mathrm{pool}}$ mixes
fresh-inspector pools, raising $q_{c}$ (N11); and periodic
re-verification re-presents the bytes to inspectors with no prior
decision, so the probability of remaining uncaught across $E_{\mathrm{rv}}$ 
rounds is at most $(1-q_{c})^{E_{\mathrm{rv}}}$ , scaling with the supply of
never-decided inspectors, not with epoch renewal (N16). The
worst-case fallback as $q_{c}\to 0$ is the local layer of
Remark S5.12, not Theorem S5.11, whose $q=1$ 
hypothesis engineered correlation directly attacks. The
per-person half of the correlation concern (re-inspection of
the same bytes is not re-randomisation)
is discharged structurally for logged inspections: no logged
repeat carries weight or a verdict (INV-1,
INV-5); across unlogged personal inspections
(Definition S4.5) it survives as a calibration obligation
inside the log-fresh class (Remark S5.19).
Status. Residual with a three-layer cap, disclosed. Not used
in any proof.

Analysis S14 (Unrecognizable poison, $q\to 0$ ).

Vector. If no honest reviewer can recognize the poison, the
probabilistic bounds are vacuous.
Disposition. Conceded, and the guarantee is calibrated to what
a human-verification design can coherently mean by “safe”: content
is treated as safe when the qualifying community, inspecting the
exact bytes at the exact tool, accepts it. Content that fools
every honest inspector is, within this paradigm,
indistinguishable from truth, and no design
that routes trust through human judgment can promise otherwise. What
the design does promise is one-sided and strong: it takes only
one honest effective qualifying rejection — a rejecting
decision at a protocol-bearing gate by an identity currently holding
dispute-initiation authority (Rule R13(i),(v)) — to
freeze promotion immediately; detections by authority-inactive
identities are advisory flags, but the same identities’ acceptances
carry no promotion weight (Definition S4.6(a),
Remark S5.18), so silencing states never trade a lost
rejection for a harvestable acceptance; shared-presence freshness ensures the attacker can never harvest a
second weight-bearing acceptance from any identity that has
already logged a decision on the bytes (INV-1,
Corollary S5.27); and the moment a refuting inspection
arrives, however late, it takes protocol-level effect immediately.
What survives this regime is the local layer of
Remark S5.12, the one-sided freeze clause above, and
re-verification (N16); Theorem S5.11 does
not apply, its $q=1$ hypothesis being the exact negation of
this regime. Safety alone can additionally be forced for any $q>0$ by
raising $m$ toward $k$ , at the price of a weaker wrongful-eviction
bound (Remark S5.19).
Status. Residual; the paradigm’s boundary disclosed
(§S7.4(VI)). Not used in any proof.

Analysis S15 (Accountability mis-calibration).

Vector. The rate trigger and ceiling require estimating the
honest baseline $1-p_{b}$ ; mis-calibration can suppress honest
dispute-initiation and crediting, reducing availability — and,
absent authority-gated crediting
(Definition S4.6(a), Remark S5.18), it
would also invalidate the uniform $q_{\mathrm{pre}}$ interpretation.
Disposition. The exposure is availability-only, never safety,
and is bounded as follows. (i) Panel verdicts are never penalised
and never charged (Rule R13(v)(a)), and verdict-eligible
rejections are cast regardless of accountability state
(Rule R13(v)(e)), so the panel arithmetic of
Theorem S5.13 and Proposition S5.20 is
independent of calibration. (ii) A capped or suspended honest
identity retains advisory flags and ordinary use — though its
acceptances are authority-inert for the duration
(Definition S4.6(a), Remark S5.18) —
loses nothing permanent, and is restored at the epoch boundary
(Rule R13(v)(d), Remark S5.5). (iii) Its ordinary, non-verdict gate inspections while
dispute-initiation and crediting authority are inactive are
excluded trials, not silenced misses: neither branch enters
the $q_{\mathrm{pre}}$ trial class — the acceptance carries no promotion
weight and the rejection no initiation
(Definition S4.6(a), Remark S5.18,
Remark S5.19) — so these inspections do not enter $1-q_{\mathrm{pre}}$ .
Eligible panel verdicts are different: verdict capacity is never
authority-gated, and every cast eligible verdict remains a $q_{\mathrm{pan}}$ 
trial regardless of accountability state
(Rule R13(v)(a),(e), Remark S5.18,
Theorem S5.13(c)). Mis-calibration may change the
composition of the authority-active ordinary class, so the
deployment must continue to validate the weakest-target $q_{\mathrm{pre}}$ 
bound over that class (N17); it does not convert
excluded inspections into misses. The remaining cost is
availability: throttled honest dispute-initiation and delayed
crediting.
(iv) The calibration enters only the budget constants,
through the deterministic bound $c_{\mathrm{acc}}$ (Lemma S5.4,
Figure S1); no safety theorem depends on it. (v) Under authority-gated crediting (Definition S4.6(a), Remark S5.18), mis-calibration cannot be exploited to harvest promotion weight from silenced honest identities — a silenced identity’s acceptance is authority-inert — so the exposure remains throttled honest dispute-initiation, with correspondingly delayed crediting, availability-only. What
mis-calibration buys an adversary is therefore throttled honest
dispute-initiation: bounded required-confirmation harm and bounded
per-epoch throughput (Lemma S5.24; wall-clock delay
unbounded, resolution being demand-paced,
Remark S5.21), no
third-party alteration of any local store (Lemma S5.8),
and required cost within the single-user cap (Theorem S6.2).
Status. Safety disposed; availability exposure residual,
disclosed. Enters budget constants only.

S7.3.5 Modelling-boundary effects

Analysis S16 (Incentive feedback on publication).

Vector. Publisher penalties, though provably rare for benign
items, depress publication; a thinner shared tier raises the
effective $\beta$ and weakens A11 directly at its coverage clause (late or absent publication inflates $U_{v,t}$ ).
Disposition. Conceded and unmodelled: the incentive feedback
between the accountability layer and the demand model of
Appendix S6 is outside the formal development. Its
impact is confined to the budget asymptotics: the cap
of Theorem S6.2 is unaffected by it (it is an identity
over required confirmations), the safety theorems are unaffected (they
do not reference $\beta$ ), and
Proposition S5.22(ii) bounds the penalty’s probability for
benign publishers at $e^{-2\delta_{b}^{2}(k-f_{\mathrm{s}})}$ per panel, with expected counts per epoch bounded by the dispute ledger (Prop. S5.22(ii), linear tier).
Status. Residual, confined to budget asymptotics. Not used in
any proof.

Analysis S17 (Release-day demand spikes).

Vector. A popular item’s demand arrives in one burst;
A10 fails, and the amortisation claims fall with it.
Disposition. A release-day spike is precisely a violation of
A10 for that item ( $\gamma_{v}\approx 1$ ): its cost is
at most the item’s own demand, once: each affected demander pays the
required confirmations (at most one per demanded pair, INV-3)
that the pathwise cap (Theorem S6.2(i)) already
bounds (whose counting identities never use
A10), and every other item’s accounting is untouched,
since the ledger of Lemma S6.7 is per-item. Degradation
under spikes is therefore item-local, bounded by Theorem S6.2, and
transient; the window- and dormancy-bucket rows of the ledger are exactly
where within-window demand, diversity wait, propagation delay, and
dormant intervals are collected, and A10 caps them at the
fraction $\bar{\gamma}$ of each item’s own demand, so popularity never
enters the per-user bound (Lemma S6.8). A pre-publication demand burst is likewise an A11 violation for that item (its early demanders are residual), priced identically at the cap.
Status. Disposed (under stated assumptions): the failure mode is priced by the
pathwise cap (Theorem S6.2(i)). Not used in any proof.

Analysis S18 (Handle-mediated arrival of certified content).

Vector. Not an attack but a representation effect: a pair
 $(v,t)$ is live- $\mathsf{CC}$ , yet a user’s copy of $v$ arrives through an
untrusted channel (a message, a tool output) and is therefore held
only as an opaque handle (Lemma S3.12); passing the
handle to $t$ gates (Rule R4(c)) despite the
certificate, and, absent a local in-scope copy, the confirmation is
required. A demand stream arriving predominantly handle-expressed can drive
the shared required load toward the standalone mass, community
certification notwithstanding.
Disposition. Deliberate and structural: exempting
certificate-matching handle resolutions from the gate would make the
gate-vs-no-gate behaviour a function of hidden $\mathsf{U}$ -resolution,
reopening the channel Rules R6/R7 close and
breaking Theorem S3.25 (two runs whose handles resolve to
different $\mathsf{CC}$ -matching byte strings would flow ungated under equal
transcripts with different sink projections); the gate on
untrusted-channel arrivals is the inspection the design intends
(Remark S6.11). The cost is bounded on both sides: each
such confirmation is once ever per (user, pair) (INV-3),
the epoch’s shared required load never exceeds the standalone mass
(Theorem S6.2(ii)), and the accepting user thereafter
holds a local in-scope copy, fast-pathing all literal reuse.
Amortisation applies to the fast-path-expressed share of certified
demand — notably trusted retrieval, which returns live entries as
readable $\mathbb{E}$ -bytes byte-matching the entry
(Definition S4.1, Rules R14(ii),
R4(a)); the handle-arrival fraction is priced inside
 $\varepsilon(M)$ of A11 and logged directly
(N17).
Status. Residual, disclosed, priced in A11;
bounded pathwise by Theorem S6.2(i) (persistence exceptions per INV-3). Not used in any
safety proof; enters budget assumptions only.

## S7.4 Scope and residuals

The guarantees’ boundaries, collected in one place.

(I) 

Per-execution, per-(value, tool). Action Integrity is per
execution; endorsement is per (value, tool) and cached indefinitely (a conforming instantiation of the
requirements-level Option O3 would bound the
scope-checked tool-authorization lifetime; O3); recombination of separately
endorsed values is ungated by design (N2,
S11).

(II) 

The human channel. A6 idealizes typing as exogenous;
influence through displayed content is recorded, not prevented
(N5, N7, S9); Reasoning Integrity is
modulo the transcript, and the compound residual is S10.

(III) 

The batch gate. Every element’s bytes are displayed, so
Definition S3.10 holds literally; but displayed-in-full is
not reviewed-in-full: A4 is weakest here, and $c_{\mathrm{batch}}$ 
should be sized accordingly (N6, S12).

(IV) 

Timing and termination are out of scope; the exclusion
concerns only implicit wall-clock channels observed from outside
(S8).

(V) 

Model intent. Isolation restricts hidden untrusted influence; it does not certify $P$ or $R_{\mathcal{E}}$ as benign or guarantee that a later combination of typed or endorsed values is appropriate. Influence through changed displays and incorrect endorsements remains possible (S10, S11). The baseline’s extensional, tool-blind trust of typed literals is Remark S3.22; Option O1 narrows that authority by sensitive tool without approving a whole transaction. One first-party residual:
 $\mathsf{evict}$ is owner-scoped but $P$ -invocable and ungated, so an
erring Protected LLM can remove its owner’s endorsed blocks; the
no-deletion guarantee is third-party only, and gating
 $\mathsf{evict}$ on $\mathbb{E}$ -blocks is a further monotone hardening
(N13).

(VI) 

Unrecognizable poison ( $q\to 0$ ): no community-truthfulness
guarantee survives this regime within a human-judgment design
(Remark S5.12); what survives is the local layer, the
one-sided freeze on any single honest recognition, and re-verification
against never-decided inspectors (S14); degradation under
engineered correlation is to the rate $q_{c}$ , transparently
(S13).

(VII) 

Federated influence through legitimate log activity is
held fixed by A8 in the noninterference statement and is
bounded instead by Theorems S5.11 and S5.13.

(VIII) 

Availability is not guaranteed; a dispute’s harm in
required confirmations is bounded per Lemma S5.24 and
N14 (including the suspension-induced, once-per-demander
confirmation, Rule R13(vi)), inspection effort
disclosed separately (N18); degradation is never destructive
(Lemma S5.8).

(IX) 

Confidentiality. Only the type-level exclusion is proved
(Lemma S5.2); semantic confidentiality of published
content rests on the publish gate (A7).

(X) 

Tool choice and zero-argument actions. Action Integrity
constrains the values crossing sensitive tool boundaries; it does not
authorize the invocation itself, and a sensitive tool with no gated
arguments satisfies the value-flow property vacuously. Deployments
requiring authorization of the act itself gate the capability (a
monotone hardening in the sense of Prop. S3.28).

(XI) 

The reasoning pseudo-target. Shared-tier accounting
treats $\mathsf{reasoning}$ as a canonical pseudo-target
(§S4.1): knowledge items amortise and are defended at
it, and $\mathsf{CC}$ -at- $\mathsf{reasoning}$ status buys community-wide
readability only, never any tool flow.

## S7.5 Summary of composed guarantees

(A) One user, deployment lifetime (trust axioms
A1–A6 alone).

Mechanization boundary, wherever machine-checking is advertised:
kernel-checked are Personal Action Integrity from the empty
configuration and from arbitrary mechanized well-formed
configurations; the map-free Reasoning Integrity core from empty
stores and from the canonical shared-endorsed seed class; and the
standalone batch-confirmation layer (§S9).
Paper-only are the run-level $\mathsf{map}$ integration, the batch
cardinality cap, Option O3 (requirements-level), arbitrary matched mid-run pairs,
the index-oracle reduction (Prop. S3.29), the federated
product system, and every probabilistic and budget result.
Gating where influence acts:
Theorem S3.21, Corollary S3.23(a). Recording where
it persuades: Theorem S3.25, Corollary S3.26.
(Phrasing discipline, here and in any summary elsewhere: Reasoning
Integrity is a fixed-horizon, termination-insensitive equivalence
modulo the full display transcript, under coupled typed input
— and, federated, a coupled log (A8) — never an
unconditional noninterference or inertness claim;
Definition S3.11, Remark S3.9.)
Residuals: the user’s own false endorsement (S10); the
post-endorsement boundary (Corollary S3.23(b), O2);
recombination (S11, O1). Deployments whose threat model emphasises data-tool
exfiltration should select Option O2 as the default profile: it closes
the baseline post-endorsement channel (Cor. S3.23(b)), is
machine-checked at the gate level (§S9), and
costs $\mathcal{O}(1)$ per reusable endorsed-value pair, freshly composed arguments remaining in the per-task stream (Cor. S6.6). Lifetime required cost: one
accepting confirmation per (value, tool) relied on
(Theorem S6.2, Corollary S6.5); effort scope per
N18.

(B) Federated, deployment lifetime.

Three layers, each
surviving the failure of the layer above. Deterministic
( $q{=}1$ , $W{>}W_{\mathrm{adv}}$ , $m{>}f_{\mathrm{s}}$ ): Theorem S5.11, scope
Remark S5.12. Probabilistic, per poisoned attempt (Definition S5.1)
(Definition S4.10): Theorem S5.13, reach-free
(INV-6); per-anchor-epoch and lifetime exposure
Corollary S5.27 (attempts keyed to anchor epochs, Rem. S5.28), Remark S5.29; system-wide
per-anchor-epoch form under the union-bound condition of
Theorem S6.13(i). Scoping caveats: S1, S4; and one
explicitly: the numeric system-wide bound covers
adversary-published attempts, counted by the rate limit at
their anchors — poison published by honest-but-deceived identities
(S10) is bounded per attempt (Thm. S5.13)
and disciplined by publisher penalties (Rule R13(vii),
Prop. S5.22), but its attempt count
 $\mathbb{E}[S_{\mathrm{anc}}]$ is not bounded in terms of the adversary’s
resources alone (Cor. S5.27). Per-attempt bounds are marginal: deployment-wide exposure is
controlled by summing over anchored attempts and
byte-distinct variants, through the union-, expectation-, and
deterministic-cap forms of Corollary S5.27 and the
lifetime ceiling of Remark S5.29. Pathwise cap:
Lemma S5.8 (third-party transitions; first-party residual
N13) and Theorem S6.2: degradation to $M$ independent
copies of (A), never below. Budget: Theorem S6.2 (pathwise once-per-pair cap and
reference-model standalone-mass ceiling, the firmest cost guarantee);
Theorem S6.9, Corollary S6.10 (conditional upper
bounds, with the $\mathsf{Bad}$ residual and the
arrival-representation caveat S18) — the vanishing clauses are conditional accounting, consuming
A9–A13, the rate conditions of
Thm. S6.9(iii), the competence bound
of Lemma S6.7, and changing time geometry
(Rem. S6.12), never consequences of population growth
alone —,
Theorem S6.13, Remark S6.12.
Scope: the federated layer is a protocol over the log service of
convention (C3), not integrated into the single-user small-step relation
(§S4).

## Appendix S8 Probabilistic Tools and Supplementary Results

## S8.1 Probabilistic framework, coupling, and the no-dispute
baseline

The adaptive-adversary framework.

Fix an epoch. All protocol events (submissions, presentations, gate
decisions, verdicts, promotions) form a discrete sequence; let
 $(\mathcal{F}_{s})_{s\geq 0}$ be the filtration generated by all logged
protocol events and all display events up to step $s$ . The adversary’s
strategy (whom to present the item to next, when to submit, re-enter,
self-dispute, or stop) is predictable with respect to
 $(\mathcal{F}_{s})$ : its action at step $s$ is
 $\mathcal{F}_{s-1}$ -measurable (convention (C2)). Two structural facts
constrain the adversary’s adaptivity. First, an entry’s bytes are
immutable once submitted (the log is append-only and the gate displays
exact bytes), so within an entry the adversary adapts
presentation and stopping only, never the inspected content.
Second, because the adversary chooses whom to present the item to, the
inspection parameter $q$ must be read as a uniform lower bound
over all honest identities and presentation contexts, the catch rate
of the most fallible honest user the adversary can target, with
flagged scrutiny only raising it (Remark S5.19,
N11).

Atomic gate events; log order.

Gate events are atomic federated-wide, exactly as at the interface
(Rule R2): a presentation and its disposition (an accepting
decision, a rejecting decision, or abandonment) occupy a single
position of the linearizable log (convention (C3)); no dialog is ever
pending, and dispositions never resolve out of log order. The analysis
reads each such position as up to three consecutive atoms: the
rendering atom (item, tool, presenting identity, dispute flag, both
provenance zones — recorded in the display transcript whether or not a
decision follows, Rule R8(e)); if and only if the user records
a decision, the header atom (the fact that a decision by this
identity on these bytes at this gate is being appended, together with its
verdict eligibility, a deterministic predicate of the prior log and the
identity, Rule R13(iii)(a)–(e)); and the content atom
(the decision $z\in\{\mathrm{accept},\mathrm{reject}\}$ ). Abandonment
appends no decision record and contributes no decision trial. Cache gates (Options O1–O2) are the one
asymmetrically recorded class: both branches occupy a decision-pane
position of the display transcript — rendering, header, and content
atoms exactly as above, all inside the filtration, which is generated
by display events as well as logged protocol events — while only
the rejecting branch additionally appends a federation-log candidate
record (Definition S4.6(iii)). We say a decision is
audit-recorded if its atoms lie on the display transcript
(every recorded gate decision, cache acceptances included) and
federation-logged if it is appended to the federation log as a
candidate decision (protocol-bearing decisions only). Trials at cache
gates are indexed by their audit-recorded atoms: their occurrence is
pre-outcome, never selected by the rejecting branch’s federation-log
append. No protocol
event, and no adversary action, interleaves between the atoms of one
position; the discrete step index totally orders all atoms, and every
trial sequence in this document is indexed by these log-ordered atoms:
inspection and arrival trials by rendering atoms, decision and verdict
trials by header atoms. In particular, every earlier trial’s content atom
precedes every later trial’s occurrence atom. The adversary’s strategy,
predictable with respect to the record-level filtration, remains
predictable with respect to this refinement: the refinement inserts atoms
inside single records and reveals nothing between records on which a
strategy could act.

Conditioning convention.

The conditioning $\sigma$ -algebra depends on the trial type. For an
arrival or inspection-occurrence trial, $\mathcal{G}_{i}$ is
generated by all atoms strictly prior to the trial’s rendering atom.
For a decision- or verdict-content trial, $\mathcal{G}_{i}$ is
generated by all atoms strictly prior to the trial’s header atom
together with that header atom itself — the fact that a
decision by this identity on these bytes at this gate is being
recorded, and its deterministic eligibility facts
(Rule R13(iii)) — but not the trial’s content atom. Thus
the occurrence event $O_{i}$ (a decision is recorded) is
 $\mathcal{G}_{i}$ -measurable in every application. Every behavioural
lower bound ( $q_{\mathrm{pre}}$ , $q_{\mathrm{pan}}$ , $p_{b}$ , $\rho_{\mathrm{seat}}$ ) is a conditional bound
given the corresponding $\mathcal{G}_{i}$ , further enlarged by the
exogenous demand factor below (uniformly over demand realisations).
Under the atomic reading above, these $\sigma$ -algebras already
contain the disposition of every earlier presentation — there are no
latent or pending dispositions — so the occurrence, measurability,
and independence conditions of Remark S8.4 hold at
them directly. Where a behavioural parameter is stated at the
disposition level ( $q_{\mathrm{pre}}$ , $p_{b}$ ), Lemma S8.1
transports it to the header-inclusive $\sigma$ -algebra. For a per-attempt bound (Theorem S5.13), the conditioning $\sigma$ -algebra is generated by all atoms up to and including the attempt’s anchor atom (Definition S5.1), the anchor preceding every decision creditable to the attempt; the trial $\sigma$ -algebras above refine it, and the behavioural bounds, asserted uniformly over histories, hold under the anchor-conditional measure.

Dispositions.

An honest inspection presented at a gate has three possible dispositions, realized at that gate’s log position: an accepting decision, a rejecting decision, or
abandonment. Only decisions are logged; abandonment leaves no record
and has no protocol effect (Rule R13(i),(iii)). The
conditional decision probabilities therefore satisfy
 $p_{\mathrm{acc}}+p_{\mathrm{rej}}\leq 1$ , abandonment carrying the
remaining mass, and every behavioural parameter ( $q$ , $p_{b}$ ,
 $\rho_{\mathrm{seat}}$ ) is stated over dispositions: abandonment counts as a miss
inside $1-q$ and as a non-verdict inside $\rho_{\mathrm{seat}}$ (Remark S5.19).

Lemma S8.1 (Decision-conditioned content bounds).

Consider a gate presentation with rendering-atom $\sigma$ -algebra
 $\mathcal{G}$ and conditional disposition probabilities
 $p_{\mathrm{acc}},p_{\mathrm{rej}}$ given $\mathcal{G}$ , with
 $p_{\mathrm{acc}}+p_{\mathrm{rej}}\leq 1$ (abandonment carrying the
remaining mass). On the event that a decision is recorded, and given
 $\mathcal{G}$ together with the header atom: (i) if
 $p_{\mathrm{rej}}\geq q$ , the conditional probability of
 $\mathrm{accept}$ is at most $1-q$ ; (ii) if
 $p_{\mathrm{acc}}\geq p_{b}$ , the conditional probability of
 $\mathrm{reject}$ is at most $1-p_{b}$ ; (iii) symmetrically, let a designated sub-branch of the rejecting
disposition (e.g. the effective qualifying rejection of
Theorem S5.13(c)) have conditional probability
 $p_{\mathrm{eff}}\leq p_{\mathrm{rej}}$ given $\mathcal{G}$ ; if
 $p_{\mathrm{eff}}\geq q$ for some $q\in(0,1]$ , then, on the event that a
decision is recorded, the conditional probability of that sub-branch at
the header atom is at least $q$ :
 $p_{\mathrm{eff}}/(p_{\mathrm{acc}}+p_{\mathrm{rej}})\geq p_{\mathrm{eff}}\geq q$ , since $p_{\mathrm{acc}}+p_{\mathrm{rej}}\leq 1$ . The header atom adds no
randomness beyond the fact that a decision is recorded: verdict
eligibility is a deterministic function of the prior log and the
identity (Rule R13(iii)). (Part (i), the accept-side special case of (iii), is retained for
reference; part (iii) serves $q_{\mathrm{pre}}$ ’s lower-bound direction as used in
Theorem S5.13 case (a); part (ii) serves $p_{b}$ ; the verdict
parameter $q_{\mathrm{pan}}$ is defined at the header atom directly,
Theorem S5.13(c), and needs no bridge.)

Proof.

The decision-recorded event has conditional probability
 $p_{\mathrm{acc}}+p_{\mathrm{rej}}$ given $\mathcal{G}$ , so the
conditional accept probability given a recorded decision is
 $p_{\mathrm{acc}}/(p_{\mathrm{acc}}+p_{\mathrm{rej}})=1-p_{\mathrm{rej}}/(p_{\mathrm{acc}}+p_{\mathrm{rej}})\leq 1-p_{\mathrm{rej}}\leq 1-q$ , using
 $p_{\mathrm{acc}}+p_{\mathrm{rej}}\leq 1$ ; (ii) is symmetric.
Eligibility being measurable in $\mathcal{G}$ and the prior log,
conditioning on it changes nothing.
∎

Demand randomness, formally.

The underlying space is a product $\Omega_{\mathrm{prot}}\times\Omega_{\mathrm{dem}}$ . The factor $\Omega_{\mathrm{dem}}$ carries
the exogenous demand indicators of the reference model
(§S6.1) — Bernoulli variables $D_{u,v,t}$ ( $u$ 
demands the pair $(v,t)$ within the epoch, with per-user probability
 $p_{u,v,t}$ ( $p_{v,t}=M^{-1}\sum_{u}p_{u,v,t}$ , §S6.1)), independent across users and of every
 $\Omega_{\mathrm{prot}}$ -coordinate — together with each owner’s
publication schedule, coupled to the owner’s own indicators per the
owner-coupling convention (§S6.1); the counts
 $n_{v,t}=\sum_{u}D_{u,v,t}$ and $n_{v}$ are measurable in this factor
alone, and every epoch containing a submission or re-entry of $v$ has $n_{v}\geq 1$ (owner coupling; the ledger’s vanishing clause on $\{n_{v}=0\}$ is event-based, §S6.1). Three demand quantities must be distinguished. Latent demand
 $D_{u,v,t}$ is the exogenous indicator above. Realized shared-tier
demand is the log-defined event of Definition S4.4: the
first interpreter event at which $u$ ’s use of the pair fires a gate or
dereferences a shared entry, per Definition S4.4’s exclusions (covered and cache gates are not demand events on either branch), occurring only while $v$ has a shared-tier
presence; its count is denoted $n^{\mathrm{log}}_{v,t}$ . Reclassified
personal demand is a latent demander’s post-retirement encounter,
charged to the single-user stream (Definition S4.4,
retirement clause). Realized shared-tier demand is dominated pathwise by
latent demand: every realized demander is a latent demander — for third parties by
Definition S4.4 (a realized demand presupposes attempted
use), for the owner by the owner-coupling convention
(§S6.1) — so
 $n^{\mathrm{log}}_{v,t}\leq n_{v,t}$ pointwise; and by the
required-approval clause (§S6.1), a reference-model
coupling, any epoch event at which a user’s attempted use fires a
gate realizes the user’s latent indicator, so every required
confirmation in the epoch belongs to a latent demander, wherever the
event falls; hence every ledger bound
stated against $n_{v,t}$ or $\mathbb{E}[n_{v,t}]=Mp_{v,t}$ is an upper bound for
the realized counts the rows charge. Which class a latent demander lands
in (realized vs. reclassified) is protocol-dependent; the total
per-pair required cost across both classes is nonetheless at most one per
(user, pair) (INV-3), which is why the floor never consults the
split. The arrival times of realized demand are
protocol-endogenous, measurable in $(\mathcal{F}_{s})$ , revealing the
corresponding indicator at that event; no independence between arrival
times and the protocol is asserted, and A10 — both its
demand clause (used for bucket attribution, Lemma S6.7) and
its first-pair-decision clause (used only for the $\bar{D}_{v}$ derivation) — is
a hindsight cap on the realised arrival and decision schedules
respectively, per item, read pathwise on completed epochs. The
behavioural lower bounds $q$ , $p_{b}$ , $\rho_{\mathrm{seat}}$ are asserted conditionally
per the conditioning convention of this section, further enlarged by the
entire demand factor: uniformly over demand realisations. This licenses
the ledger’s two-stage argument (Lemma S6.7): the freeze rows
bound conditional expectations given the demand coordinates, by
Lemma S8.2 under the demand-conditional measure, whose
hypotheses hold there by the uniform reading; the expectation over demand
is taken last, by the tower property, and the pathwise domination
 $n^{\mathrm{log}}_{v,t}\leq n_{v,t}$ carries every realized-count row under
the latent masses.

Lemma S8.2 (Adaptive conditional coupling and domination).

Let $(\mathcal{F}_{s})$ be a filtration and let $Z_{1},Z_{2},\dots\in\{0,1\}$ 
be decision indicators observed at strictly increasing stopping times
 $\tau_{1}<\tau_{2}<\cdots$ . Write $O_{i}$ for the event that the $i$ -th
observation occurs, so that $O_{1}\supseteq O_{2}\supseteq\cdots$ and
 $O_{i}\in\mathcal{F}_{\tau_{i}^{-}}$ ; unrealised observations are read as
 $Z_{i}:=0$ off $O_{i}$ . Fix $p\in[0,1]$ and $n$ . There is an enlargement
of the probability space carrying i.i.d. $\mathrm{Bernoulli}(p)$ 
variables $Y_{1},Y_{2},\dots$ such that:

(i) 

(Majorant.) If
 $\mathbb{P}[Z_{i}=1\mid\mathcal{F}_{\tau_{i}^{-}}]\leq p$ on $O_{i}$ for every
 $i\leq n$ , then $Z_{i}\leq Y_{i}$ pointwise for every $i\leq n$ ; hence for
every $x$ ,
 $\mathbb{P}[\sum_{i\leq n}Z_{i}\geq x]\leq\mathbb{P}[\mathrm{Bin}(n,p)\geq x]$ , and
the same bound holds for any sum truncated by a predictable stopping
rule, truncation removing only nonnegative terms.

(ii) 

(Minorant, joint form.) If
 $\mathbb{P}[Z_{i}=1\mid\mathcal{F}_{\tau_{i}^{-}}]\geq p$ on $O_{i}$ for every
 $i\leq n$ , then $Y_{i}\leq Z_{i}$ pointwise on $O_{i}$ for every $i\leq n$ ;
hence for every $x$ ,

 $\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\Big[O_{n}\cap\Big\{\sum_{i\leq n}Z_{i}\leq x\Big\}\Big]\ \leq\ \mathbb{P}[\mathrm{Bin}(n,p)\leq x],\qquad\text{in particular}$ 

 $\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\big[O_{n}\cap\{Z_{1}=\cdots=Z_{n}=0\}\big]\ \leq\ (1-p)^{n}.$ 

No claim is made about the conditional law of $\sum_{i\leq n}Z_{i}$ 
given $O_{n}$ : the occurrence events may depend on the realised $Z_{i}$ ,
so only joint-event bounds are asserted.

(iii) 

(Counting.) If the hypothesis of (i) holds for every
 $i$ , then for the total count $T$ of realised observations,
 $\mathbb{E}\big[\sum_{i\leq T}Z_{i}\big]\leq p\,\mathbb{E}[T]$ .

Proof.

Construction. All protocol randomness lives on a standard
Borel space: the event alphabet of §S8.1 is countable and
the filtration is generated by a discrete event sequence
 $(\xi_{s})_{s\geq 0}$ , so regular conditional distributions exist, and
the process admits a sequential quantile representation:
there are measurable maps $(\phi_{s})$ and i.i.d. $\mathrm{Uniform}[0,1]$ variables $(V_{s})$ , adjoined by the
Ionescu–Tulcea extension, with
 $\tilde{\xi}_{s}:=\phi_{s}(\tilde{\xi}_{0},\dots,\tilde{\xi}_{s-1},V_{s})$ 
reproducing the joint law of $(\xi_{s})$ coordinate by coordinate
(each $\phi_{s}(\,\cdot\,,V_{s})$ realising the regular conditional law
of $\xi_{s}$ given the past by quantile inversion). We choose the
representation so that, for each $i$ , the coordinate deciding the
 $i$ -th observation’s value uses a dedicated uniform $U_{i}$ , with the
decision indicator realised as
 $\tilde{Z}_{i}=\mathbf{1}\{U_{i}\leq p_{i}\}$ on $\tilde{O}_{i}$ , where
 $p_{i}:=\mathbb{P}[Z_{i}{=}1\mid\mathcal{F}_{\tau_{i}^{-}}]$ evaluated along the
redefined history. Because the whole trajectory is rebuilt
recursively, the stopping times, occurrence events, and every other
coordinate are measurable functions of the redefined past, and the
redefined process has the same joint law as the original; every
conclusion below is a property of that joint law, so we argue on the
redefined process and drop the tildes. Set
 $Y_{i}:=\mathbf{1}\{U_{i}\leq p\}$ ; the $Y_{i}$ are i.i.d. $\mathrm{Bernoulli}(p)$ , each $U_{i}$ independent of the past at its
use.

(i) Majorant. On $O_{i}$ , $p_{i}\leq p$ , so $Z_{i}=1$ entails
 $U_{i}\leq p_{i}\leq p$ , hence $Y_{i}=1$ ; off $O_{i}$ , $Z_{i}=0$ . So
 $Z_{i}\leq Y_{i}$ everywhere. Pointwise domination of each term transfers
superlevel events of the sums, and dropping terms of a
 $\{0,1\}$ -valued sum only lowers it, giving the truncated form.

(ii) Minorant, joint form. On $O_{i}$ , $p_{i}\geq p$ , so $Y_{i}=1$ 
entails $U_{i}\leq p\leq p_{i}$ , hence $Z_{i}=1$ ; on $O_{n}$ every $O_{i}$ with
 $i\leq n$ holds, so
 $O_{n}\cap\{\sum_{i\leq n}Z_{i}\leq x\}\subseteq\{\sum_{i\leq n}Y_{i}\leq x\}$ ,
an event of unconditional binomial law; $x=0$ gives the all-zeros
bound.

(iii) Counting. Iterated conditioning:
 $\mathbb{E}[\sum_{i}\mathbf{1}_{O_{i}}Z_{i}]=\sum_{i}\mathbb{E}\big[\mathbf{1}_{O_{i}}\,p_{i}\big]\leq p\sum_{i}\mathbb{P}[O_{i}]=p\,\mathbb{E}[T]$ .
∎

Remark S8.3 (Alternatives to pathwise coupling).

Readers preferring to avoid the sequential quantile construction may
replace parts (i)–(ii) by exponential supermartingale bounds, at the
cost of the pointwise dominations; the pathwise form is kept because
the truncation and joint-event arguments of
Theorem S5.13, Proposition S5.22(ii), and
Lemma S5.6 consume domination, not merely tail bounds.

Remark S8.4 ( $\sigma$ -algebra form).

Lemma S8.2 holds verbatim with the pre-stopping-time
 $\sigma$ -algebras $\mathcal{F}_{\tau_{i}^{-}}$ replaced by any
increasing $\sigma$ -algebras
 $\mathcal{G}_{1}\subseteq\mathcal{G}_{2}\subseteq\cdots$ such that, for
each $i$ : $O_{i}\in\mathcal{G}_{i}$ ; $Z_{j}$ is
 $\mathcal{G}_{i}$ -measurable for every $j<i$ ; and the coupling
variable $U_{i}$ is independent of $\mathcal{G}_{i}$ . The proof is
unchanged: the conditional-quantile construction and the pointwise
dominations use only these three properties. The applications take $\mathcal{G}_{i}$ per the conditioning convention
of §S8.1: for arrival and inspection trials, all atoms
strictly prior to the rendering atom; for decision and verdict trials,
all prior atoms plus the trial’s own header atom (content atom
excluded), so that $O_{i}\in\mathcal{G}_{i}$ ; both enlarged by the demand
factor. All trials being log-ordered with earlier contents preceding
later occurrence atoms, the three conditions hold at these
 $\sigma$ -algebras directly.

Lemma S8.5 (Delayed marks).

Let $\mathcal{G}_{1}\subseteq\mathcal{G}_{2}\subseteq\cdots$ be
 $\sigma$ -algebras, $O_{i}\in\mathcal{G}_{i}$ occurrence events, and let
 $X_{i}$ be $[0,1]$ -valued random variables with
 $\mathbb{E}[X_{i}\mid\mathcal{G}_{i}]\leq p$ on $O_{i}$ for every $i$ . Then, with
 $N:=\sum_{i}\mathbf{1}_{O_{i}}$ finite almost surely,

 $\mathbb{E}\Big[\sum_{i}\mathbf{1}_{O_{i}}X_{i}\Big]\ \leq\ p\,\mathbb{E}[N].$ 

No ordering of the times at which the $X_{i}$ resolve, no measurability
of any $X_{i}$ with respect to any $\mathcal{G}_{j}$ , and no independence
is required: each $X_{i}$ may resolve arbitrarily later than its
occurrence event, interleaved with other trials’ occurrences and
resolutions.

Proof.

 $\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{1}_{O_{i}}X_{i}]=\mathbb{E}\big[\mathbf{1}_{O_{i}}\,\mathbb{E}[X_{i}\mid\mathcal{G}_{i}]\big]\leq p\,\mathbb{P}[O_{i}]$ by the tower property; sum over $i$ .
∎

Remark S8.6 (Realised-at-time vs. delayed marks).

Lemma S8.2(iii) is the special case in which $X_{i}$ is a
decision indicator realised at its own stopping time.
Lemma S8.5 is the form consumed by trials whose
outcome resolves only later — anchor-indexed promotion
attempts (Corollary S5.27) and opening-indexed panel
instances (Proposition S5.22(ii), linear tier) — where
later trials may open before earlier outcomes resolve, so outcome
order need not equal occurrence order. The coupling machinery of
Lemma S8.2 is neither needed nor invoked there.

Lemma S8.7 (Predictable cell allocation).

In the setting of Remark S8.4, fix finitely many
cells $(j,\ell)$ , $j\leq r$ , $\ell\leq K$ . Suppose each realised trial
 $i$ is assigned to at most one cell, injectively across trials, by an
assignment that is $\mathcal{G}_{i}$ -measurable (decided at the trial’s
occurrence, before its content). On the enlargement of
Lemma S8.2 carrying the coupling variables
 $Y_{i}=\mathbf{1}\{U_{i}\leq p\}$ , define the cell-indexed array
 $X_{j,\ell}:=Y_{i(j,\ell)}$ where $i(j,\ell)$ is the trial assigned
to cell $(j,\ell)$ , and $X_{j,\ell}:=\mathbf{1}\{U^{\prime}_{j,\ell}\leq p\}$ 
with fresh independent uniforms $U^{\prime}_{j,\ell}$ for never-filled cells.
Then $(X_{j,\ell})_{j\leq r,\,\ell\leq K}$ is i.i.d. $\mathrm{Bernoulli}(p)$ .

Proof.

This is optional skipping in the sense of Doob, extended to a
predictable partition. Order trials by occurrence. At trial $i$ ’s
occurrence its cell assignment is $\mathcal{G}_{i}$ -measurable, while
 $U_{i}$ is independent of $\mathcal{G}_{i}$ (Lemma S8.2,
construction) — in particular of all previously assigned cells and
all previously revealed coupling variables; the fresh $U^{\prime}_{j,\ell}$ 
are independent of everything. Fix any
 $\varepsilon:\{(j,\ell)\}\to\{0,1\}$ and reveal the array in
assignment order, completing with the fresh uniforms: at each
revelation, the conditional probability that the next revealed entry
equals its prescribed value is $p^{\varepsilon}(1-p)^{1-\varepsilon}$ 
for that entry, by the independence just stated. Induction along the
revelation order gives
 $\mathbb{P}[X_{j,\ell}=\varepsilon(j,\ell)\ \forall j,\ell]=\prod_{j,\ell}p^{\varepsilon(j,\ell)}(1-p)^{1-\varepsilon(j,\ell)}$ ,
i.e. the array is i.i.d. $\mathrm{Bernoulli}(p)$ .
∎

Lemma S8.8 (Hoeffding tails: independent and predictable-weight forms).

(i) 

(Independent, deterministic weights.) Let
 $X_{j}\in\{0,1\}$ be independent with $\mathbb{P}[X_{j}{=}1]\leq\varepsilon$ ,
and let $w_{j}\in[0,w_{\max}]$ be deterministic, $j=1,\dots,n$ . Then for
 $t>0$ ,
 $\mathbb{P}[\sum_{j}w_{j}X_{j}\geq\varepsilon\sum_{j}w_{j}+t]\leq\exp\!\big(-2t^{2}/(nw_{\max}^{2})\big)$ Hoeffding (1963); the unweighted
case gives
 $\mathbb{P}[\mathrm{Bin}(n,\varepsilon)\geq(\varepsilon+\delta)n]\leq e^{-2\delta^{2}n}$ .

(ii) 

(Predictable weights, conditional success bound.) Let
 $(\mathcal{H}_{j})_{j\geq 0}$ be a filtration, $X_{j}\in\{0,1\}$ adapted
with $\mathbb{P}[X_{j}{=}1\mid\mathcal{H}_{j-1}]\leq\varepsilon$ , and
 $w_{j}\in[0,w_{\max}]$ predictable (each $w_{j}$ 
 $\mathcal{H}_{j-1}$ -measurable). Then the bound of (i) holds with the
identical exponent.

Proof.

(ii) Set $D_{j}:=w_{j}\big(X_{j}-\mathbb{E}[X_{j}\mid\mathcal{H}_{j-1}]\big)$ , a
martingale-difference sequence whose $j$ -th increment lies, given
 $\mathcal{H}_{j-1}$ , in an interval of predictable length
 $w_{j}\leq w_{\max}$ . The Hoeffding–Azuma inequality in its
conditional-range form (Hoeffding’s lemma applied conditionally to
each increment, then Markov on the exponential moment) gives
 $\mathbb{P}[\sum_{j}D_{j}\geq t]\leq\exp\!\big(-2t^{2}/\sum_{j}w_{j}^{2}\big)\leq\exp\!\big(-2t^{2}/(nw_{\max}^{2})\big)$ .
Since $\sum_{j}w_{j}\mathbb{E}[X_{j}\mid\mathcal{H}_{j-1}]\leq\varepsilon\sum_{j}w_{j}$ 
pathwise, the event $\{\sum_{j}w_{j}X_{j}\geq\varepsilon\sum_{j}w_{j}+t\}$ is
contained in $\{\sum_{j}D_{j}\geq t\}$ . (The bounded-difference form of
Azuma’s inequality, with increments bounded in absolute value by
 $w_{\max}$ , would give only $\exp(-t^{2}/(2nw_{\max}^{2}))$ ; the range form is
the one used.)
∎

Theorem S8.9 (No-dispute baseline: reach-dependent).

Without Rule R13, with rejections protocol-inert and a
poisoned item shown to at most $R$ honest users each erroneously
accepting with probability $\leq\varepsilon$ independently: if
 $W>W_{\mathrm{adv}}+\varepsilon Rw_{\max}$ ,

 $\mathbb{P}[\text{promotion}]\ \leq\ \exp\!\Big(-\frac{2(W-W_{\mathrm{adv}}-\varepsilon Rw_{\max})^{2}}{R\,w_{\max}^{2}}\Big).$ 

Proof.

Accepting weight is at most $W_{\mathrm{adv}}+S$ with $S=\sum_{j}w_{j}X_{j}$ over
 $\leq R$ honest users; apply Lemma S8.8 with
 $t=W-W_{\mathrm{adv}}-\varepsilon Rw_{\max}$ .
∎

Comparison with the no-dispute baseline.

The comparison isolates exactly what the panel and the
gate-rejection-as-report rule buy. In Theorem S8.9 rejections
are inert, so honest false acceptances accumulate with reach: the
sample size is the attacker-chosen reach $R$ , the bound requires
 $W$ to grow linearly with $\varepsilon R$ merely to stay non-trivial,
dragging the budget of Appendix S6 with it, and spreading
the poison farther helps the attacker: the bound is not reach-free in
the sense of INV-6. In Theorem S5.13, because
every honest inspection carries its own report
(Rule R13(i)), the no-dispute event shrinks with every
additional honest viewer, and the false-accept mass that matters
after a dispute is confined to a bounded jury of $k-f_{\mathrm{s}}$ honest
verdicts, a protocol constant the attacker cannot multiply, and one
the demand-paced formation rule fills without any deadline the attacker
could exploit. Reach drops out of the bound entirely, and spreading the
poison cannot loosen the bound, while any single honest gate rejection
freezes it. The dispute log additionally makes wrongful rejection a
costly, attributable act, and panel eviction makes poisoned submission
a costly, attributable act (Rule R13(v),(vii)), neither of
which the inert-rejection baseline can express.

## S8.2 The blast-schedule promotion law

This subsection derives, in closed form, the promotion probability of one
simulated presentation schedule under one simulated model of
honest behaviour. It is an analysis tool for the empirical evaluation, not
a protocol guarantee: no rule, assumption, definition, or theorem of
Appendices S2–S6 cites it, and nothing in
this document depends on it. Its purpose is to turn the simulator’s output
into a falsifiable prediction, so that agreement is evidence the
implementation realises the rules of Appendix S4, and
disagreement localises a defect in one of the two.

Definition S8.10 (Blast schedule; independent honest coin).

The blast schedule is the non-adaptive presentation strategy that
(i) submits a poisoned item (Definition S4.10), with all
endorsement and all demand at a single tool, (ii) spends every adversarial
identity as a pre-dispute accepting endorser at that tool, and (iii) then
presents the item, once each, to $R$ honest identities in a fixed order
(the reach, as in Theorem S8.9), continuing through every
one of them whatever any of them decides. Four stipulations fix the
simulated setting.

(a) 

One window. All $R$ presentations, and any panel the schedule
induces, fall within a single finalisation window of one pipeline
entry, with the freeze excluded from the window clock
(Rule R13(iv)) and no epoch boundary closing the window early
(convention (C4)). In particular the entry is open throughout, so no
decision is banked (Definition S4.6(b), which requires no
open entry) and no re-entry occurs: every weight-bearing decision is
credited to that one entry.

(b) 

Independent honest coin. Each of the $R$ inspections is,
independently of the others, a rejecting decision with probability $q$ and
an accepting decision with probability $1-q$ , with no abstention.

(c) 

Coupled reviewer quality. The same $q$ governs pre-freeze
gate inspections, panel verdicts, and post-resolution confirmations
( $q_{\mathrm{pre}}=q_{\mathrm{pan}}=q$ ); the decoupled reading, under which those three classes
may carry different parameters, is Remark S8.17.

(d) 

Seat weight and eligibility. Every identity carries
attestation weight at least $w_{\mathrm{seat}}$ , so a fresh decision on the item is
verdict-eligible whenever a dispute is open
(Rule R13(iii)(a)); every decision, the $f$ adversarial
pre-dispute endorsements included, is authority-active
(Definition S4.6(a)) and taken at a non-covered, non-cache
gate, so every accepting decision is creditable and every rejecting
decision takes effect; and identities are assigned to the $D_{\mathrm{pool}}$ pools
round-robin.

Independence across inspections is a property of this simulation only: no
result of Appendices S4–S6 assumes it, and
Theorem S5.13 explicitly does not (§S8.1,
Remark S8.14).

Proposition S8.11 (Blast promotion law).

Adopt Rules R12–R13 with the default bars of
Rule R13(iv) ( $m_{\mathrm{rej}}=k+1-m$ , hence
 $m+m_{\mathrm{rej}}-1=k$ ), and the blast schedule and independent honest
coin of Definition S8.10. Suppose

 $N-f\;\leq\;m,\qquad N-f\;\leq\;R\;\leq\;2m,$ 

and suppose the deployment’s constants and pool assignment leave the
credit caps of Definition S4.6(f) non-binding for promotion
at every reach in that range (Step 1). Define

 $S(J)\;:=\;\sum_{r=0}^{\min(J-m,\;m_{\mathrm{rej}}-1)}\binom{m-1+r}{r}\Big(\frac{q}{1-q}\Big)^{\!r}\quad(J\geq m),\qquad S(J):=0\quad(J<m).$ 

Then the item is promoted to $\mathsf{CC}$ with probability
 $\pi_{\mathrm{blast}}(R)=\pi_{\mathrm{ord}}(R)+\pi_{\mathrm{pan}}(R)$ ,
where the two components — promotion with no dispute ever opened, and
promotion after a dispute — are disjoint and equal

 $\pi_{\mathrm{ord}}(R)=(1-q)^{R},\qquad\pi_{\mathrm{pan}}(R)=q\,(1-q)^{R-1}\sum_{J=m}^{R-1}S(J).$ 

Neither expression contains $k$ , $f$ , $f_{\mathrm{s}}$ , or $D_{\mathrm{pool}}$ .

Proof.

Step 0 (no adversarial verdict). Blast spends its $f$ identities as
pre-dispute accepting endorsers before any honest presentation. By
byte-permanence of freshness (INV-1) each identity’s first
logged decision on the bytes is its only weight-bearing inspection, and
verdict eligibility requires freshness (Rule R13(iii)(b)); so
when the freeze lands no adversarial identity is verdict-eligible, and
every verdict is cast by an honest identity. Hence $f$ and $f_{\mathrm{s}}$ do not
appear.

Step 1 (no capacity, verdict-pool, or credit cap binds). Under the
default bars $m+m_{\mathrm{rej}}-1=k$ , so at most $k$ verdicts are cast
before a bar is reached (Lemma S5.7(ii)) and the capacity
cap of Rule R13(iii)(e) is never the binding constraint. Two
further caps are properties of the simulated population rather than
consequences of the rules, and are disclosed as such. With the
round-robin assignment of Definition S8.10(d), no pool
contributes more than $\lceil k/D_{\mathrm{pool}}\rceil$ verdicts among any prefix of
 $k$ arrivals, so the per-pool verdict cap of Rule R13(iii)(d)
does not bind. Likewise the per-pool and total credit caps of
Definition S4.6(f) truncate crediting at
 $\lceil N/D_{\mathrm{pool}}\rceil$ per pool and $N^{+}$ in total, and those caps are
promotion-neutral in the sense stated there — $N^{+}\geq N$ , and a
credited set meeting $N$ under the per-pool cap necessarily spans at least
 $D_{\mathrm{pool}}$ pools — provided enough accepting identities are
available in each pool at the reach in question. That availability is a
property of the constants and the round-robin assignment, verified for the
deployment studied here and assumed in the statement; where it fails, the
law does not apply and $D_{\mathrm{pool}}$ re-enters. Hence, under the stated
hypothesis, $k$ and $D_{\mathrm{pool}}$ do not appear.

Step 2 (the ordinary component). Suppose no honest inspection
rejects. No dispute opens; the accepting decisions are all
authority-active (Definition S8.10(d)), so credited accepting
endorsements number $\min(f+R,N^{+})\geq N$ , using $R\geq N-f$ with Step 1;
and the entry promotes at its window’s close
(Definition S4.6(c)). Conversely, a rejecting decision at
any point in the window is a qualifying rejection that freezes
promotion at every tool immediately (Rule R13(i)–(ii)), while
finalisation occurs only at the window’s close, which by
Definition S8.10(a) lies after all $R$ presentations — so a
single rejection, however late, denies the ordinary path. The ordinary
component is therefore exactly the all-accept event, of probability
 $(1-q)^{R}$ .

Step 3 (the panel component; conditioning). Condition on the first
rejection falling at honest position $j$ , an event of probability
 $q(1-q)^{j-1}$ . The item is unpromoted and unfrozen at that moment, so
that rejection is dispute-initiating (Rule R13(i)–(ii)). The
remaining $J:=R-j$ inspections are, by Steps 0–1 and
Definition S8.10(d), honest, fresh and seat-weighted; each is an
eligible verdict for as long as the dispute is unresolved, and an ordinary
accepting or rejecting confirmation thereafter. Steps 4–5 treat the two
segments separately.

Step 4 (no rejection after the $m$ -th acceptance). Vindication
resumes the interrupted entry but never itself promotes: promotion occurs
only at the window’s close (Definition S4.6(c),
Rule R13(iv)). A rejecting decision logged after vindication is
therefore a fresh qualifying rejection against an unpromoted entry, which
re-imposes the byte-level freeze (Rule R13(ii)). Verdict
capacity is scoped to the dispute instance (INV-5) and
so is available afresh in the second instance; what is not available is
freshness, which is byte-permanent (INV-1,
Rule R13(iii)(b)): the second instance draws eligible jurors
only from identities with no prior logged decision on the bytes, of whom
at most $m-1$ remain (Step 7). It therefore cannot vindicate; its only
other outcomes are eviction, which ends the presence outright, and an
unresolved instance, under which the window clock does not run out at all
because the freeze is excluded from it (Rule R13(iv)). In
either case the entry never reaches an unfrozen window end, so it cannot
finalise. Under blast every one of the $J$ post-freeze identities decides,
so the qualifying sequences are exactly those whose tail after the $m$ -th
acceptance is unanimously accepting.

Step 5 (the count, and the credited threshold). Fix $J$ and index
the post-freeze inspections $1,\dots,J$ . By Step 4, promotion requires:
the $m$ -th accepting verdict at position $m+r$ for some $r$ , with exactly
 $r$ rejecting verdicts before it and $r\leq m_{\mathrm{rej}}-1$ (otherwise
the eviction bar is reached first) and $m+r\leq J$ ; and every one of the
remaining $J-m-r$ decisions accepting. The first $m+r-1$ positions hold
 $m-1$ acceptances and $r$ rejections in any order, $\binom{m-1+r}{r}$ 
ways, and every such sequence carries $r$ rejections and $J-r$ 
acceptances. The credited count is sufficient on every such
sequence: the accepting decisions credited to the entry are the $f$ 
pre-dispute endorsements, the $j-1$ pre-freeze honest acceptances, the $m$ 
accepting verdicts (credited on vindication, Rule R13(iv)) and
the $J-m-r$ post-resolution acceptances, all authority-active by
Definition S8.10(d), totalling $f+R-1-r$ ; since
 $r\leq J-m=R-j-m$ and $j\geq 1$ , this is at least $f+m\geq N$ by the
hypothesis $N-f\leq m$ , and Step 1 keeps the caps from truncating below
 $N$ . Hence

 $\mathbb{P}[\text{promotion}\mid J]=\sum_{r=0}^{\min(J-m,\,m_{\mathrm{rej}}-1)}\binom{m-1+r}{r}q^{r}(1-q)^{J-r}=(1-q)^{J}S(J),$ 

which is $0$ for $J<m$ , no arrangement of fewer than $m$ verdicts reaching
the $m$ -bar.

Step 6 (collapse). Summing over $j$ , and using
 $(1-q)^{j-1}(1-q)^{R-j}=(1-q)^{R-1}$ with the substitution $J=R-j$ ,

 $\pi_{\mathrm{pan}}(R)=\sum_{j=1}^{R-m}q(1-q)^{j-1}\,\Big[(1-q)^{J}S(J)\Big]_{J=R-j}=q(1-q)^{R-1}\sum_{J=m}^{R-1}S(J).$ 

Step 7 (validity, and exhaustiveness). The hypothesis $R\leq 2m$ is
what makes Step 4’s tail requirement exactly right. At the $m$ -th
accepting verdict, $j+m+r$ inspections have occurred, so at most
 $R-j-m-r\leq R-m-1\leq m-1<m$ remain: too few for any second dispute to
reach its own $m$ -bar, whatever its verdicts. The two cases — no
rejection anywhere, and a first rejection at some position — are
exhaustive and disjoint, so
 $\pi_{\mathrm{blast}}=\pi_{\mathrm{ord}}+\pi_{\mathrm{pan}}$ .
∎

Remark S8.12 (Where each hypothesis is consumed).

The proposition’s hypotheses and the stipulations of
Definition S8.10 are not interchangeable, and each is used once.
 $R\geq N-f$ is Step 2’s threshold. $N-f\leq m$ is Step 5’s, and it is not
implied by the others, since $N-f\leq R\leq 2m$ permits $N-f=2m$ — at
which reach a counted panel sequence can credit as few as $f+m<N$ 
acceptances and the formula would over-count; it is also what makes the
plateau identity of Corollary S8.13(iv) meaningful, that
identity evaluating $\pi_{\mathrm{ord}}$ at $R=m$ , which requires
 $m\geq N-f$ . $R\leq 2m$ is Step 7. The credit-cap non-bindingness is
Steps 1, 2 and 5. The one-window stipulation of
Definition S8.10(a) is Step 2’s converse and Step 4’s re-freeze;
the seat-weight and authority stipulations of
Definition S8.10(d) are Step 3’s eligibility and Steps 2 and 5’s
crediting. At the geometry studied here ( $N=10$ , $f=2$ , $m=20$ ,
 $m_{\mathrm{rej}}=12$ ) the first two hold with slack,
 $N-f=8\leq\min(m,R)=\min(20,R)$ for every $R\geq 8$ , and the binding
hypothesis is $R\leq 40$ .

Corollary S8.13 (Five structural consequences).

Under the hypotheses of Proposition S8.11:

(i) 

(Structural zero and switch-on.)
 $\pi_{\mathrm{pan}}(R)=0$ for every $R\leq m$ , and its first nonzero value
is at $R=m+1$ , where it equals exactly $q(1-q)^{m}$ (since $S(m)=1$ ).
This is a support property, invariant under any change of measure.

(ii) 

(No $k$ , $f$ , $f_{\mathrm{s}}$ , $D_{\mathrm{pool}}$ .) As stated, by Steps 0–1.

(iii) 

( $m_{\mathrm{rej}}$ enters late.) The cap in $S(J)$ first
differs from $J-m$ at $J=m+m_{\mathrm{rej}}$ , and $\pi_{\mathrm{pan}}(R)$ 
reaches $J=R-1$ ; hence $\pi_{\mathrm{pan}}$ is independent of
 $m_{\mathrm{rej}}$ for all $R\leq m+m_{\mathrm{rej}}$ and first depends on
it at $R=m+m_{\mathrm{rej}}+1$ , where the difference between the capped
and uncapped forms is exactly the single $r=m_{\mathrm{rej}}$ term at
 $J=m+m_{\mathrm{rej}}$ .

(iv) 

(Plateau identity.)
 $\pi_{\mathrm{blast}}(m)=\pi_{\mathrm{blast}}(m+1)=(1-q)^{m}$ exactly,
since $(1-q)^{m+1}+q(1-q)^{m}=(1-q)^{m}$ . The switch-on is therefore
invisible in the total rate and visible only in the decomposition
— which is why the figure that exhibits it plots the two components
separately rather than their sum.

(v) 

(Peak and fall.) $S(J)$ is constant in $J$ for
 $J\geq m+m_{\mathrm{rej}}-1$ , so $\sum_{J}S(J)$ grows linearly in $R$ 
while $(1-q)^{R-1}$ decays geometrically: $\pi_{\mathrm{pan}}$ rises,
attains an interior maximum, and falls.

Remark S8.14 ( $\pi_{\mathrm{pan}}$ is not the theorem’s panel term).

 $\pi_{\mathrm{pan}}(R)$ and the panel term of $B_{\mathrm{att}}$ 
(Remark S5.17) are different objects and must not be conflated.
The theorem’s term is a reach-free upper bound over all adaptive
presentation, stopping and re-entry strategies, holding under a
conditional lower bound on honest behaviour alone and assuming no
independence — the exact binomial tail arises there from the coupling of
Lemma S8.2, not from an independence hypothesis;
 $\pi_{\mathrm{pan}}(R)$ is the exact realised probability of one
non-adaptive schedule under the independence of
Definition S8.10(b). A large gap between them is expected and is
not evidence that the bound is loose: tightness is a separate
empirical question, addressed by strategies that reserve seat-eligible
identities and stop at the first bar reached, which empirically
attain the flat panel term — precisely because they can stop,
while blast cannot, which is why blast’s realised rate peaks and falls
where the theorem’s term is constant. Two schedules, one mechanism,
opposite consequences, both predicted.

Remark S8.15 (Validity, and the direction of failure beyond it).

The law holds in the single-dispute regime $R\leq 2m$ , and Step 7 is where
that hypothesis is consumed. Beyond it a post-vindication rejection can
open a second dispute among still-unpresented, still-fresh jurors, whose
own $m$ -bar is now reachable; the single-dispute form therefore
under-counts, and the direction of failure is fixed in advance
rather than discovered. Consequently no reading of the law beyond $2m$ is
a prediction: past that reach the schedule’s rate is a measured quantity
and the law is not a model of it. Nothing about the failure bears on any
bound: $B_{\mathrm{att}}$ is reach-free (INV-6) and continues to
hold at every reach, and INV-6 disclaims monotonicity of the
realised rate precisely so that a plateau or a rise in a measured
sweep is inside the theory rather than against it.

Commentary
 (non-normative; the validity boundary, measured). At the deployed geometry ( $q=0.6$ , $N=10$ , $f=2$ , $k=31$ , $m=20$ ,
 $m_{\mathrm{rej}}=12$ , so $2m=40$ ) the boundary of
Remark S8.15 is measured, not merely asserted. At the last
in-validity reach the law gives
 $\pi_{\mathrm{pan}}(40)=1.049\times 10^{-5}$ against $9$ promotions in
 $10^{6}$ trials. At $R=64$ the same schedule yields $8$ promotions in
 $10^{6}$ trials ( $8.0\times 10^{-6}$ , Clopper–Pearson $95\%$ 
 $[3.45,\,15.76]\times 10^{-6}$ ) against an extrapolation of
 $1.057\times 10^{-14}$ — a factor $7.57\times 10^{8}$ , i.e. $8.88$ 
orders of magnitude above it, in the disclosed under-counting direction.
At equal trial count $R=40$ and $R=64$ are statistically
indistinguishable ( $z=0.243$ ), so the realised rate plateaus rather than
continuing its geometric decay. The plateau does not persist: at $R=100$ 
the rate is bounded above by $3.69\times 10^{-6}$ , a bound that overlaps
 $R=64$ ’s lower confidence limit, so the claim is no rise, not
a fall. Every measured cell of this sweep lies below
 $B_{\mathrm{att}}=8.957\times 10^{-3}$ : the boundary cell $R=40$ by
 $3.00$ decades and every cell beyond it by at least $3.05$ . The
generating experiment gates on that conformance, at every cell of every
sweep, before writing its artifact. Inside the validity window the margin
is far smaller — about $0.58$ decades at the panel-path peak $R=32$ ,
where the measured rate is $2.34\times 10^{-3}$ — which is where the
ceiling is informative rather than slack. No mechanism is proposed for the
plateau’s magnitude: beyond $2m$ the sweep is measured, not
modelled.

Remark S8.16 (Testability of the $m_{\mathrm{rej}}$ entry).

Corollary S8.13(iii) is sharply testable only for small
 $m_{\mathrm{rej}}$ . The entering term is weighed against the partial sum
 $\sum_{J=m}^{R-1}S(J)$ that $\pi_{\mathrm{pan}}$ carries at the entry
reach, and that sum grows with $m_{\mathrm{rej}}$ far faster than the
single entering term does, so the relative effect shrinks sharply as the
eviction bar rises. The claim is therefore made where it is resolvable —
at small $m_{\mathrm{rej}}$ , for two values of $m$ — while for large
 $m_{\mathrm{rej}}$ the testable statement is agreement with the uncapped
form below the entry reach. Two of the three confirmations available are
support properties rather than hypothesis tests: below the entry reach the
capped and uncapped forms are equal, so two panel widths sharing
one coin stream must produce byte-identical counts, which cannot be
passed by luck.

Commentary
 (non-normative; the $m_{\mathrm{rej}}$ entry, measured). At $m=20$ and $q=0.2$ : at $m_{\mathrm{rej}}=4$ , entry reach $R=25$ , the
entering term is $34.59$ against a partial sum of $112.5$ , a $+30.75\%$ 
effect; at $m_{\mathrm{rej}}=12$ , entry reach $R=33$ , it is $8.411$ 
against $2003.05$ , a $+0.42\%$ effect. The corresponding rates are
 $\pi_{\mathrm{pan}}(25)=0.10625$ capped against $0.13892$ uncapped, and
 $\pi_{\mathrm{pan}}(33)=0.31740$ against $0.31873$ . Reading a five-sigma
requirement as five standard errors of the difference between two arms of
 $K$ trials each, with the variance evaluated at the capped rate —
 $K=25\cdot 2p(1-p)/\Delta^{2}$ — those effects need $4.5\times 10^{3}$ 
and $6.1\times 10^{6}$ trials per arm respectively. The prediction is
accordingly claimed at $m_{\mathrm{rej}}=4$ , where it is confirmed three
ways: byte-identical counts across two panel widths sharing one coin
stream at every reach below the entry; a $+13.6\sigma$ divergence at the
entry reach itself; and two $\pi_{\mathrm{pan}}$ profiles whose maxima
over the sampled reach grid fall where the law places them, $R=26$ with
 $4{,}715$ observed against $4{,}705$ predicted and $R=30$ with $14{,}030$ 
against $14{,}001$ . One scope note on the maxima: the
 $m_{\mathrm{rej}}{=}4$ profile’s exact maximum lies at $R=27$ , a reach
that grid does not sample, so $R=26$ is a maximum over the sampled grid
and not the argmax of $\pi_{\mathrm{pan}}$ ; the two differ by $2.2\%$ in
value.

Remark S8.17 (Decoupled reviewer quality).

Definition S8.10(c) couples three decision classes that the
protocol distinguishes: pre-freeze gate inspections, panel verdicts, and
post-resolution confirmations. Under a decoupled reading the first and
third are ordinary gate inspections, governed by $q_{\mathrm{pre}}$ , while only the
second are verdicts, governed by $q_{\mathrm{pan}}$ ; Steps 0–5 then go through with

 $\pi_{\mathrm{ord}}(R)=(1-q_{\mathrm{pre}})^{R},$ 

 $\mathbb{P}[\text{promotion}\mid J]=\sum_{r=0}^{\min(J-m,\,m_{\mathrm{rej}}-1)}\binom{m-1+r}{r}q_{\mathrm{pan}}^{\,r}(1-q_{\mathrm{pan}})^{m}(1-q_{\mathrm{pre}})^{J-m-r},$ 

the $m+r$ verdicts charged at $q_{\mathrm{pan}}$ and the unanimous tail of $J-m-r$ 
post-resolution confirmations at $q_{\mathrm{pre}}$ . But Step 6’s collapse fails —
the two geometric factors no longer share a base — and the panel
component remains the double sum
 $\sum_{j=1}^{R-m}q_{\mathrm{pre}}(1-q_{\mathrm{pre}})^{j-1}\mathbb{P}[\text{promotion}\mid R-j]$ .
Setting $q_{\mathrm{pre}}=q_{\mathrm{pan}}=q$ recovers Proposition S8.11 exactly,
which is why the closed form is specific to the coupled reading and why
the figures that exhibit the law run coupled reviewer quality while the
decoupled figures report ceilings only. A deployment or simulator adopting
the decoupled reading must additionally fix, and disclose, which parameter
governs the post-resolution tail: assigning it to $q_{\mathrm{pan}}$ instead — a
defensible modelling choice, since those inspections occur under the
dispute flag — replaces $(1-q_{\mathrm{pre}})^{J-m-r}$ by $(1-q_{\mathrm{pan}})^{J-m-r}$ and
yields $q_{\mathrm{pre}}(1-q_{\mathrm{pre}})^{j-1}(1-q_{\mathrm{pan}})^{J}S_{q_{\mathrm{pan}}}(J)$ with $S_{q_{\mathrm{pan}}}$ 
the sum $S$ at ratio $q_{\mathrm{pan}}/(1-q_{\mathrm{pan}})$ . The two forms differ except in the
